# Front Cover

->**[Binding Description]**<-

This volume is bound in deep red leather, decorated with intricate gold-leaf patterns <term>gold tooling: a decorative technique where heated metal stamps are used to press gold leaf into leather</term>. A double gold border frames the edges of the cover.

Within the corners of this frame, four heraldic emblems are stamped in gold:

*   **Top Left and Bottom Right:** A stylized castle or fortified building.
*   **Top Right and Bottom Left:** A lion rampant <term>lion rampant: a heraldic symbol showing a lion standing on its hind legs with forepaws raised, signifying bravery</term>.

The central leather panel is currently plain, though it bears faint, weathered marks <unclear>illegible traces of former inscriptions or scuffs</unclear> that have faded over time.

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page follows the flyleaf of the manuscript. It is the formal title page of the work, printed in Rome in 1650.</meta>

# THE WORK OF ATHANASIUS KIRCHER
## OF FULDA, PRIEST OF THE SOCIETY OF JESUS <note>The Society of Jesus refers to the Jesuits, a Catholic religious order known for its focus on education and scientific research.</note>

# UNIVERSAL 
# MUSIC-MAKING <term>Musurgia: a term coined by Kircher from Greek roots meaning the "work" or "making" of music.</term>
## OR THE
# GREAT ART
## OF CONSONANCE AND DISSONANCE
### ARRANGED IN TEN BOOKS.

->Wherein the entire doctrine and philosophy of sounds, and the science of music, both theoretical and practical, is handed down with the greatest variety; the wondrous powers and effects of Consonance and Dissonance in the world—and thus in all of nature—are opened and demonstrated through a new and exotic display of various examples for unique uses, not only in nearly every academic faculty, but especially in Philology, Mathematics, Physics, Mechanics, Medicine, Politics, Metaphysics, and Theology.<-

### Volume I.

<image-desc>A central oval frame contains an illustration of a butterfly resting atop a lyre. The lyre has six visible strings and a decorative base. Surrounding this central emblem is a circular library stamp from the Universidad Complutense de Madrid. To the left and right of the oval are short poetic inscriptions.</image-desc>

<margin>
The cicada strives 
to strike the quill, <note>original: "plectra," the tool used to pluck strings.</note>
[repairing] the broken [string]
and [besting] the pride of Eunomius,
</margin>

<margin>
it suddenly supplied 
 the string for the victor 
with its voice. 
From an ancient gem of Aristo. <note>This refers to a famous Greek legend where the musician Eunomius was competing in a contest; when a string on his lyre broke, a cicada landed on the instrument and sang the missing note, allowing him to win.</note>
</margin>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page serves as the table of contents and summary for the entire work, outlining the structure of the two volumes and the specific themes of the ten books. It follows the main title page translated previously.</meta>

## SYNOPSIS
# OF UNIVERSAL MUSIC-MAKING <term>Musurgia Universalis: Kircher’s title for his comprehensive study of the "work" or "making" of music.</term>
### DISTRIBUTED INTO X. BOOKS.

->Of which the first seven are contained in Volume 1. The remaining three in Volume 2.<-

---

**Book I.** *Physiological*, demonstrates the natural Genesis <note>The origins or creation.</note>, nature, properties, and effects of sound.
**Book II.** *Philological*, investigates the first institution and propagation of artificial sound, or Music.
**Book III.** *Arithmetical*, teaches the science of harmonic motions through numbers and a new Algebraic Music.
**Book IV.** *Geometrical*, shows the origin of consonant-dissonant intervals through the Geometric, Algebraic, and Mechanical division of the monochord <term>Monochord: an ancient scientific instrument with a single string used to measure musical intervals.</term> in multiple varieties.
**Book V.** *Organic*, opens the structure of musical instruments of every kind through new experiments.
**Book VI.** *Melothetic* <note>From the Greek roots for "melody" and "placing" or "composition."</note>, produces a new and demonstrative method for composing every kind of song; and it contains whatever can be desired concerning this curious, rare, and secret business.
**Book VII.** *Diacritic* <note>From the Greek for "distinguishing" or "judging."</note>, institutes a comparison of ancient music with the modern, detects abuses, commends the dignity of Ecclesiastical song, and opens the method by which the perfection of "pathetic" <note>In this context, "pathetic" refers to music intended to evoke deep emotion or "pathos."</note> music may finally be reached.
**Book VIII.** *Miraculous*, exhibits a new "Musarithmic" art <note>Kircher's invention: a system of mechanical composition using tables and slides.</note>, by which anyone, even one unskilled in music, can reach a perfect knowledge of composing in a short time; it contains Combinatorial, Poetic, and Rhetorical Music. It adapts the Musarithmic art to the Universal Language <note>original: "Panglossiani," referring to Kircher's system for a universal tongue.</note> through a new device.
**Book IX.** *Magical*, produces the more hidden secrets of all Music; it contains the Physiology of consonance and dissonance. Furthermore, it teaches Musico-medical Magic; "Phonoicamptics" <term>Phonoicamptica: Kircher's term for the science of echoes and sound reflection.</term> (or the perfect doctrine regarding the Echo, how it is to be measured and constituted); the new construction of "otic" <note>Hearing-related.</note> or auricular tubes; likewise the structure of Autophones (self-sounding instruments) such as statues and other musical instruments, as well as Sympathetic structures, through curious and new experiments. To which is added the Cryptology of Music, by which the hidden concepts of the mind are manifested at a distance through sounds.
**Book X.** *Analogical*, exhibits the "Decachordon" <note>A ten-stringed instrument, used here as a metaphor for the harmony of the universe.</note> of nature, by which it is demonstrated through 10 steps—as if through 10 Registers of Nature—that God respected musical proportions in the making of the 3 Worlds: the Elemental, the Celestial, and the Archetypal.

<image-desc>A large curly brace encompasses the following ten "Register" entries, with the word "exhibet" (he/it exhibits) printed to the right of its center point.</image-desc>

**Register 1.** The Symphonies of the Elements, or Elementary Music.
**Register 2.** The admirable Symphony of the Heavens in their motions, influences, and effects.
**Register 3.** [The Symphony] of Stones, Plants, and Animals, in Physical, Medical, and Chemical matters.
**Register 4.** The Music of the Microcosm with the Megacosm, that is, of the smaller [man] with the greater world.
**Register 5.** "Sphygmic" <note>From the Greek for "pulse."</note> Music, or that manifesting itself in the pulse of the veins and arteries.
**Register 6.** Ethical Music, shining forth in the sensitive and rational appetite.
**Register 7.** Political, Monarchic, Aristocratic, Democratic, and Economic Music.
**Register 8.** Metaphysical Music, or the comparison of interior Powers to the Angels and God.
**Register 9.** Hierarchical Music, or that of the Angels distributed into 9 choirs.
**Register 10.** Archetypal Music, or the harmony of God with all of nature.

->Upon a ten-stringed psaltery I will sing praises unto thee. <note>A reference to Psalm 144:9.</note><-

<summary>This page provides a systematic overview of the ten books of the Musurgia Universalis, ranging from the physics of sound and mechanical composition to the "Music of the Spheres" and divine harmony.</summary>
<keywords>synopsis, monochord, musarithmical art, echoes, musical magic, decachordon, microcosm, celestial symphony, musical cryptology</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page follows the blank flyleaf and serves as the formal dedication of the work. It establishes the relationship between the author, Athanasius Kircher, and his patron, Archduke Leopold Wilhelm of Austria.</meta>

<image-desc>A rectangular decorative header composed of six horizontal rows of repeated ornamental fleurons, creating a dense pattern of floral and geometric motifs across the top of the page.</image-desc>

## TO THE MOST SERENE PRINCE
# LEOPOLD
## WILHELM
### ARCHDUKE OF AUSTRIA,

->Grand Master of the Teutonic Order, Governor of Belgium<-
->and Burgundy, etc.<-
->To my Most Clement Lord.<-
->**ATHANASIUS KIRCHER**<-
->*of the Society of Jesus,*<-
->prays for his Life and Happiness.<-

<image-desc>A large historiated woodcut initial 'S' featuring the letter surrounded by ornate foliage and scrollwork, flanked by two figures, possibly putti, within a square frame.</image-desc>

THE **GREAT ART OF CONSONANCE AND DISSONANCE** <note>original: "ARS MAGNA CONSONI, ET DISSONI." This is the subtitle of Kircher's massive work on music and harmony, "Musurgia Universalis," published in 1650.</note> presents itself before the most radiant sight of YOUR Serenity. It does so at the very time when your Germany—the parent of the Roman Empire and of great Princes—recalls the excessively discordant <note>meaning "clashing" or "unharmonious"</note> customs of the Christian Republic to a tranquil harmony of peace. **GOD**, **NATURE**, and **POLITICS** <note>original: "POLITICE," referring here to the science of governing a state</note> teach us that consonance <note>harmony</note> cannot possibly exist without dissonance, nor dissonance without consonance. **GOD THE BEST AND GREATEST** <note>original: "DEVS OPT. MAX." or "Deus Optimus Maximus," a standard Latin epithet for God.</note> in his most wise moderation of the world, while he distinguishes all things by number, solidifies them by weight, and defines them by measure, what else does he fulfill but the office of a <term>Harmosta</term> <note>From the Greek "harmostes," meaning a regulator or one who fits things together into a harmonious whole.</note> perfect in every part? The midwifing hand of **NATURE**, relying on this same aid within the hidden fibers of the things committed to her care, what [does she]...

<sig>* 2 .</sig>
<meta>The text on this page ends mid-sentence; the catchword "non" indicates the next page begins with "non" (not).</meta>

<summary>This page is a formal dedication by the Jesuit scholar Athanasius Kircher to Archduke Leopold Wilhelm of Austria, framing his book on music theory as a metaphor for divine and political harmony.</summary>
<keywords>Athanasius Kircher, Archduke Leopold Wilhelm, Musurgia Universalis, music theory, Baroque patronage, harmony and discord, Teutonic Order</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the formal dedication to Archduke Leopold Wilhelm, following the opening address which established God, Nature, and Politics as the primary domains of harmony.</meta>

...does He not build? Finally, in **POLITICS**, in tempering empires and directing peoples toward the goals of happiness, He employs this unique art as a mistress and guide. Nor is it a wonder: for this is the sole discipline of order, the measure of things, the basis of human actions, and the support of worldly preservation.

**YOU, MOST SERENE PRINCE**, represent these three <note>God, Nature, and Politics</note> through your outstanding deeds and virtues. We contemplate the justice of your clemency, the piety within your fortitude, and the modesty within your magnanimity, all working in agreement within your breast. Indeed, you are entirely harmonious, an admirable <term>Harmost</term> <note>original: "Harmosta." Originally a Spartan term for a military governor, Kircher uses it here to mean a "harmonizer" or "tuner" of the state.</note> who seizes not only the hearts of your subjects but also those of your enemies; though they may often clash in customs and religion, they nonetheless sound in unison with you in their affection and will. For you Princes participate in a divine <term>harmonizing power</term> <note>original: "ἁρμόζουσαν" (harmózousan)</note> that creates a universal symphony. Just as God, through that power, brings celestial and earthly things into accord at once, in no different way do you couple peoples to Kings and kingdoms to kingdoms in an admirable covenant—acting, in a way, as mimics and vicars of divinity. 

If the Socratic Sirens <note>A reference to the Platonic idea of Sirens sitting upon the celestial spheres, creating the "music of the spheres."</note> used to chant praises to the supreme Architect with the sound of the celestial orbs, it would be fitting for the mouths of all humanity to be opened in praise of good Princes as well. But, as the saying goes, it is a type of the most exquisite music to "sing within." What excellent <term>Harmosts</term> achieve through their art, we perform out of necessity: being unequal to the task of describing the most singular merits of **YOUR** Serenity, we celebrate them with a devoted silence. 

As for me, who like the Egyptian <term>Memnon</term> <note>Kircher refers to the "Singing Statues" of Memnon in Thebes, which were famous in antiquity for emitting a musical sound when struck by the first rays of the morning sun.</note> have more than once been stirred to song by the rays of **YOUR** munificent Serenity, I would be harder than that stony Memnon himself if I did not resonate in some way with the perpetual faith of a grateful soul toward **YOUR** truly royal benevolence. 

Farewell, ornament of the Imperial House, pillar of Germany, delight of Belgium, model for Princes, and unique exemplar of the Great Art of Consonance and Dissonance.

->Rome, December 8, 1649.<-

<summary>Athanasius Kircher concludes his dedication to Archduke Leopold Wilhelm, praising the Prince as a "Harmost" (harmonizer) who reflects divine order in his governance and personal virtue. He compares his own response to the Prince's patronage to the musical statue of Memnon responding to the sun.</summary>
<keywords>Leopold Wilhelm of Austria, Harmost, political harmony, Socratic Sirens, Colossus of Memnon, music of the spheres, patronage, Habsburg</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the prose dedication to Archduke Leopold Wilhelm into a poetic tribute (a "Canzone") authored by Pompeo Colonna. It continues the theme of the Archduke as a "harmonizer" of the state, now expanding the definition of music to encompass all sensory experiences of beauty and order.</meta>

->TO THE MOST SERENE<-

# L E O P O L D

## ARCHDUKE OF AUSTRIA

->On the Great Art of the Consonant and the Dissonant<- <note>This refers to the subtitle of Kircher's monumental work, *Musurgia Universalis*: "The Great Art of Consonance and Dissonance."</note>

->BY FATHER ATHANASIUS KIRCHER<-

->A S O N G.<- <note>original: "Canzone." A traditional Italian verse form often used for high-minded or philosophical subjects.</note>

->BY THE MOST ILLUSTRIOUS AND EXCELLENT LORD<-

# P O M P E O C O L O N N A

## PRINCE OF GALLICANO. <note>Pompeo Colonna (died 1661) was an Italian nobleman and poet, prince of the Gallicano estate near Rome, and a patron of the arts.</note>

---

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'S' at the start of the poem, featuring a landscape scene with figures and classical architecture.</image-desc>

*LORD, you who among instruments of war,*
*To make the burdens of your scepter less heavy,*
*Turn to listen to sweet voices,*
*And fill your heart with musical harmonies.*

*And showing your noble breast at once strong and gentle*
*In such delights,*
*You make your subjects recognize in you*
*A joyful yoke, mixed of love and fear.* <note>The "joyful yoke" is a classical paradox describing a ruler who is both loved for his kindness and feared for his power, maintaining a "harmonic" balance in the state.</note>

*It is indeed right that if ATHANASIUS*
*Now explains the breadth of the musical art,*
*To your name, which the world so prizes,*
*He consecrates the pages of his profound knowledge.*

*Music is not only that which we hear,*
*But all that is smelled and touched,*
*And all that the mouth ever tastes,*
*Which we call flavor, and everything we ever see.* <note>Colonna is introducing Kircher’s core philosophy: universal harmony. In this view, "music" is the mathematical proportion found in all beautiful things, whether they are sounds, buildings, or flavors.</note>

*One looks upon a man, a building, a face,*
*And to some it brings delight, and to others displeasure.*
*Know, Lord, that it is a silent music*
*Which charms one person and turns another to hatred;*

<meta>catchword: S’ac.</meta>

<summary>A poetic dedication by Prince Pompeo Colonna to Archduke Leopold, framing the Archduke's leadership as a form of harmony and introducing the concept that "music" exists in all sensory perceptions of beauty and proportion.</summary>
<keywords>Leopold of Austria, Pompeo Colonna, Athanasius Kircher, Musurgia Universalis, universal harmony, silent music, aesthetics, patronage</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Canzone" by Pompeo Colonna. The previous page ended mid-sentence, introducing the idea that music extends to all senses (smell, touch, taste). This page elaborates on how the "humors" of a person determine whether they perceive something as harmonious or discordant, specifically focusing on sight (color), smell, and taste.</meta>

*What brings delight and creates harmony*
*Aligns with the humors* <note>humors: In early modern medicine, the four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, black bile, and yellow bile—thought to determine a person's physical health and personality.</note> *of the one who beholds it.*
*But to another it must bring distress*
*If it clashes with their own, turning them to anger.*

*From this, the common proverb was born:*
*That only what seems beautiful to someone truly is so,*
*And natural beauty is worth very little*
*If it is not judged to be so by the eye.*

<margin>A</margin> *As for colors: one is fond of Azure,*
*While another abhors it and turns the eye to green;*
*There is another who finds purple pleasing,*
*While another scorns it, satisfied with yellow.*

*Nature, my Lord, wove within her womb*
*Certain primary colors first;*
*In whose likeness she then grafted*
*The four humors into the human creature.*

*Now, just as these, mixed in various ways,*
*Create infinite varieties of different humors,*
*So too do those colored forms,*
*Through new mixtures, acquire new qualities.*

*And if, among those mixtures that create*
*A harmonic proportion,* <note>proporzione armonica: A mathematical relationship between parts that creates a pleasing whole, a concept applied to both music and visual arts.</note> *the eye of man*
*Finds agreement with those colors, he is at peace;*
*But if it is not found there, they are held in hatred.*

*Understand the same of scents and flavors:*
*When a scent brings distress to one*
*While bringing delight to another, it leads you to contemplate,*
*And there you perceive a hidden music.*

*I know a man who, if by chance*
*He happens to catch the scent of a crimson rose,*
*Runs a near-mortal danger to his life;* <note>This refers to the concept of "antipathy," where an individual's specific biological makeup (humors) reacts violently against a particular substance, a topic Kircher explored deeply in his scientific studies.</note>
*Such is the dissonance hidden within him.*

<summary>This page of the poem explores the subjectivity of beauty and the senses, arguing that "harmony" is a result of a physical alignment between external objects and a person's internal "humors." It uses the examples of color preferences and allergic reactions to scents to illustrate that music (as a principle of order) exists in all sensory experiences.</summary>
<keywords>humors, harmony, subjectivity, color theory, aesthetic perception, dissonance, antipathy, sensory music</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Canzone" by Pompeo Colonna. Having previously discussed how sight, smell, and taste rely on a "hidden music" or harmony with an individual's humors, the poet now extends this musical theory to the field of medicine and the sense of touch.</meta>

<sig>K</sig>

*If the follower of Hippocrates and Galen,* <note>Hippocrates and Galen: The foundational figures of ancient Western medicine whose theories of the "four humors" dominated medical practice until the 19th century.</note>
*Understood music in his healing:*
*And if, through his studies, he knew how to investigate*
*The various notes of Nature in full:*

*He would perceive, with less harm to us,*
*That illnesses are not removed by opposites,* <note>The author challenges the traditional medical maxim of "contraria contrariis curantur" (opposites are cured by opposites), suggesting instead a "consonance" or sympathy between the cure and the disease.</note>
*But he would see over time those natural consonances*
*That the remedy shares with the disease:*

*He would see how Rhubarb* <note>original: "Reobarbaro." Historically used in medicine as a powerful purgative to clear the bowels of "excess" bile.</note> *carries*
*Bile away from the body with it,*
*Because it possesses a quality similar to it,*
*And thus restores the sick to health.*

*A body in which poisonous leprosy creeps,*
*Wretched, if deprived of all human aid;*
*When no medical hand can help,*
*A serpent then, moved by pity, cures it.* <note>This likely refers to "Theriac," a famous ancient medical concoction that often included viper flesh as a primary ingredient to treat poisons and skin diseases.</note>

*The Viper’s venom levels the Viper.*
*And the Scorpion is the remedy for the Scorpion.*
*And if ever a pestilential season arrives,*
*The pestilential Toad heals the plague.* <note>The poet is describing "sympathetic medicine," the belief that a creature that causes a sickness (like a venomous animal) also contains the harmony required to cure it.</note>

*This is all because that concordant harmony,*
*Which exists between the remedy and the sickness, unites them.*
*From this is born the effect*
*Of drawing out that which is discordant with man.*

*For what else is health but consonance,*
*Born from the mixture of one humor with another?*
*And what else brings distress to the heart*
*But discordant and dissolute qualities?*

*Now you will have seen through all five senses,*
*If you add touch to those already told:*
*That whatever was ever made perceivable*
*Belongs to either the consonant or the dissonant.*

<meta>catchword: For (Cui)</meta>

<summary>On this page, the poet argues that medicine is a form of music, where healing occurs through "consonance" (sympathy) between a remedy and an illness rather than through opposites. He concludes that all five senses—culminating in touch—are governed by the musical principles of harmony and discord.</summary>

<keywords>humoral medicine, Hippocrates, Galen, sympathetic healing, Rhubarb, leprosy, Theriac, harmony and dissonance, five senses, Pompeo Colonna</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "Canzone" by Pompeo Colonna. Having previously discussed "sympathetic" medicine—where like cures like, such as viper venom treating leprosy—the poet now explores how individual temperaments find "harmony" with specific physical qualities (heat, cold, texture) before introducing Athanasius Kircher as the ultimate master of these hidden natural laws.</meta>

One likes the cold, and another delights in heat:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;One is healthier in winter, and another in summer,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And by some, dry things are craved,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And on the contrary, others crave the wet. <note>These four qualities—hot, cold, dry, and moist—were the building blocks of the "humoral theory" that explained human health and personality in the 17th century.</note>

And if we look at the fertile qualities [of touch],
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;One touches the hard willingly, another the soft,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;There is one who exalts the rough and the knotty,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And one to whom liquid things are pleasant.

All are streams from the same fountain.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;But perhaps my speech would be too long,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;If his other merits were to follow still,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And his praises were fully made known.

**ATHANASIUS** <note>original: "ATANASIO." This refers to Athanasius Kircher (1602–1680), a famous Jesuit polymath known as the "Master of a Hundred Arts."</note> it will be, who shall supply
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;What my short speech lacks, by unveiling high mysteries
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Previously unknown to the common people, but true,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Whereby human genius remains absorbed.

**ATHANASIUS**, that one, from whom is not hidden
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Anything written in any language,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Whereby he can share with everyone
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The most abstruse and deepest information; <note>Kircher claimed to be able to read almost every known language, including those of the ancient Near East.</note>

He who, without having proven the risks
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Of exposing himself to make the voyage there,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Fully knows how to explain the pyramids
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And the obelisks of portentous Egypt. <note>Kircher published "Oedipus Aegyptiacus," claiming to have deciphered hieroglyphics through symbolic and mystical means long before the Rosetta Stone was found.</note>

You tell it, Rome, to whom through him are known
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The meanings of so many silent stones. <note>original: "muti sassi." A reference to the Egyptian obelisks standing in Rome's piazzas, which Kircher interpreted for the Popes.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Let the schools speak, and the remote nations
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Now made rich with unknown languages.

He will unfold those harmonic orders which the Earth
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Preserves in its production,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And the peaceful union, and the war
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Of animals among themselves, and herb with herb. <note>This refers to "sympathy" and "antipathy" in nature—the idea that certain plants and animals are naturally "friends" or "enemies" based on their inner harmonies.</note>

<note>Vocabulary: Athanasius, Egypt, pyramids, obelisks, Rome, languages, mysteries, harmonics</note>

<meta>This page continues the "Canzone" (song) by Pompeo Colonna, dedicated to the Jesuit scholar Athanasius Kircher. In the rich kingdom of minerals, then,
Nature crafts wondrous works,
From which naturalists will receive
Weighty news of memorable consonances. <note>In the 17th century, "naturalists" were scholars of natural history and science. Here, the poet suggests that even stones and metals contain hidden musical proportions and "consonances."</note>

<image-desc>A musical staff with five lines containing a short melody in early modern diamond-shaped notation.</image-desc>

[He will tell] how one element shares
Its quality with another, and how it then passes
With regulated, harmonious steps
To form the noble concert of mixed things. <note>Aristotelian physics held that all matter was a "mixture" of the four elements (Earth, Water, Air, Fire). The poet frames this chemical combination as a musical arrangement.</note>

<image-desc>A musical staff with five lines containing a short melody in early modern notation.</image-desc>

He will tell of it. And how from the ethereal fields,
From those beautiful bodies adorned with splendor, <note>A reference to the stars and planets.</note>
With an ordered and stable tenor,
The flashes of light descend, influencing here below. <note>The word "influendo" refers to astrological "influence"—the belief that celestial bodies emit invisible flows that affect earthly life and health.</note>

<image-desc>A musical staff with five lines containing a short melody in early modern notation.</image-desc>

And how, finally, by ascending to the Creator
From the contemplation of created music:
No living thing is found here below
That does not sing of its Maker, even if it be mute. <note>This reflects the concept of *musica mundana*—the "music of the world"—the silent harmony of the cosmos and nature that reflects divine order.</note>

<image-desc>A musical staff with five lines containing a short melody in early modern notation.</image-desc>

How with ineffable harmony
That divine Trinity unites as one,
And how our Soul may enjoy
That eternal good in supreme melody. <note>original: "divino Ternario." The poet connects the musical triad (a chord of three notes) to the Christian Holy Trinity.</note>

<image-desc>A musical staff with five lines containing a short melody in early modern notation.</image-desc>

Now, why does that noble hand still delay,
And not yet unfold its illustrious labors?
My Muse prays to you in the name of all,
That you give them to us: now let not the prayer be in vain.

<image-desc>A musical staff with five lines containing a short melody in early modern notation.</image-desc>

But I already hear the fortunate linens
Being pressed by the printing press for the common good:
Let Rome now rejoice, and the farthest provinces,
In these displayed concerts, sublime and divine. <note>The "fortunate linens" refers to the paper, which was made from linen rags during this period. The "printing press" (torchio) indicates that Kircher's book is currently being manufactured.</note>

You, magnanimous **LEOPOLD**, in the meantime,
While **ATHANASIUS** prepares the learned pages for you,
Listen with a kindly ear and receive
(Whatever it may be) the song of my Muse. <note>"Leopoldo" refers to Archduke Leopold (later Emperor Leopold I), a talented musician and Kircher's patron. "Atanasio" is Athanasius Kircher himself.</note>

# MUSICAL GAMES PRESENTED IN HONOR OF THE MOST SERENE ARCHDUKE.

A four-voice fugue <note>original: "Fuga Tetraphona." In this period, a "fuga" often referred to what we now call a canon, where voices enter one after another singing the same melody.</note> based on the six musical notes <note>original: "sex Voces musicas." These are the six syllables of the hexachord: Ut, Re, Mi, Fa, Sol, La.</note> at the lower octave with a fifth, at the fifth, and at the fourth; in which (if desired) a fifth part harmonizes in tenths with the Bass. It can also be transposed and sung in various ways and voices, especially in the two following ways.

## A musical word-puzzle in the form of a "hypertritus" canon, in contrary motion. <note>A "logogriphus" is a word-puzzle. This musical version is a canon where the second voice moves in "contrary motion"—when the first voice goes up, the second goes down. "Hypertritus" is a technical term for a specific rhythmic or melodic proportion.</note>

<image-desc>A musical staff with a C-clef and diamond-shaped notes. Text is interspersed between the notes: 'ma te nequit cat inclyta virtus.'</image-desc>

->Resolution of the Word-Puzzle.<-

->**Fame cannot remain hidden; illustrious virtue shines like the sun.**<-
<note>original: "**FAma LATeRE nequit MICat VT SOL inclyta virtus.**" This is a "musical rebus." The capitalized syllables correspond to the musical notes on the staff: Fa, La, Re, Mi, Ut, and Sol. The sentence only makes sense when the names of the notes are read as part of the words.</note>

<meta>This page serves as the transition from the celebratory musical games dedicated to Archduke Leopold Wilhelm into the formal theoretical portion of the book. It introduces Athanasius Kircher’s primary philosophical motivation for writing this work: the belief that light and sound follow the same universal laws of harmony.</meta>

# FIRST PREFACE

## TO THE READER

<image-desc>A large ornamental drop cap 'S' begins the first paragraph, set within a square decorative frame featuring elaborate floral and scrolling leaf motifs.</image-desc>

<margin>Book 8</margin>
Truly, in this undertaking of ours, I have realized the truth of that saying by Lucretius <note>Titus Lucretius Carus (c. 99–55 BC) was a Roman poet and philosopher.</note>:

> Just as wave is pushed by wave,
> So various endeavors drive restless <note>original: "tumidas," literally "swollen" or "heaving" like a sea</note> minds.

Five years have now slipped away, benevolent Reader, since I presented to the public, according to the small measure of my talent, the work titled <margin>Book 2</margin> *The Great Art of Light and Shadow* <note>original: "ARS MAGNA Lucis & Umbræ," Kircher's massive 1646 treatise on optics and light.</note>. In that work, I gathered together a vast variety of subjects, including everything that could be related to sight, the properties of visible things, and the logic and utility of shadows—in other words, the Science of Vision. <margin>Book 3</margin> When I learned that this work was received by the Republic of Letters <note>The "Republic of Letters" was the long-distance intellectual community of scholars and scientists in the 17th century.</note> with a favorable eye, a desire to always prepare new things immediately took hold of my mind, which was pregnant with foreign ideas. That is to say, a most burning desire seized me to treat Acoustics <note>original: "Acusticen"</note>, the sister of Vision—or the workshop of hearing—with a similar argument and an equal abundance and wealth of material.

I established a certain parallel comparison, for I found a remarkable affinity between Light and Shadow and Concord and Discord <note>original: "Consonum & dissonum," referring to musical harmony and clashing sounds.</note>. Indeed, I found that light is nothing other than a kind of "concord" for the eyes, while shadow is something like "discord" for the eyes. <margin>Book 6</margin> Yet, from the mixing of both, a great fitness and a remarkable harmony is born, placed into the world by Nature. Thus, it was not difficult for my laboring mind to apply all the secret mysteries of Light and Shadow to Concord and Discord through a certain analogy. However, as for the method and way of exhausting the difficulties that stood in the way—"here is Rhodes, here is the jump" <note>original: "hic Rhodus, hic Saltus." A Latin proverb meaning "here is the challenge, prove what you can do here and now."</note>.

<margin>Book 5</margin> For the science of sound has its boundaries stretched out no less than those of light, and its inner chambers are utterly hidden and hitherto inaccessible. It can hardly be described what incredible labors had to be exhausted to overcome these, or how much sweat was required to resolve this subtle and arduous faculty into its genuine principles. Nevertheless, the immense and tireless impulse of my mind and an innate inclination for investigating the treasures of hidden nature conquered every labor. Because of this, I have left nothing untried and nothing unexplored so that I might finally reach my conceived goals.

And so, I have begun this ten-part work <note>original: "Decupartitum Opus"</note>, which, following a certain analogy to Light and Shadow, we call the Great Art of Concord and Discord, or *Universal Music-Making* <note>original: "Musurgia Universalis"</note>. In this work, <margin>Book 4</margin> as if on a well-ordered ten-stringed instrument <note>original: "decachordo," referring to the ten books of the treatise.</note>, we demonstrate the origin and propagation of sounds and the remarkable variety emanating from them. <margin>Book 7</margin> At last, this present "Organ," constructed from Concords and Discords, has emerged—a thing to be wondered at in the arrangement of its registers—which we place before the eyes of the curious Reader in these ten books. So that the Reader may see deeply into our mind, we have thought it best to place an "Idea" <note>An overview or conceptual map.</note> of the entire work here.

Since it was necessary to perfectly distinguish the innermost recesses of "phonosophy" <note>original: "phonosophias," a word Kircher coined meaning the "wisdom of sound."</note> and to build another structure of the musical encyclopedia upon foundations of equal depth, and since it would be difficult for these to lie without a perfect knowledge of the acoustic and phonic organ <note>The human ear and vocal apparatus.</note>, I first had to attempt a living anatomy of the said organs. I sought to understand to what end provident Nature ordained so many channels in the ear, so many bones, so many cartilages, and so many pathways for sound and voice; and with what intention she arranged so many nerves and muscles in the larynx as the parents of sound and voice. From this, that great sound...

<summary>Athanasius Kircher introduces his treatise on music, explaining that he intends to apply the same scientific principles to sound (acoustics) that he previously applied to light (optics), treating harmony as the auditory equivalent of light.</summary>
<keywords>Athanasius Kircher, acoustics, optics, harmony, Musurgia Universalis, phonosophy, anatomy of the ear, concord and discord, music theory</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's introductory preface, moving from his personal motivations to a structured summary of the first seven books of the treatise. It outlines his systematic approach to music, starting from biological anatomy and moving through mathematics, geometry, and eventually to the emotional power of composition.</meta>

# PREFACE I

<margin>Book I</margin>
Wise nature has led us to understand, as if from a certain anatomical spring, how the diversity of sounds and the modulation of voices shine forth, both in the collision of solid bodies and in the vocal organs of animals. All of these matters are treated extensively in the **First Book**, in which we have first explained the exact anatomy of both humans and animals who were designed by nature for voice.

<margin>Book II</margin>
In the course of time, human industry refined these sounds—which were previously unformed and disordered <note>original: "ἀτάκτως," Greek for "without order"</note>—and organized them according to their composition <note>original: "κατὰ τὴν σύνθεσιν"</note> into distinct syllables, words, and sentences. Just as this gave rise to languages and dialects in the human Republic, so too did the origin of all harmony emerge from the qualities of sounding bodies, specifically the intervals between high and low sounds. All of these topics are pursued at length in the **Second Book**, which treats the first origins of <term>Artistic Music</term> and poetry, the construction of ancient instruments, and those used by the first men of the world, as well as the Hebrews and Greeks.

<margin>Book III</margin>
Furthermore, since I realized that sounds of this kind could by no means be understood without numbers and proportions, I have scrutinized the more hidden corners of the science of numbers. I have left nothing undone to place the sources and origins of each before the eyes of the Curious Reader, revealed and fortified by the undeniable arguments of demonstrations. This is what the **Third Book** aptly provides, in which we have treated the various forms of harmonic numbers with the greatest possible diligence.

<margin>Book IV</margin>
Additionally, noting that the science of sound claims for itself a great part of the science of Geometry, and supported by its aid, I have investigated the various qualities of sounding bodies through the division of strings and chords. I have attempted to assign the logic of each in musical theory through various divisions of the <term>monochord</term> <note>A monochord is an ancient scientific and musical instrument consisting of a single string stretched over a soundboard, used to demonstrate the mathematical relationships between musical intervals.</note>. The **Fourth Book** (which we therefore call the *Monochord* or *Musical Geometry*) presents this with the greatest variety.

<margin>Book V</note>
It remained for those things which I had contemplated in various ways according to theory <note>original: "κατὰ τὴν θεωρίαν"</note> in the preceding book to finally be put into practice. Thus came the **Fifth Book**, which exhibits the foundations of the entire <term>symphoniurgy</term> <note>original: "symphoniurgia," Kircher's term for the art of composing harmony.</note> or <term>melothesia</term> <note>original: "melothesia," the art of composing melody.</note>. It provides the logic and methods for properly arranging intervals through an innumerable variety of subjects. In this book, you will find whatever is rare, unusual, hidden, new, or ingenious in the art of composition, treated with a manifold abundance of examples through specimens of exquisite models.

<margin>Book VI</margin>
Truly, as I realized that nothing in the preceding sections was so hidden or obscure that it could not be applied to instruments, I organized **Instrumental Music**, or the construction of instruments. We have exhibited the qualities of proportions latent within each, the method for properly dividing and arranging them, and countless other things that are entirely curious and hitherto attempted by no one, unless you except **Mersenne** <note>Marin Mersenne (1588–1648) was a French polymath often called the "father of acoustics"; Kircher here acknowledges him as his only contemporary peer in this field.</note>. All of these things the **Sixth Book** reveals.

<margin>Book VII</margin>
Since I saw that a great controversy was being stirred up among musicians regarding the primacy of Ancient versus Modern Music, we have composed the **Seventh Book**. In it, by means of a certain parallel comparison, we explain the nature and the characteristic qualities of both ancient and modern music. We restore **Pathetic Music** <note>In the 17th century, "Pathetic" referred to the "pathos" or the power of music to move the human emotions and passions.</note>, and we teach a new method for **Chromatic and Enharmonic** compositions, previously unseen and unheard of. In a word, in this entire book, we exhibit whatever is unusual, rare, and recondite in the whole scope of music—not for beginners <note>original: "Tyronibus"</note>, but for the Masters in the art, and those most supreme and skilled. Therefore, I trust that all who drink these prescribed rules deeply into their minds will, in a short time, arrive not only at a perfect knowledge of composition, but also at that more hidden part of Music which, by a certain admirable virtue and nearly divine energy, seizes and agitates men into whatever emotions it desires.

<summary>Kircher provides an overview of the first seven books of his treatise, moving from the biological anatomy of voice to the mathematical and geometric foundations of sound, and finally to the emotional and "divine" power of musical composition.</summary>
<keywords>anatomy of voice, musical history, harmonic numbers, monochord, symphoniurgy, melothesia, Marin Mersenne, Pathetic music, music theory</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>The previous page introduced the first three books of the treatise, covering the anatomy of voice and the mathematical foundations of musical intervals. This page transitions into the more advanced "Universal" methods of the later books, specifically focusing on automatic composition, secret communication, and the "magic" of acoustics.</meta>

->TO THE READER.<-
†† 3
<meta>catchword: this</meta>

# TO THE READER.

But truly, so that we might not seem to have prepared this work solely for the benefit of musicians, and so that we might extend this "Universal Method" prescribed in the preceding books as far as the limits of human genius allow, we have devised a wonderful system of *Music-making* <term>Musurgia</term>, which the **Eighth Book** teaches. <margin>Book VIII.</margin> Through the assistance of the *Art of Combinations* <term>Combinatoria: the mathematical study of how sets can be arranged</term> and using rhythmic musical patterns <term>musarithmos: Kircher’s term for mathematical formulas used to compose music</term>, we present to the world a new art—one never before attempted by anyone, to my knowledge. This art is built upon such a hidden mechanical arrangement that anyone, in a very short amount of time, even if they are entirely unskilled in music, can reach a perfect understanding of composition. A specimen of this was first exhibited here in Rome—to the great admiration of many men of the highest authority and dignity—by Bernardino Rocci, the nephew of Cardinal Ciriaco Rocci and a most worthy prelate endowed with all natural talents; I encourage the reader to contemplate this where it is placed in its proper section. Indeed, to demonstrate the vast scope of this invention more fully, we have adapted the method to all the principal languages of the world—namely Latin, Greek, Hebrew, Chaldean, Syriac, Arabic, Samaritan, Ethiopic, Armenian, German, Italian, French, Spanish, and Illyrian—so that no nation is so foreign that it could not, by relying on this art, perform learned and elegant compositions set in its own native tongue.

Lest this invention become common and "known even to the bleary-eyed and the barbers," as the saying goes <note>A reference to Horace’s Satires, meaning something so common it is known to everyone</note>, we have intentionally omitted the more hidden and "most universal" <note>original: "καθολικωτάτην," Greek for 'most universal'</note> method, reserving it for Princes, great men, and friends. Furthermore, beneath these musical algorithms <note>original: "Musarithmos"</note> of ours lie certain secrets belonging not only to musicians, but also the hidden and hitherto inaccessible seeds of the deeper sciences and arts of *Chemistry* and *Medicine*. I have no doubt these will flourish into abundant fruit in their own time. Moreover, by occasion of this invention, we have happened upon another far more excellent one, by which one might—with equal daring—write letters in any character and language under heaven, and understand letters written by the same method. But since we are reserving these for a separate work, God willing, it suffices to have merely hinted at them here.

<margin>Book IX.</margin> Furthermore, when I was carried along in a chariot far loftier than that which they say bore Triptolemus <note>A figure in Greek mythology given a winged chariot by Demeter to spread the knowledge of agriculture</note> through every order and class of natural things, I more attentively considered that wonderful marriage of the starry world with the terrestrial. I found nothing in the innermost recesses of the world’s structure that did not have the principles and elements of its composition from *Consonance* and *Dissonance*. Thus, I have constructed a new *Magic of Consonance and Dissonance*, which the **Ninth Book** presents. In this book, after investigating the causes of consonance and dissonance, we show what efficacy music has in medical practice and in stirring the passions of the soul. The explanations for miraculous sounds collected from the histories of all ages are unraveled; the science of *Phonocamptics* <term>Phonocamptica: the science of reflected sound or echoes</term> is drawn from its deepest foundations over an entire book. The new construction of *Otic tubes* <term>Hearing trumpets or acoustic listening devices</term> is described; *Echotectonics* <term>The art of designing buildings or palaces specifically for their acoustic and echo effects</term> to be practiced in the palaces of Princes is taught. Many other miracles of both nature and art to be performed through sound are revealed: the construction of *Automated* instruments, *Hydraulic organs*, and other machines that play themselves perpetually, together with *Cryptology* <term>Secret communication</term>, by which the hidden counsels of the mind are indicated to those who are absent through sounds in a variety of ways. All of these things the Ninth Book shows, as I have said, and which we have therefore not inappropriately named the *Magic of Consonance and Dissonance*.

Since finally music is nothing else than—as Plato testifies—"the order of first ideas..." <note>original: "τάξιν πρώτων ἰδέαι," Kircher begins a Greek quote from Plato regarding the cosmic order of music</note>

<summary>A preview of Books VIII and IX, describing Kircher's invention of an automated musical composition system and his "acoustic magic," which includes echoes, hearing aids, and secret communication via sound.</summary>

->P R E F A C E<-

<margin>Book X.</margin>
...that is, so that I might come to know the series and order of all things. Furthermore, I contemplated that this sensible World is nothing else than a certain brilliant and mystical ten-stringed instrument <term>decachordon: a Greek term for a ten-stringed harp, used here as a metaphor for the universe</term> arranged in ten categories of Being. By this, the Most Wise Creator—using a mixture of Consonants and Dissonants, which is to say, harmonic proportion—produced that admirable harmony and concord of all things, which all Philosophers until now have not been able to admire enough. We have composed the **Tenth Book**, in which, through the ten Symphonisms of Nature—just like the various registers of this great mundane Organ—we have demonstrated the harmonic unity of all things. For you see here, in the mixture of the elements, a certain exact Symphony forged from the harmony of opposites; in the admirable consensus of the Heavens, a most absolute system of harmonic proportions; and in the fabric of the human body, the political world, the Angelic realm, and the Divine, a wondrous order of harmonic proportions, and innumerable other things, which, as I said, the Tenth Book shows most extensively. Not content with this, I have introduced the modes and reasons by which a wise philosopher—with the aid of harmonic proportions—may reach the inner shrines <term>adyta: the most sacred, private parts of a temple</term> of the secret Arts: Physics, Medicine, Chemistry, Logic, Mathematics, Metaphysics, and Theology. And this is the sum of our <term>Musurgia Universalis</term> (Universal Music-making). To ensure this work reached its desired port with favorable winds, it did not lack noble promoters. First among these was the most August and Wise Emperor **FERDINAND III**, our most merciful Lord. Through the great talent in which he excels—a quality truly rare and unusual in so great a Prince—he promoted this work exceedingly, both with his counsel and his assistance. To him was added the Most Serene **Leopold, Archduke of Austria**, who, being equally devoted to both Mars and Minerva <note>A common metaphor: Mars represents military prowess and Minerva represents wisdom and the arts</note>, quite deservedly put the final hand to this work with his generous munificence. For this benefit bestowed upon all posterity, we confess ourselves eternally bound to him. The most Excellent Lord **Pompeius Colonna**, Prince of the Gabi (commonly called of Gallicano), also assisted among other Princes of the City of Rome. He is a man cultivated in every kind of literature; because he believes that nobility of blood is adorned by no better means than the inheritance of knowledge, and because of the highest zeal with which he burns for the promotion of the sciences at all times, he deservedly draws all men into admiration and love of himself. How much he excels with a mind equipped with various scientific knowledge is clearly demonstrated by that learned four-line Ode <term>tetrastycha: a poem of four lines</term> composed in the Italian idiom, which we have prefixed to the work. Added to these our endeavors were various aids from the excellent Musicians of the City of Rome, by which this Work, being adorned, will—if I am not mistaken—sufficiently protect its reputation among posterity.

But if indeed any "Momuses" <note>Momus was the Greek god of mockery; Kircher uses the term here to refer to spiteful critics</note> of a more spiteful nature should rise up against it, it must be borne with the same spirit as all those are forced to bear who, spurred by a similar effort, keep a generous mind continually raised and intent upon any high and sublime things. I have always considered it the mark of a vile and abject mind to care greatly for such plucking and nibbling. Let it suffice me to have fulfilled the commands of the Emperor, the Archduke, and other supreme Princes according to the modesty of my talent; as they incited me to this, so also under their patronage we trust we shall remain safe and secure from all spite of the critics. Finally, if anything worthy of praise should occur in this Work, I would wish it to be ascribed solely to Him from whom all things have flowed, God the All-giver <note>original: πανταδότῃ (pantadotē), a Greek epithet for God as the source of all things</note>. **Farewell.**

->P R E F A C E II.<-

# To the Learned Professors of Musical Affairs.

Having long been engaged in the constant pursuit of musical work, and weighing more precisely the wide and far-reaching scope of all Music, I have wondered greatly why no one in such an illustrious age has stepped forward to cultivate this most universal Faculty from its foundations, according to its true dignity. I was even more stunned when I frequently learned from meetings with musical friends that no Faculty in this time is thought to have reached such a high state of cultivation—to the point where it seems nothing can be added or taken away. 

Therefore, to discover the truth of such a boastful claim, I re-examined the works—bringing them back to the "anvil" <note>original: "monumenta ad incudem reuocans"; a metaphor for refining or rigorously testing work</note>—of not only common authors but also the more excellent ones who have earned no small fame in the world for their mastery of musical doctrine. I decided to evaluate their <term>melothesias: musical compositions or the art of setting melody to text</term> with the greatest care. 

But while I searched into each detail more precisely, I encountered many things that were mangled, defective, awkward, and bound by no consistency of rules. These reveal no small ignorance of the musical profession in these authors (who, to your surprise, are already celebrated through the publication of their books). Many printed volumes are witnesses to this; if you transpose their compositions, you will find nothing applied according to the exact rule <note>original: "amussim," referring to a carpenter’s leveling tool or a strict standard</note> of musical precepts, a fact that has also astonished several more distinguished musicians. 

It is not enough that these works, while performed, tickle the ears of the ignorant with a sweet sound. The hidden errors of composition <note>original: "sphalmata"</note> can easily be covered by the industry of experienced <term>Phonascōrum: voice trainers or singers</term> and hidden so that the <term>Symphonetæ: composers of polyphonic music</term> seem to have achieved something great. But one who scores these works correctly will find errors in the harmonic progressions, in the full harmony, in the very rules commonly known, in the obligations of <term>fugarum: fugues or imitative counterpoint</term>, and in the positioning of accidental signs. These errors are by no means tolerable. When I had gotten a scent of these mistakes, I nearly cast aside the high opinion I had previously held of these otherwise distinguished men. 

Thus, so that Music might finally reach its perfection, I investigated with the most stubborn study all the modes and methods that seemed in any way to contribute to our efforts. I have gathered all of these in **Books V, VI, and VII**, adapting them to theoretical foundations so that I am confident curious musicians will find no small help in perfecting themselves in so noble a faculty.

However, since this task seemed too heavy to be completed by me alone—distracted as I am by countless other literary subjects—I decided to consult through letters the greatest Masters of this time, both those present and those abroad. I did this so that, supported and bolstered by their work and advice, I might execute my planned design with a more secure structure. Since I saw that the systems of musical styles were diverse, and I knew that not everyone was equally excellent and consummate in all areas, I chose those assistants who had attained notable knowledge and fame in a particular style. In this way, in the <insert>ecclesiastical</insert> <note>The text cuts off here with the catchword "clesia-," which completes the word "ecclesiastico" or "ecclesiastical" on the following page.</note> style...

If they do not attain this understanding, I would wish them to ascribe it not to my insufficiency, but to their own unskillfulness and lack of knowledge. Let it suffice that all our works have been minutely examined and approved by public vote by the most excellent <term>Symphoniarchs</term> <note>original: "Symphoniarchis"; conductors or master composers of an ensemble</note> of this time, and in this Roman theater of the City and the World, and have come forth into the public light safe and secure.

I hear, among other things, this objection raised against me: With what face could the Author—since he is not a <term>Musician</term> <note>original: "Musicus"; here meaning a professional practitioner or performer</note> by profession—presume with more than modest audacity to correct and amend masters in the art who were nurtured in it almost from their cradles, and what is more, to offer himself as a Master to them? To these I respond: It is true that I am by no means a Musician by profession, nor have I been, as such a pursuit is foreign to my Religious institute <note>The author refers to his status as a Jesuit priest, whose primary "profession" is theology and the order's work, not secular music.</note>; yet those <gloss>unmusical ones</gloss> <note>original Greek: "ἄμουσοι"</note> shall not condemn me because I have not handed down the elements of the Alphabet to boys as a <term>Schoolmaster</term> <note>original: "Ludimagister"</note>, because I have not acted as a public <term>Choir Leader</term> <note>original: "Choragum"</note> in Churches, or because I have not exhibited myself as a mercenary in compositions for the sake of some gain. Hence, detractors of this kind do not seem to be well-versed in the rules of Logic; for they do not know that this is a very poor inference, and ridiculous in Logic: "He has not professed the Art, therefore he does not know it" (I speak always of that profession by the support of which one sustains life). Did the <term>Prince of Venosa</term> <note>Carlo Gesualdo (1566–1613), a famous nobleman and composer who was not a "professional" musician.</note> not profess Music, therefore he did not know it? <term>Ptolemy</term> and <term>Alphonso</term>, most famous Kings, did not profess Music and Astronomy, therefore they did not know them? This is indeed a very poor inference, as I said, of unskillful musicians—for I speak always of them, and not at all of prudent judges of musical matters. Therefore, although I have never professed music in the stated manner, it is nevertheless known that from my early youth, just as I have applied myself to the more noble arts and sciences, so also I have brooded over the practice of music with the greatest zeal and most persistent labor. Nor should they persuade themselves that I have been occupied only with <term>Theoretical Music</term>; for various compositions of mine, printed however under the names of others, are circulated in Germany to the greatest pleasure of the listeners and are held in high esteem; and the specimens published in this book can abundantly testify to what I know and what I do not know. For since my intention was to restore music, and I judged it <gloss>impossible</gloss> <note>original Greek: "ἀδύνατον"</note> to achieve my aim without Theoretical music, I have always applied myself to both with the most ardent study, so that most skilled Theoretical contemplation might support practical methods that are otherwise weak and infirm. Unless musicians join this theory to practice, let them know for certain that they will labor in vain in advancing music. For <term>Practical Music</term> has certain laws of combination, hidden in the more remote inner chambers of theory; he who knows these will find by experience itself that nothing is denied to him in carrying out practice with the highest perfection and ease. And we have shown this in this book by various specimens. He who calls to the balance the specimens of our wonderful music, even those performed by various people unlearned in music, will recognize the truth of that philosophical <term>Concluding Remark</term> <note>original: "Epiphonema"</note>: "no one gives what he does not have"; since—let boasting be absent from the word—it is my opinion that such things are not produced by everyone, which can be compared without envy to the compositions of the highest Masters in the Art. If, therefore, even <gloss>unmusical</gloss> <note>original Greek: "ἄμουσοι"</note> students can perform such things,

# PREFACE II.

Therefore, let the ignorant judges of things cease to bark, and let them learn what they do not understand; which I trust will happen when they have studiously weighed this *Musurgia* <note>The title of the work, *Musurgia Universalis*, referring to the "work of the Muses" or the craft of music-making</note> of ours, not with a malicious desire for nitpicking, but with a true zeal for making progress. Marin Mersenne <note>A French polymath and priest (1588–1648) often called the "father of acoustics"</note>, a man of supreme standing among the few, published before me a vast volume which he calls *Universal Harmony*; but since in that most laudable work he acted not so much as a practical musician as a philosopher, I judged it to be my role to aptly join this philosophy with mathematics—the bridesmaid of practical music—to show the secret combinations of things, to thoroughly explain the properties of harmonic numbers; and finally, to open a most wide gate for all those who desire to be engaged in cultivating music, not for the sake of profit, but with a true zeal for philosophizing. Having been let in through this gate, they may find treasures hitherto unknown, and thus finally be led by the hand to a perfect knowledge of music. But if perhaps a headlong impulse of goodwill <gloss>original Greek: εὐνοίας (eunoias)</gloss> has deceived me, I see nothing else remaining but to place my praise in the attempt; and to console myself with that sole desire of helping the Republic of Letters <note>The international community of scholars and intellectuals</note> in whatever way I can; and to leave what is imperfect in this work, like seeds having been cast, to be cultivated by others of a further Wisdom <note>original: Minervæ, referring to the Roman goddess of wisdom and the arts</note>. If, however, anything perfect and worthy of praise should be encountered, let them credit it to God, whose honor alone I endeavor to serve. Farewell. And look with favor upon these undertakings.

# JAMES VIVA

->Priest of the same Society <note>The Society of Jesus, or the Jesuits.</note><-

## Greetings to the Benevolent Reader.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut drop cap 'M' within a square frame featuring floral and scrolling motifs.</image-desc>

I also considered it my duty, Kind Reader, to meet with you briefly here regarding this work, the *Universal Music-Making* <note>original: "Musurgiæ Vniuersalis"</note>, as one who has been continuously present at the author's side from start to finish during its publication; thus, I have been permitted to draw for myself no small understanding and knowledge of it.

I foresee, therefore, two types of things you will encounter in this work: some which you will praise, marvel at, and look up to—as they are excellent, rare, curious, new, and profound—especially regarding the business of Music. You should know the author has been exceptionally practiced in this from his earliest youth. Consequently, he began to contemplate this work long ago to provide for the science of Music (which he noticed was neglected and lying idle compared to other disciplines). Finally, under happy auspices—yet only after innumerable consultations with the most skilled masters of musical matters (to which I can testify as an eyewitness)—he has made it public. In this work, moreover, he has revealed to posterity not a few singular secrets of Music (which perhaps someone else might have preferred to keep hidden away as secrets for themselves), so that he might provide a spirit and a spur to other musicians to attempt similar things with laudable effort. For this reason, you will also find in this work some things that are only touched upon briefly and in passing, both so that the opportunity to perfect these things might be granted to others, and so that the work would not grow to an excessive size. Just as there is no doubt that all these things will be of great benefit to the public good hereafter, so no one will deny that they are most worthy of praise and commendation.

Regarding the second point, however: I suspect there will perhaps be some "Zoili" <note>A "Zoilus" is a harsh, nitpicking critic, named after the ancient Greek grammarian known for his biting criticism of Homer.</note> who will attempt to pluck at certain things in this work with a biting tooth. But you, kind Reader, will easily condemn or certainly despise their quibbles and slander if you more attentively consider and weigh in a fair balance: the laborious mass of two volumes completed within the space of a single year; the labors and difficulties of the innumerable figures both in copperplate and woodcut; the abundance and explanation of most intricate subject matters; the recesses of more hidden Music, known to very few, which were penetrated with no small industry (such as, among other things, those matters regarding the Chromatic and Enharmonic genres <note>Technical terms for ancient Greek musical scales that Kircher attempted to revive or explain.</note> which are handed down with an ingenious and new method); the incredible weariness and troubles of musical typesetting; the carelessness of the printers; and finally, the constraints of time. You will not only refrain from severe judgment, but you will also rather strive to easily excuse, protect, and defend with a kind and friendly interpretation whatever happens to be less polished, erroneous, mutilated, or in any way defective. Indeed, perhaps you will marvel not a little at how it was finally possible for such a great mass to rise to this perfection in so little time. And these are the things, Friend Reader, of which I wished to warn you here in passing. Farewell.

***

### OR THE

## BOOK ONE: ANATOMICAL
### ON THE NATURE OF SOUND AND THE VOICE.

## PREFACE
->On the excellence and nobility of hearing.<-

<margin>How sound is made.</margin>
As I am about to establish a universal Idea of the Art of Consonance and Dissonance, shining forth in every single thing of this worldly fabric, it is fitting that I begin its vast dealings there, where its first cradles are established. If, therefore, there were no motion in this visible machine of the world, there would likewise be no striking of bodies; and if this ceased, there would be no agitation of the air. If there were no agitation of the air, all things would remain motionless and silent, condemned to a perpetual silence utterly repugnant to nature. From the motion of bodies, therefore, comes the impulse and the agitation of the air; from the agitation of the air comes the collision of bodies; and finally, from the collision of bodies, various sounds are born according to the condition of the bodies struck. This is the sole object of the <term>acoustic faculty</term> <note>The power or sense of hearing</note> or the organ of hearing. It has seemed right to speak now of its nobility, excellence, and wonderful uses, so that having perceived the beauty of these most worthy things, the curious Reader may apply his mind to acquiring the knowledge and science of these matters, which are now to be more fully explained. You, meanwhile, of the senses...

<summary>This is the opening page and preface of Athanasius Kircher's monumental work on music, establishing that sound originates from physical motion and the agitation of air.</summary>
<keywords>Musurgia Universalis, acoustics, physics of sound, anatomy of hearing, consonance and dissonance, Athanasius Kircher</keywords>

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory discussion on the physics of sound, moving from general definitions to the specific mechanics of how sound is generated through the collision of bodies.</meta>

# Book 1. Anatomical Treatise on the Nature of Sound and Voice.

<margin>
Sound as defined by Aristotle.
</margin>
<margin>
The Author’s definition.
</margin>
[Aristotle] defines it as the motion of that which is capable of being moved by that motion with which things that rebound from bodies struck against each other are moved; some define it as: a sensible quality, and the proper object of the sense of hearing, striking the hearing; Boethius <note>Anicius Manlius Severinus Boethius (c. 480–524 AD), a philosopher whose works on music were foundational in the Middle Ages.</note> says sound is nothing other than a percussion of the air remaining unbroken until it reaches the ears; others define it otherwise. We define sound as: *A successive passive quality produced from the trapping and squeezing out of air or water, following the collision of sounding bodies, suited to move the sense of hearing.* Where it must be noted that three things can be considered in all accidents <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, an "accident" is a property that does not change the essence of a thing (like color or sound).</note>, from the knowledge of which their nature is revealed: namely, their subject, genus, and cause. Depending on the intention and goal of the definer, accidents are defined either by expressing all three, or only the subject and genus while omitting the cause, or by taking the cause alone without expressing the others. The first way provides perfect and absolute knowledge; the two latter ways provide imperfect, lame, and mutilated knowledge. Thus, if an Eclipse is defined as the deprivation of light in the Moon because of the interposition of the earth between the Sun and the Moon, all three elements are contained in the definition. Likewise, thunder is a sound in a cloud <note>The "subject" or location.</note> because of the extinguishing of fire <note>The "cause."</note>; once those three are explained, the definition will be established. We can declare the same thing through the subject and genus alone if we say: "Thunder is a sound in a cloud"; similarly through the cause alone: as in, "it is the extinguishing of fire." We set these things forth so that no one can argue that we have described a triple nature through the definition given, since according to Aristotle a definition is a specific speech explaining the nature of a single thing.

<margin>
What is required for sound.
</margin>
But someone will say that three bodies do not always concur for a sound as the definition indicates, and therefore it cannot perfectly stand, since neither air nor wind—being of a fluid nature and bodies devoid of all hardness and solidity—are suited to produce sound; yet when cloth is torn into pieces, a certain sound is produced, for the production of which neither two bodies nor hard bodies concur, as is also apparent in the voice. I respond that the number of these bodies is not taken from their quantity, but from the variety of actions that intervene to generate sound. Furthermore, we assert that three bodies, distinct in the production of sound, are required—if not distinct in reality, then at least distinct in logic. In this way, one thing existing in reality will deserve a double name, because it performs the duty of both the striker and the struck: likewise, it may act as both the sounding part and the trapped part, and will be considered triple if it sustains the roles of all. In sound excited by wind or exhalation, the wind performs the role of the striker; the air performs the office of both the struck and the trapped. Hence, air has the role of the mover and the moved (or the squeezing) under different considerations. For, being overcome by the swift and vehement motion of the exhalation and wind—not unlike a storm arising at sea where waves overtaking each other in their course resist one another—so the air opposes the exhalation and becomes the cause of its own squeezing. The matter is the same in the other two differences of sound. But the sound produced by the tearing of cloth ascribes its whole action and its whole being to the air alone; for in the tearing of cloth, as the parts of the air rush together at the sides of the cloth very swiftly for fear of a vacuum <note>The "horror vacui" or "nature abhors a vacuum," a common belief in pre-modern physics.</note>, and the front parts are pushed and broken by the rear parts, the same air acts as the striker as it pushes from one side, and as the struck and trapped body as it is pushed and squeezed from the other side. Anyone who rightly weighs these things will be able to apply them to all other differences of sound; for when soft bodies, such as air, wind, and the like, emit a sound, it is certain that they do not sound unless they acquire a certain state of a solid body. It is clear, therefore, how all the said difficulties regarding the definition can be solved.

## CHAPTER II.
### On the Genesis or production of sound.

<margin>
Air is required for the collision of bodies.
</margin>
Since from the preceding chapter sound is nothing other than a successive passive quality produced from the trapping and squeezing out of air or water following the collision of sounding bodies; order now demands that we show how sound is produced from the clashing of bodies. And indeed, daily experience teaches that by the collision of two bodies, sound [is made]...

<summary>This page defines sound as a physical quality resulting from the compression of air or water between colliding bodies. It addresses potential contradictions, such as the sound of wind or tearing cloth, by explaining how air can act as both the striker and the struck object.</summary>
<keywords>definition of sound, Aristotle, Boethius, acoustics, physics of air, collision, vacuum, thunder, fluid dynamics</keywords>
