<meta>This page continues the philosophical and anatomical investigation into the nature of sound, specifically debating whether sound exists within the object making the noise or the medium (air) through which it travels.</meta>

# Book I. Anatomical Treatise on the Nature of Sound and Voice.

...as we said previously, sound does not reside in the object, but in the subject—or what is the same thing—not in the sounding bodies, but in the intermediate medium. For this medium, as was stated in the preceding sections, concurs in the <term>genesis of sound</term> <note>The physical creation or "birth" of sound</note> as its material cause. If sound resided only in the sounding bodies, the sound flowing from them would be minimally hindered by the wind, yet experience teaches that this is false. Again, if sound resided in the sounding bodies, one would either have to deny that sound results from the breaking and compression of the intermediate body <note>The air</note>, or assert that this broken and worn-down intermediate body reacts upon the sounding objects themselves and produces the sound within them. No philosopher of sound mind would admit either of these.

To respond to the arguments proposed at the beginning of this chapter, we say that it is peculiar and proper to sound not to cling to the object, as do other sensible qualities like smell, taste, and color. Those qualities have a fixed and stable existence and flow from the very composition of their natural objects; they are the signs and means by which we both know and discern the objects themselves. Sound, however, does not exist except while it is being made, and it does not stay with the substance of the sounding bodies. Therefore, where it is born—that is, in the intermediate body—there it is received as if in a subject (although it reveals its objects, the sounding bodies which are said to be heard, no differently than other sensible qualities). This, however, does not apply to other sensible qualities; for they only bring into the senses the appearance of the things in which they reside. For example: if color were in the medium of seeing <note>The air or light</note>, the appearance of the medium, not the object, would come into the sense. To this is added the fact that when taste is corrupted and infected by another quality hostile to its nature, things tasted are judged poorly.

As for the previous objection that the same thing cannot be both the medium and the subject at the same time, that is only to be understood regarding parts proceeding from one and the same thing. For in respect to different parts, it is not at all absurd for the same thing to be both medium and subject. Regarding the third objection above—that sound resides in sounding bodies because they are named "sonorous" from "sound"—this is not to be understood <term>subjectively</term> <note>meaning the sound is a property of the object's matter</note>, but <term>effectively</term> <note>meaning the object is the cause of the sound</note>. That is, bodies that emit sound are called "sounding" not because they are affected by sound, but because they are the things that effect the sound. For as has already been said, that intermediate thing which we establish as the passive subject of sound is nothing other than the air, which concurs in the genesis of sound not as the efficient cause, but as the material cause. Our proposition is therefore clear.

---

# C H A P T E R I V.

## Concerning the requirements of bodies concurring both effectively and subjectively in the genesis of sound.

**In order** that what has been said previously may be better illuminated here, let us now see what is required in bodies concurring for the perfection and genesis of sound. To proceed in order, we say first that the <term>sonative motion</term> <note>The specific movement that produces sound</note>—by which sound is birthed through the stroke of bodies striking one another—demands above all else, as if by its own right, that said bodies be endowed with the power of shattering the intermediate medium very swiftly. Indeed, only those bodies possess this faculty which are fit for attacking each other vehemently, because only these are able to compress the medium tightly, to propel it forcefully and rapidly once compressed, and to aptly break the impulse. Therefore, bodies of this kind are required first: those which possess the power of mutually resisting one another and of colliding with each other quickly. For by reason of strong resistance, one receives the stroke of the other powerfully—powerfully, I say, if the percussion itself is joined with strength. If you take this resistance away from bodies, they become unfit for the genesis of sound and acquire a certain impotence in the production of sound. For example, if two bodies otherwise fit for vehement collision are struck softly and gently or languidly—as if you strike a table or your other hand with your hand without any impetus, by way of touch or gentle application—no breaking occurs in the intermediate medium, and consequently, no sound will be caused.

<meta>The catchword "Secun-" indicates the next page begins with "Secundo" (Secondly).</meta>

<summary>The author argues that sound exists within the air (the medium) rather than the vibrating object itself, using the effect of wind as evidence. He then outlines the physical requirements for sound production, emphasizing the need for forceful collision and resistance to displace the air.</summary>

--- Page 36 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the physical requirements for sound production, specifically focusing on the speed of impact and the material properties of bodies. It follows the previous page's introduction to the necessity of resistance in sound.</meta>

# Of the Great Art of Consonance and Dissonance
<meta>Catchword: and so</meta>

Secondly, to produce sound, the speed of the blow must necessarily be joined to the resistance of the bodies. Since this swift motion causes a certain tremor in the bodies (as will be declared later), it is rightly the sole foundation of the entire diversity of sounds; for according to its increase or decrease, bodies sound now higher, now lower, as will be shown more extensively shortly. Moreover, speed depends on the force and velocity of the colliding body. Just as hardness grants bodies the power of resisting, and flatness adapts bodies for percussion, so swift motion makes the resistance more violent. Hence all soft bodies, such as sponges, wool, needles <note>likely referring to soft fibers or pins that do not strike with mass</note>, and the like, emit an imperceptible sound. Furthermore, just as hard, flat, and swiftly and forcefully moving things are required for sound simply to exist; so, not absolutely, but for the sake of a better quality of sound, the bodies to be sounded ought to be smooth, concave, porous, etc., and situated in a due and convenient manner. Smooth things, because of their even surface (by which they are fit to intercept and strike the air as one continuous whole), contribute to the force, smoothness, and clarity of the sound. Uneven and rough things, however, because of their protrusions and depressions—on account of which they do not crush the air as a whole or as one continuous mass, but break it into small pieces—emit an imperfect, rough, and diminished sound.

<margin>The cause of rough and smooth sound.</margin>
For just as sound is produced by the breaking of the medium <note>the air</note>, so, where the medium is not one, continuous, and equal, but is extended and diminished in parts and fragments, or through frequent inequalities, a rough sound arises. But where the whole is one continuous mass and collected together, not rebounding from them in fragments, a forceful, smooth, and clear sound arises: not unlike what happens with light, which produces a greater illumination the smoother the surface it falls upon. All these things are confirmed by various experiences; for when straw is scattered in an orchestra <note>the performance area of a theater</note>, there is no other cause for the voice of the choir being heard less clearly than the inequality of the floor. The same must be said of a hall decorated with various tapestries, in which, since the sound is as if suffocated, it is no wonder that the sound is much duller than if the hall were stripped of them. For the same reason, music in a church filled with people is perceived less clearly and distinctly than in an empty church.

<margin>Concave things intensify sound.</margin>
Furthermore, concave things, by their cavity, produce many blows through reflection after the first one; because the medium enclosed within, when it is shaken, is struck back against the concave sides, which contributes greatly to the extension and prolongation of the sound. Air also—that is, bodies participating in much air <note>porous materials</note>—has the greatest power for a clear, great, and perfect sound, since those things concur not only as the efficient cause, but also as the material for producing sound. Whence bronze and silver are more sonorous than lead, not by reason of solidity or softness, as some think, but for reasons to be opened in the following sections. Therefore, for sound, there are required strong resistance, recoil, and speed of blows, likewise hardness, smoothness, concavity of the body, and the continuity of its parts; which was to be proved, etc.

## CHAPTER V.
### On the various Species of sound.

<margin>Division of sounds.</margin>
Following the explanation of the required conditions of sounds are the various differences of sounds, which order demands we examine. Since, therefore, sound can be produced in various ways, various species necessarily emanate from the various combinations of the same. There is sound that is long, short, high-pitched, and low-pitched; another is direct, and another reflected. One kind of sound exists in act <note>term: *in actu* - currently sounding</note>, another in potential <note>term: *in potentia* - the capacity of a material to sound</note>; the formal and inherent subject of the former is air, water, fire; of the latter, iron, bronze, silver, gold, stones, wood, and other hard and smooth bodies. Furthermore, if we consider the manner of proceeding of sounds, one sound is from the breaking of the air caused by the collision of solid bodies; another from dashing against; as when air moved by a violent wind is dashed against a solid body; thus the lung dashes the air against the hard parts of the windpipe <note>original: *Tracheæ*</note> in breathing. Another is born from coming together, as happens in the tearing of cloth or paper; for lest a vacuum be created, the parts of the air very swiftly come together at the sides of the cloth, and there the first parts, pushed by those behind, are broken.

It also begins a classification of sounds based on their duration, pitch, and the materials (like bronze or air) that produce them.</summary>

Book I. Anatomical Treatise on the Nature of Sound and Voice.

...and they produce. Certain sounds are produced by the extension of air, as in hissing; others by the constriction of air, as in pipes; others are found in caverns and other openings into which the wind drives and constricts the air; some arise from sounding bodies, of which some are natural and others violent. We call those "natural" which are formed by bodies colliding by an inherent principle, and these are again of two kinds: namely, those of animate bodies and those of inanimate bodies. The latter are produced by the movement of winds, air, and similar things; the former, however, are produced by the power of the moving soul. These again differ according to the nature of the organs that draw them forth. But time would fail me if I wished to recount every single difference of sounds here, since there are as many diverse species of sound as there are diverse species of colliding things among inanimate objects; <margin>The variety of sounds is infinite.</margin> and as many as there are diverse organs found in living creatures from which they can be drawn forth. For just as in men the variety of face, gait, and inclinations is almost infinite, so also the diversity of voices arising from the condition of bodies and the constitution of organs is utterly infinite. But these matters will be treated at length throughout this entire work; therefore, let us move to other things.

## CHAPTER VI.
### Concerning the proximate and principal cause of sound.

Since sound arises from no other source than from the trembling of colliding bodies, as has been said, it is necessary that innumerable differences of sounds are also born according to the varying condition of the tremblings. We shall, therefore, in this chapter open the door to the hidden power of sounds and to the understanding of utterly paradoxical operations with no difficulty; wherefore I advise that you apply yourself with the greatest zeal to understanding it. But to the matter at hand.

Just as, therefore—as we said in the <term>Art of Light and Shadow</term> <note>original: "Ars Magna Lucis et Umbrae," Kircher's famous work on optics and light</note>—the air is filled with infinite images of things radiating from objects through a medium, so also is it filled with infinite species of sounds. However, only those present themselves to the hearing which are carried in a manner proportioned to the auditory power by a sensible motion. Furthermore, just as the nature of things is engaged in perpetual motion, so all things are agitated by perpetual motion. From this perpetual agitation of things <margin>Species of sounds emulate visible species.</margin> arises the collision of bodies; finally, from the collision, according to the condition of the sounding bodies, infinite varieties of sounds come forth. I do not speak here, however, only of sounds that always fall under the senses, but of those which would be perceived if the hearing were strengthened either by divine power or by an <term>acoustic instrument</term> <note>A device for amplifying sound, such as a primitive hearing aid or megaphone</note> (concerning the construction of which I have written at length in our <term>Magic of Consonance and Dissonance</term> <note>original: "Musurgia Universalis," specifically the section on "Magia Phonotactica"</note>).

I say, therefore, first: it is the condition of bodies sounding from collision that, while they sound when an impulse is made, they tremble; and I assert that from the trembling of the air a "fraction" <note>A breaking or displacement of the air</note> occurs, and from this, finally, sound is born. And without this trembling (which is itself a certain species of impulse) <margin>Sound is nothing other than trembling.</margin> it is impossible for sound to be made. Let no one persuade himself, however, that this trembling is always sensible <note>i.e., perceptible to the touch or sight</note>, as I said; for since this consists of infinite <term>diadromas</term> <note>original: "diadromis," a term used for the oscillations or "crossings" of a vibrating string</note>, it deservedly far surpasses all the sharpness of the senses. Only experience in certain bodies, in which this trembling falls under the senses, has led us into this knowledge.

Moreover, this trembling is a certain species of local motion, or as I said, an impulse or impetus, by which a certain collided body trembles entirely within itself, and is shaken by certain minute undulations and successions. Every body is capable of this trembling, whether it be solid or liquid, homogeneous or heterogeneous, hard or soft, rough or smooth; indeed, not only does the body itself tremble, but it likewise makes the air (or the intervening medium) tremble; so that the air also trembles according to the undulations, successions, or tremblings of the collided body. Hence, the more fit things are for trembling, the more fit they are for producing sound, and the more "sonorous" they are. However, the more difficulty they have in trembling, the duller the sound they produce will be. All of which visible and perceived experience teaches us: for if anyone touches a stretched string even lightly, he will immediately see it tremble, animated into sound. Thus, the trembling which the eyes perceive, the hand also feels; in the same way, a bell struck even very lightly by a needle trembles all over, striking the neighboring air with continuous undulations: whence also the ringing lasts for so long...

<summary>Kircher argues that sound is fundamentally caused by the vibration (tremor) of bodies, comparing the way sound fills the air to the way light and images travel. He asserts that even when we cannot see or feel it, all sound requires this mechanical oscillation of both the object and the surrounding air.</summary>

# FIRST COROLLARY.

## Concerning the marvelous Deaf Stone in Scotland.

**H**ector Boethius <note>A Scottish philosopher and historian (1465–1536) known for his "History of the Scottish People."</note> reports that in Scotland, in a province named Pifa <note>Likely referring to Fife.</note>, there is a certain rock called "deaf" by the natives, because (no matter how great a sound is raised, even if a cannon is fired) those hiding beneath it perceive nothing except a violent agitation of the air. The reason for this can be nothing other than the excessive solidity of that rock, so fashioned by nature that, due to the perfect joining together of its individual parts, it blocks every entrance to the external air necessary for producing sound.

For the same thing happens with sound as happens with vision through a transparent medium: the purer, clearer, and more refined it is, the further it carries visible images; but the more vaporous it is, the less fit it is for seeing. But let us clarify the matter with an experiment.

### An experiment beautifully clarifying the difficulty of the matter.

<margin>Experience.</margin> **F**irst, if someone takes a sheet of Selenite—commonly called Talc—as thin as can possibly be made, and looks through it, he will see everything as clearly as if nothing were placed between the eye and the object. Now let the sheet be doubled, and you will see everything clearly indeed, but not as before; then let the sheet be tripled, and now objects will appear a little more obscurely; and thus objects will always be represented more obscurely the more you multiply said sheets, until they finally turn into a porous, opaque, <unclear>dense</unclear>, and impenetrable body to the sight. For by the multiplication of the sheets now degenerating into an opaque body, a barrier is placed against the sight so that it can no longer reach the object; and yet it is certain that in that whole opaque body, plenty of air lies hidden for carrying visible images. In a not dissimilar way, if a bronze plate—or one made of any material most suitable for producing sound—were given and set into a wall, it is certain that from the opposite side, sound would be perceived as easily as if nothing were interposed. Now let the said plate be multiplied, applied precisely to the first; it is certain that the sound will not emanate so clearly from the opposite side. Triple the plate, and now you will hear somewhat more dully, and thus, according to the plurality of plates applied precisely to one another, you will hear more and more faintly, until the plates reach such an increase that you can perceive nothing at all, just as was said regarding vision. For although air is more abundant between the plates, because it is incapable of receiving images due to the multiple discontinuity, and cannot vibrate, it is therefore as if absorbed and dissipated in the medium; failing entirely in the transport of sound, it can by no means present itself to the power of hearing on the opposite side.

Furthermore, this happens in all thicker masses; for even if these have internal air—hidden in individual, tiny, and entirely imperceptible pockets of the whole substance to prevent a vacuum—nevertheless, because the impact is violently blunted by the encounter with multiple surfaces, it follows that the continuous vibration of the parts is gradually dissipated and absorbed in the thick medium; it can in no way be communicated to the deeper parts of the mass. Instead, weakened in the medium, it as if expires; just as the ripples caused in ponds by throwing in a stone: the further they are from the center of motion, the more imperceptible they always [become]...

they vanish until they pass into nothingness. These similarities, expressed through a twofold experiment, demonstrate quite beautifully the progression of sound through solid bodies. For the same reason, a voice carried through the medium of the air eventually perishes in a "uniformly-difform" <note>A scholastic term meaning a rate of change that is itself changing at a constant rate; here, a gradual fading.</note> manner, not so much from its own nature as from the multiple impediments of the accumulated intervening air, and as if by certain obstacles resisting the sonorous motion. By these, the sound is weakened little by little and by degrees, until it finally fails altogether; just as we have said regarding the medium of sight. Thus, objects far remote appear blue—a color very close to black—for which there is no other reason than the aforementioned thickening of the atmospheric layers. When the visual species <note>The "images" or "forms" that 17th-century science believed traveled from an object to the eye.</note> cannot penetrate these layers, they degenerate into a shadowy color similar to black. For what white and black are in colors, the acute-violent and the obtuse (or deep at the lowest degree) are in sound. ## SECOND COROLLARY.
### *Experimental.*

**A**nd from this discourse—perhaps longer than was proper—it is sufficiently clear by what means sound can both arise within water and, having arisen outside, be perceived within. For since water is like a thick kind of air (indeed, it is air in potential, as we taught in *The Magnetic Art*, Book 3, on the magnetism of the elements), when two bodies collide violently within it, the sound is carried through the trembling medium just as sight is carried through foggy air, and in this way presents itself to the ears. For immediately after the collision is performed, a wave-motion is perceived in the nearest water; after this, a second and a third are noted; and thus the undulations or tremors of the water, the carriers of sound, are continued all the way to the surface, where they appear sensibly; and from there they are finally carried through the air to the ears.
<margin>Experiment of sonorous undulation in liquid.</margin>
Multiple experiences have taught me that this is so, especially regarding the Maltese divers. These men, in order to hunt for "date-shells" <note>original: "dactylos"; a type of lithodome or boring mollusk.</note> (this is a type of shellfish that hides within the very substance of rocks under the water and cannot be obtained unless the stones are split), would lower themselves beneath the water. I observed, therefore, as a present and most curious spectator, that as soon as they struck the submarine rock, behold, at every stroke the water on the surface would ripple perfectly, just as if it were moved by a light wind. After the agitation of the water, an obtuse sound would be perceived, and this occurred after every stroke—which is a most manifest sign of the undulations that are born from the blows and continued through the medium of the water to the surface. I also arranged for them to attempt similar blows in the deepest parts, but I could note no ripples on the surface of the water. Therefore, I decided the matter should be investigated more carefully; I ordered that they produce the sound with equal violence first in the deep, then in the middle, and finally in the surface area near the submarine space. And behold, what I had imagined I finally found to be true: in the deep I observed hardly any rippling, in the middle it was very notable, and near the end it was greatest. From this experiment, I finally concluded that undulations in water behave in exactly the same way as sounds carried through any thick mass; and the collision must be violent for the sound to be perceived outside of it.
<margin>Why the movement of fish in water is not perceived.</margin>
From this it is clear why the movement of fish in water—which undoubtedly produces some sound—is not perceived: namely, because a slighter collision causes weak undulations which expire before they emerge to the surface. From this experiment, I also recognized the causes of why the Strait of Sicily <note>The Strait of Messina, famous for its treacherous currents (Scylla and Charybdis).</note> is perpetually rippled even in the clearest and calmest weather, as if agitated by perpetual winds.
<margin>Why the Strait of Sicily is perpetually rippled.</margin>
For it suffers violent tides and has entirely contrary currents, which are carried with such force that they move even the bottom of the sea, as I can be an "ear-witness"; for while I was exploring the said strait in the year 1638, I more than once distinctly perceived the crash of flints being driven by the tide at the bottom. From their violent collision, it is necessary that undulations also arise, and since these reach all the way to the surface, it is no wonder that it is subject to so many ripples, which are greatly promoted by the violent collisions and struggles of the contrary waves. It is clear, therefore, from what has been said...

...to supply the needs of struggling nature, or even to penetrate the very pores of the glass. There are those who think that air enters not through the pores, but between the sides of the tube and the mercury through the outermost surface itself; they say that the mercury does not sit so tightly against the sides of the tube that some air cannot always substitute itself in the place of the receding mercury, entering from the outside around the cylindrical boundaries of the tube and the mercury, especially in a time of "struggling nature" <note>A reference to the Aristotelian concept of *horror vacui*, or nature's "abhorrence" of a vacuum.</note>. Certain others, philosophizing more freely, think that the mercury is held within the tube at a certain height by some unknown external balancing force. Finally, some think that the parts of the tube abandoned by the quicksilver <note>mercury</note> are filled with a thin substance or spirit exhaling from the mercury itself; they argue that while the weight of the adhering mercury cannot bear further distension, and since no other body can succeed it, the mercury is violently sustained within the tube at such a measure and proportion, and consequently at such an elevation. And these are the opinions explaining the reason for that space which certain Moderns <note>the "Neoterici," likely referring to followers of Torricelli or Pascal</note> asserted to be a pure vacuum; of which opinions the last rightly holds the first place among the probable ones.

<margin>A beautiful experiment against the existence of a vacuum</margin>
For it is certain that a vacuum can by no means exist there, since a most manifest sound is perceived within it. This is something I tested here in Rome several years ago together with Gaspare Berti, a most ingenious mathematician. He ordered a lead pipe one hundred feet long and as thick as a finger to be prepared, upon the upper end of which he placed a glass flask of remarkable thickness, specially blown for this purpose and joined to the neck of the tube with such industry and fortified with such skill that every entrance for air was blocked. Inside the flask, at my suggestion, he inserted a small bell together with a hammer with such dexterity that the iron hammer, attracted and raised by a magnet from the outside, would—once freed from the magnet—strike the bell by its own weight and produce a sound.

When everything required for the experiment was prepared, he filled the entire tube and flask with water, and having closed the orifice at the other end with a stopper, he immersed it in a vat full of water. This done, when the stopper was opened, the water descending within the lead tube did not empty itself entirely, but stopped at about ten feet within the tube, just as was said a little while ago regarding the quicksilver enclosed in a glass tube. From this, many spectators of this most admirable experiment attempted to infer that the space vacated by the water was necessarily a vacuum, since no other body could have been substituted there. We, however, in order to demonstrate the falsity of their opinion by the experience of the ear itself, applied a magnet we had grabbed to the outside of the glass flask opposite the iron hammer; it soon raised the attracted hammer, and when the magnet was pulled away, the hammer, struck by its own weight against the bell, produced a most clear sound. From this, certain more stubborn-headed people present immediately inferred that sound could be produced in a vacuum; but the more sensible majority learned from this so manifest an experiment that a vacuum can by no means exist where such manifest signs of air are exhibited in sound. And by this single experiment, all were convinced and abjured the opinion of the vacuum for the future. We wished to place a figure here for the better information of the curious reader. The lead tube was D E F G; the glass flask joined to the tube D E; inside which was the bell C attached by an iron wire, and the hammer O; the stopper G; the vat full of water M I K L; the magnet applied from the outside A. The rest was done as described just before.

<margin>How air enters an inaccessible place.</margin>
<margin>Nature is ingenious in necessity.</margin>
The nullity of the vacuum is thus most clearly evident from what has been said. But by what means, or through what hidden paths the air substitutes itself in the place of the receding water—if anyone should ask me, I shall satisfy him as well, provided he first explains to me by what means the magnet penetrates glass and other hardest bodies, or how light penetrates the most solid crystal. For the paths of struggling nature are so secret, so hidden, that they can by no means be comprehended by human ingenuity. Nature is so ingenious in a time of necessity that she seems to elude every scrutiny of the human intellect; yet experience makes it certain that air entered there, though how and from where remains hidden. Thus, those who philosophize foolishly, while they conceive of a thing not at all, pronounce rather thoughtlessly on the nature of things.

<margin>Sound can by no means occur in a vacuum.</margin>
We conclude, therefore, that even if a vacuum were possible in the nature of things, sound could by no means occur in it. For since sound is an affection of the air—indeed, air is the material cause of sound—if air is lacking, sound must also necessarily be lacking; and on the contrary, from the proposed ex-

## CHAPTER VII.
### On the organ of hearing and its Anatomy.

<margin>The wondrous architecture of the ear.</margin>
After we have discussed the nature, property, and production of sound—the object, as it were, adequate to the power of hearing—perhaps more at length than was proper, the order of nature now of its own accord invites us to a description of the acoustic or auditory power. Since, however, a power cannot exist without an organ, we shall begin the material of our discourse from that. Whoever has more deeply scrutinized that admirable providence of God, the Best and Greatest, in the fabric of the organs of the human body, and the wondrous skill of His art (that is, nature) in arranging them, will be compelled to confess that no fate, no fortuitous clash of atoms could have been the architect of such great things. Furthermore, omitting the organs of the remaining senses, we shall briefly disclose here those things which occur as worthy of consideration specifically regarding the ear and the organ of hearing. Now, the ear is a bodily organ disposed by nature in living creatures for receiving sounds; since it consists of various parts, it can not incongruously be defined as *The aggregate of all those things which, in any living being, are dedicated to the sense of hearing.* This organ is conspicuous and manifest in certain animals; in some, this auditory power lies hidden, and is secret and so difficult to find that even to this day it remains concealed. For <margin>It is doubtful whether fish have ears.</margin> as for fish (which lack the lungs by which other animals draw in and return breath and air), it has not yet been fully explored by anyone through which part they hear. For whether the holes placed before the eyes were given for hearing or for smelling is still debated. Nevertheless, it is certain that in some of these, ears are entirely lacking. For those that lack eyes also lack ears, such as mussels, oysters, and all those covered with a hard shell; although these contract when they are sought with iron hooks, they seem to feel this by the agitation of the water rather than to hear by audition. But in fish that breathe and have lungs, such as whales, dolphins, and seals, the ears are not hidden, although in them the exterior passage is difficult to investigate. Therefore, leaving these, let us return to the organs of terrestrial animals. For just as fish hear dully within water—as if within a thicker medium that retards the species <note>In this context, "species" refers to the sensory images or forms of sound traveling through a medium.</note> of sound—so too they do not have an organ of hearing as exquisite as the rest of the terrestrial animals that live in the medium of the air.

<margin>Description of the ear.</margin>
The ear of a living body, therefore, is not one single and simple part; nor is it one single substance formed from many things mixed and confused together; but it is a certain aggregate of many greatly diverse and dissimilar parts, distinct from one another and worthy of the highest admiration. For this organ, prominently projecting outside on the head, arranged in a wondrous order with ducts and passages (which are like certain paths for sounds), excavated into little caverns for various uses, formed with little bones as if with certain supports, stretched with nerves and muscles, and finally animated by implanted air, represents an economy perfect in every respect. However, not all these things concur in the same number, nor do they all concur to constitute the ear of every living creature. For in some there are three little bones <note>The ossicles: malleus, incus, and stapes.</note>, in some two, in others one; nor are there lacking those in which none are found, which we have recognized as true regarding the muscles. But I return to the ear, which we establish as twofold: internal and external, or what we commonly call the auricle. The former has its site in the stony bone <margin>Stony bone. Mastoid.</margin> <note>The petrous part of the temporal bone.</note> between those processes which protrude like breasts, hence called *mastoid*, and between that apophysis <note>A natural outgrowth or projection on a bone.</note> which constitutes a portion of the yoke-bone <note>The zygomatic bone.</note>; the latter [projects] significantly on both sides from the sides of the head, [like] wings...

But having seen the anatomy of the individual parts of the acoustic organ, nothing remains except that we now explain the duties of each one.

# CHAPTER VIII.
## On the duties of the individual parts of the acoustic organ.

<margin>The wondrous structure of the ears.</margin>
Just as that admirable majesty of Nature is evident in all things, so it is most clearly seen and most worthy of admiration in the structure of the ears. So that the curious Reader may understand it, we shall take the beginning of our dissertation from its duties and uses. It is established, therefore, that the ear is not constituted of one simple and uniform part, nor of many parts mixed or confused together; but is aggregated from many parts existing separately and distinctly, so that it might be most fit for performing its function. For since the perception of sound had to be entrusted to a nerve, its substance did not permit it to be extended outside the head because of the many dangers the organ could encounter; therefore, an internal medium had to be placed between the nerve and the air, by means of which sound could be conveyed to the nerve. Furthermore, lest air—now cold, now hot, now affected by some other harmful quality—should enter and bring harm to the internal medium and the nerve, it had to have cavities; these are closed to the external air, yet open to the "species" <note>In medieval/Renaissance science, "species" refers to the sensible form or image of a thing (like sound or light) traveling through a medium</note> of sound. For the sake of this, the little bones, muscles, and ligaments had to be created.

<margin>The utility and purpose of the individual parts of the hearing organ.</margin>
Furthermore, if we look at the location, Nature could not have assigned a more convenient or opportune place for the ear; while it is near the mouth—the gateway for food and drink—and the maker of speech, it has been allotted a place of equal dignity. Truly, the ear is doubled, lest if one were corrupted, the entire sense should perish. It is open, so that it might always be ready, and so that we might be awakened by necessity even in sleep. It is shaped in the manner of a snail shell <note>original: "cochlea"</note>, so that the species of sound, united therein, might present themselves more strongly to the sense, being smoothed and multiplied through the various reflections of the caverns. For this same reason, Nature expanded that shell-like winnowing fan <note>referring to the outer ear or pinna</note> on the outside, so that the species of sound, gathered in this way, might strengthen the hearing. Hence, those who are hard of hearing, in order to hear better, curve their hand spread over their ears; for the sound, collected and multiplied there, flows more strongly into the organ; yet, lest a more vehement sound should bring some harm to the organ,

<meta>catchword: natu- (nature)</meta>

first, as is evident from anatomy <note>original: "anatome"</note>, it is diverse. Nature has disposed all these things according to the state of the condition and nature of each living creature, so that harmony might appear more evidently in all things.

### CHAPTER IX.

## On the nature of the internal air, and where exactly hearing occurs; whether it is propagated through a real or a notional being.

<margin>
What is the internal air?
</margin>

In the cavities of the stony bone <note>The petrous part of the temporal bone, which houses the inner ear.</note>, no one can deny that air is enclosed, unless perhaps he is one who admits a vacuum in the nature of things; only this doubt could be raised: How is it present? And is it distinguished from the external air? What is its function? And how is it preserved? However, to quickly free myself from these questions: I say that this air was implanted in the ears themselves from the very beginning; whence the Philosophers call it "congenital," "implanted," and "built-in." It is no less a medium for producing sound than the external air; it is most pure, devoid of all sound, quiet and immobile, and finally, it is abundant and copious. Furthermore, we assert that this is not the same as the external air. For just as the air drawn in from the outside through respiration is first altered and prepared by nature in the lungs—lest by its coldness it should too greatly affect the heart—and afterwards, mixed with the vapor of the blood, becomes the material for the generation of the vital spirits; so the air drawn in by respiration through the nostrils and penetrating the brain does not become the material for the regeneration of the animal spirits <note>In historical physiology, "animal spirits" were thought to be the fluid or gas responsible for sensation and movement, traveling through the nerves.</note> until it has been altered and well-prepared, so that it no longer possesses the nature of external air. Moreover, the reasoning regarding the air drawn in for generating the vital and animal spirits seems to me no different than that regarding the air which

<margin>
Implanted air is the same as the animal spirit.
</margin>

is implanted in the ears; whence we assert it to be of the same nature as the animal spirit (which is established to be of an airy nature), and therefore it was called by the name of "air" by most of the more perceptive Philosophers. Indeed, it has a most thin and transparent substance in the manner of air, and it agrees with the external air in its very temperament; for it is not as hot as the vital spirit, which has been made colder by the substance of the brain and the surrounding air drawn in through the nostrils; nor is it as dry as the vital spirit, because the moisture of the brain has tempered it. Therefore, being warm and moist and well-tempered, it can by the best right be called by the name of "air." Nor is it one in number <note>i.e., it is not a single, permanent body of air.</note>, but is successively capable of being generated and corrupted; for since it is of the same nature as the animal spirit, just as the latter is daily dissipated and dissolved into the breezes and new spirit is generated, so also is the condition of the internal air. Thus, it shares the same origin as the vital and animal spirits contained in the arteries, cavities, and ventricles of the brain. And this internal air is the medium in which hearing occurs, with the acoustic nerve as well as the three small bones <note>The ossicles: malleus, incus, and stapes.</note> effectively concurring. But so that these things may be better explained: I suppose that they concur for hearing in a twofold way, either as an efficient cause or as a subject.

For the faculty not only draws out the act of hearing from the "species" <note>In Scholastic philosophy, a "species" is the form or image of an object that travels to the sense organ.</note> impressed upon it, but it also receives the elicited act into itself. Therefore, just as the bringing forth of the act of hearing (which the faculty informed by sound draws out) is considered active, so insofar as it receives sounds from the object, it is considered passive. It brings forth the act of hearing from the species received by it, because it is not fit to act upon the sound or its species unless the sound or its species has first acted upon it and excited it, so that once excited, it may react. Furthermore, since a physical action and passion <note>In this context, "passion" means the state of being acted upon.</note> do not occur if the agent and the patient are distant from one another, sound must necessarily be produced in that part of the air which is not so distant that they cannot be mutually transferred and touch each other; for we do not perceive sounds from just anywhere or at any distance, but those excited in nearby and not overly remote places. It is necessary, therefore, for the sound to extend itself all the way to the ears; since the ears emit no power outside themselves to meet the sound or which the sounds might receive.

<margin>
How is sound propagated: through a real being or an intentional being?
</margin>

But with these things thus established, let us now see how sound is propagated: whether through a real being <note>Physical matter.</note>, or through a notional or intentional being <note>A mental or formal representation.</note>, or through its own species? I find a threefold opinion among Authors concerning this business: Some say that sounds are propagated only according to their real being,

<summary>This page discusses the "internal air" of the ear, identifying it as a refined substance similar to the "animal spirits" of the brain, which serves as the essential medium for the perception of sound. The author explores whether sound reaches the ear as a physical reality or a philosophical "species" (image).</summary>

# Of the Great Art of Consonance and Dissonance
## Page 20

<margin>Epicurus said the voice was a flow of atoms.</margin>
It is a wonder how great a dissonance exists among the most confused Authors in assigning the genuine causes of the voice. Epicurus maintained that the voice is a flow of atoms which we perceive coming out of the mouth while we are speaking; for he says this flow is emitted either by those calling out, sounding, or making a noise, which, when it encounters similar shapes, is broken so that when it falls upon the ears, the sense of hearing then arises. Following this same opinion, Democritus defines the voice as a splitting of atoms into infinite parts, which he believes results in hearing and sensation as they are rolled through certain circles to the ears; some modern <note>original: "Neoterici"</note> petty philosophers follow these men, so much so that no opinion is so absurd that it does not find applause among eccentric minds. The Stoics contend that the voice is produced by air driven into a fluctuating circle; others devise other theories.

<margin>Definition of the voice.</margin>
But leaving to each his own opinion, we judge that the voice should be defined thus: *The voice is an animal sound produced by the glottis from the striking of breathed air to explain the affections of the mind.* By these words, we explain the nature and essence of the voice so that in it, all the causes pertaining to the constitution of the voice are revealed as if in an epitome; we establish sound as the formal cause of the voice, air as the material cause, the larynx as the efficient cause, and finally, we determine the final cause to be that intention by which we wish to signify something through the voice.

<margin>Causes of the voice.</margin>
Sound, therefore, rightly holds the place of the "genus" in the definition of the voice, and since the voice is a certain species of sound, we have rightly assigned air as its material cause, by the breaking of which it is produced. However, air is not the matter of the voice in just any state, but as it is agitated and expelled outward from the lungs. The remote efficient cause of the voice is the body of a living creature; I say "living creature" because even if the sound of inanimate things is occasionally called a "voice," it is only to be understood metaphorically. For the sound of musical instruments—now intense, now relaxed, now deep, now shrill, and now the distinction of certain intervals like syllables into voices—is transferred to the "true voice" through a certain analogy. But the proximate efficient cause and the most principal instrument of the voice is the Larynx, and especially that part of the larynx which is called the <term>glottis</term> <note>original Greek: γλωττίς</note> or <term>epiglottis</term> <note>original Greek: ἐπιγλωττίς</note>; for this, being moderately covered and joined by certain muscles, emits the voice, as will be clear from the anatomy to be presented shortly.

<margin>The cause of the voice.</margin>
Therefore, the lung is not the cause of the voice, since the air which it blows out has far too little collision to form the voice, but it only supplies the air like a bellows. Neither the tongue, nor the throat, nor finally the lips, teeth, or palate are properly the organ of the voice, since the office of these parts in man is to pronounce the voice—already generated by the glottis—articulately and according to the legitimate sounds of syllables. Indeed, certain letters cannot be formed except by the lips, such as B, M, P, so that it is impossible for one deprived of lips to pronounce them; some only by the tongue, like D, T, L, N; some only by the teeth, like Sch, Z, S, R; some only by the throat, like A, H, Ai; and some only by the palate, like K, X, I, G.

<margin>The glottis is the chief cause of the voice.</margin>
You see, therefore, how the principal cause of the sound is the glottis, while the remaining parts of the mouth are nothing but instruments for the better and more proper forming and pronouncing of the voice. The tongue, however, like a certain <term>Choragus</term> <note>The leader or director of a choir in ancient Greek drama.</note>, moderates and disposes everything leading to the perfection of the voice. How the voice is formed through the glottis of the larynx will be discussed later.

<margin>The final cause of the voice.</margin>
Finally, the final cause is the intention to signify something; for the voice seems to have been instituted by nature in living creatures for no other end than to indicate the passions and affections of the soul. In man, it is ordained for the declaration of the will; in brutes, for the explanation of natural affections.
<margin>The voice of a cat.</margin>
For example, a Cat has one voice when it is hungry, another when it is incited by jealousy, another when it fawns, and another when it blazes out in anger; and so it is with each of the other living creatures, as was pointed out in the preceding pages.

---

## COROLLARY

<margin>Whether any sound can be called a voice.</margin>
From what has been said, it is clear that not every sound emitted by an animal is a voice, but only that which is made by the glottis with the intention of signifying something. Hence the chirping of certain insects, such as Cicadas and crickets, the humming of bees and wasps, and the hissing of serpents...

<summary>This page defines the "voice" as a specific type of animal sound produced by the glottis for the purpose of communication, distinguishing it from general noise or inanimate sounds. It details the physiological roles of the larynx, lungs, and mouth parts in speech production.</summary>

<page-num>21</page-num>
-> Book I. Anatomical Treatise on the Nature of Sound and Voice. <-

...should not properly be called voices, but rather a sound caused by the striking of wings, or the tongue, or some other part, as will be said later in the anatomy of certain animals. Fish also, just as they lack the instruments for breathing and forming a voice, are likewise unable to emit voices by any means; and if indeed a sound is perceived as being raised by larger fish, it is nevertheless not by the help of instruments destined for forming a voice: but is produced by the dashing of air and water within the various and winding caverns of the beast's head. Furthermore, we reject from our definition the speech of those possessed by spirits or "belly-speakers" <note>original: γαστρομύθων (gastromythōn); refers to ventriloquists, literally "belly-myth-tellers"</note> produced from the cavity of the belly. Just as I remember observing in certain market-peddlers, who spoke everywhere with their mouths and lips closed for the sake of lamentation.

### CHAPTER XI.
## Concerning the organ of the voice and its anatomy.

Since we have said above that the Larynx is the organ of the voice, and all kinds of voice depend upon this organ, we must explain what its nature and structure are, and of how many parts it consists, so that the principles of the genuine voice may become better known to the curious Reader. The Larynx, therefore—the head or the final part of the rough artery or Trachea <note>The "rough artery" (arteria aspera) is the historical term for the windpipe</note>—is nothing other than an organ consisting of cartilages producing the glottis and membranes, endowed with muscles and nerves, constructed primarily and properly for the sake of emitting the voice. It is, moreover, a hard body, dense and thick, large and ample, consisting of length, breadth, and depth, with a certain protuberance standing out especially from the necks of men, being also round and circular, yet with a certain slant. For in the anterior part it is exactly circular, but in the posterior part which faces the Esophagus, it gradually falls away from the perfect figure of a circle and attains some length. Within the body of this is a certain opening, which is called its fissure or glottis, the elegance of which is no less worthy of all praise than its utility. For the action of the larynx is entrusted chiefly to this part, and by it the voice (the proper action of this organ) is formed, namely when the passing air, being compressed and struck, produces the voice in a truly wonderful manner by the help of 13 muscles: But since these things can be perceived by no means better than from its anatomy, we subjoin it here.

Therefore, in *Figure VIII of Scheme II*:

<margin>The constitution of the glottis</margin>
You see, therefore, that two parts especially worthy of the greatest consideration occur in the Larynx: the glottis and the epiglottis. The glottis is the membranous, fatty, and glandular substance of the Larynx, composed of a peculiar flesh and parenchyma <note>functional tissue</note>, having at its end a slit resembling the reed of a flute <note>original: "fistulæ"</note>, as marked by the letter M. For just as the Retina is the seat of vision, and the parenchyma of the liver is the maker of blood, so we establish this membranous, fatty, and glandular body—anointed with a viscous and oily moisture—as the proper and genuine organ for producing the voice.

<margin>What is the epiglottis?</margin>
The epiglottis, however, is a certain part of the glottis which, acting as a lid, stands accurately above it to prevent any food or drink from flowing down through the glottis into the larynx; for this reason, it is called the door, the guardian, and the bulwark of the larynx. By its opening, we both breathe in and breathe out, and it has its own muscles assigned to it by nature, by which it is now closed and now opened; but these matters will be discussed more fully in what follows.

# CHAPTER XXII.

->On the various differences of voices which are expressed by means of the Larynx.<-

<margin>Difference of voices from the constitution of the larynx.</margin>
We have seen in the preceding chapter the structure of the organ of the voice; now let us see how it forms such a great variety of sounds. For it is certain that from the various constitutions of this organ, various differences in voices are born. This organ produces voices that are large, small, high-pitched, deep, smooth, harsh, steady, trembling, strong, weak, thick, subtle, clear, hoarse, cheerful, and mournful. Furthermore, there is one voice for the sober, another for the healthy, and another for the insane; in a word, as many differences of voices can be conceived as there are colors. To give the reasons for each of these would be a work of almost infinite labor; we shall assign the causes of only some of them here, treating first of the human voice, and then also of the voice of brute animals.

<margin>The triple cause of the difference of voices.</margin>
The differences of voices can therefore be reduced to three causes: either to the natural constitution of the larynx, or to the air which is the material and subject of the voice, or to the act of exhaling. Those things pertaining to the larynx are either from its temperament <note>the balance of bodily humors</note> and the things following that temperament, or from accidental qualities.

<margin>The quality of the Larynx.</margin>
The temperament of the Larynx, as with all its other parts, is either moist, hot, cold, or dry, or predominating from these mixed together <term>well-balanced <note>original Greek: "ευκράτως"</note></term> or <term>ill-balanced <note>original Greek: "δυσκράτως"</note></term>.

<margin>Whence a dark and obscure voice arises.</margin>
A moist temperament of the Larynx without an overflow of humors produces a dark, obscure, and confused voice; if it is accompanied by an overflow of humors, it will produce a hoarse voice.

<margin>Hot or cold temperament of the Larynx.</margin>
A dry temperament of the Larynx produces a melodious, clear, and resonant voice; and if dryness predominates, it produces a ringing, shrill, clanging voice, not unlike the cries of Cranes. A hot or cold temperament produces no differences of the voice by itself, but only by accident, insofar as heat either dries out or dilates the Larynx, while cold con-

<meta>catchword: con- (likely "constringit" or "contracts")</meta>

<meta>This page continues the anatomical discussion of the voice, specifically focusing on how the physical structure of the larynx and the quality of air produce different vocal characteristics.</meta>

<header>Book 1. Anatomical Treatise on the Nature of Sound and Voice.</header>

[The larynx] constricts and narrows. A well-balanced <note>original: "$\varepsilon\upsilon\kappa\rho\alpha\tau\text{ον}$" (eukraton) - a Greek term meaning well-tempered or perfectly blended</note> or moderate temperament of the larynx produces a voice that is sonorous, sweet, pleasant, charming, clear, transparent, and calm; and this pertains to the temperament of the larynx. Furthermore, the natural constitution of the larynx—which consists of its shape, size, position, passage, surface, etc.—gives birth to other differences in voices.

<margin>
What the shape of the larynx can do regarding vocal differences.
</margin>
An internal shape of the larynx that is round in its length makes the voice even, sonorous, and undistorted. The passage produces various voices, not by itself, but in respect to its size; for if this is wide and ample, the voice is rendered deep and large; if, on the contrary, it is narrow, the voice is thin and small; as is evident in pipes and flutes, and will be demonstrated at length in the section on musical instruments. The surface is either polished and smooth, or rough and uneven: from roughness, a similarly rough and unpleasant voice will emanate; from smoothness, a smooth, pure, sweet, and charming one. The position or site of the larynx emits a voice that is either diminished or distorted, or removes it entirely.

<margin>
What air can do regarding vocal differences.
</margin>
Air also, being the material cause of the voice, produces its own vocal differences; for it is either thin or thick. The thickness of the air favors a deep voice, while thinness favors a high pitch; hence voices are deeper in winter than in summer due to the thicker constitution of the air. If the air is abundant and swift, it will produce a very sharp <note>high-pitched</note> sound; if abundant and slow, a very deep one. If it is scarce and swift, it will produce a moderately sharp sound; if scarce and slow, a tiny voice.

<margin>
What exhalation contributes to vocal differences.
</margin>
Finally, exhalation makes the voice either deep or sharp, small or large, high or thin. In addition, it makes it constant, even, firm, and consistent with itself and harmonious, if the exhalation is constant, immovable, and firm; or inconstant, weak, trembling, inconsistent, and dissonant, if it is weak, inconstant, and trembling.

# FIRST COROLLARY.
## On the Cause of Large and Small Voices.

<margin>
How much the slit of the glottis contributes to vocal differences.
</margin>
But now we must examine from where a large and small voice takes its origin. It is certain, therefore, that the various affections of matter are the causes of various differences in the composite whole. Therefore, whoever perceives that a large and small voice arises from large or small motion (as Plato did), or from the narrowness or width of the passage (as Galen did), or from heat and cold (as Avicenna <note>original: "Abēsina"</note> did), perceives correctly. For they assign partial causes for the formation of a small and large voice, which, when joined into one, constitute the adequate cause. This is evident in the loud sound of a trumpet, from which a large voice necessarily emanates if the trumpet is large and joined with a vehement blowing. The size of the trumpet is capable of holding much air, and for the impelling of much air, the internal heat of the trumpeter is needed, in which the force and strength of blowing is placed. We experience a small sound from a trumpet from the opposite causes. I say, therefore, that not only large or small air, heat and cold, and the width or narrowness of the windpipe <note>term: "aspera arteria" - the historical name for the trachea</note> are required, but most especially the width or narrowness of the small slit or glottis is required for the formation of a small or large voice. This is rendered wider by heat and narrower by cold; it also intensifies the force of a greater or lesser exhalation. From this it follows that those who are warm by nature produce a large voice, and the cold produce a small one; for the force of heat easily draws both spirit and air to itself—the more so, the more abundant it is—and just as heat strengthens respiration, so cold weakens it. From this, the reason for a small or large voice is most clearly revealed. Hence, those who are greatly afraid are accustomed to emit only a small and broken voice. For in such fear, the heat seeks the lower parts, leaving the upper parts; when these are destitute of heat, cold necessarily follows, and consequently weakness, and finally from this, a small voice is born.

<summary>This page analyzes how the physical dimensions of the larynx, the quality of inhaled air, and internal body heat determine the pitch, volume, and texture of the human voice. It synthesizes the theories of Plato, Galen, and Avicenna to explain vocal mechanics.</summary>

<header>Of the Great Art of Consonance and Dissonance</header>

## SECOND COROLLARY.
### On the causes of deep and high-pitched voices.

<margin>
Why children and women produce a small voice.
</margin>
**H**ence it is also evident that the same causes concur in the formation of deep and high-pitched voices as those we previously stated for the formation of loud and soft voices. For in children and women, the formation of a high-pitched voice depends on the narrowness of the <term>canal</term><note>The trachea or windpipe</note>, which, as it gradually dilates with age, necessarily causes the voice to pass from high-pitched to deep; until in old age, the organ having once again acquired a drier constitution, it shifts from deep back to high-pitched.

<margin>
Why those reaching puberty change their voice.
</margin>
This is why a change of voice occurs in boys reaching puberty and in women when they are "swelling" <note>likely referring to menstruation or physical maturation</note>, due to the great similarity and dependence that the <term>spermatic vessels</term> have with the vocal vessels. For when the former divert into themselves the heat and moisture intended for the vocal organs, those organs—deprived of those supports—necessarily acquire a different vocal conformation. This is true not only in man, but also in brute animals. For bulls bellow more sharply than cows, and castrated capons crow more sharply than those not cut, and so on for the rest.

<margin>
Why women and Eunuchs permanently retain a high voice.
</margin>
The reason why women and Eunuchs permanently keep a thin voice is because, since the spermatic vessels in women do not have such a necessary dependence on the vocal organs (even if they too feel some change of voice at the time of puberty), yet because this change is almost imperceptible compared to a man's, women therefore always retain nearly the same high-pitched voice. In Eunuchs, however, because the spermatic vessels have been removed, the vocal organs necessarily always preserve the same constitution they acquired from the beginning. This is also evident in animals themselves; for castrated bulls bellow more sharply than uncastrated oxen. But more on these matters at length in our *Musical Physiognomy*.

---

## THIRD COROLLARY.
### On the cause of a rough and smooth voice.

<margin>
Whence comes a smooth and rough voice.
</margin>
**I**t is also clear from what has been said that the smoothness and evenness of the canal produces a smooth voice, while its roughness produces a rough voice. Musical instruments provide a most obvious reason for this; for when the inner surface of a trumpet is corroded, or covered in rust, or roughened by moisture, it produces a rough and hoarse sound. The same appears in pipes, concerning which consult our *Organic Music*. Likewise, we experience that when the organ <note>the throat/larynx</note> is roughened by a cold or superfluous phlegm, a hoarse and uneven voice necessarily follows. But these things are treated more fully in the *Magic of Consonance and Dissonance*.

---

## CHAPTER XIV.
### On the Analogy of the vocal organ with pneumatic Musical instruments.

<margin>
Comparison of the Larynx with a pneumatic organ.
</margin>
**J**ust as air is necessary to produce the sound of a church organ, so in an animal the voice cannot be formed without air. And just as the lungs are endowed with lobes and various receptacles for receiving air, so in a church organ there are several bellows. Furthermore, as air is either drawn in or breathed out by the expansion and contraction of the chest—and consequently by the lungs and the muscles, both those between the ribs and those occupying the outer surface of the chest—so in the [musical] organ, as the bellows are expanded and released by the arm, air now enters, now exits. Just as, moreover, nature constructed the <term>Trachea</term> and the <term>bronchi</term> in animals as the vehicles for air;

<meta>This page continues the anatomical comparison between musical instruments and the human vocal apparatus, specifically focusing on the larynx and trachea, before transitioning into a discussion on the voices of animals.</meta>

<header>Book I. Anatomy of the Nature of Sound and Voice.</header>

constructed; so also for the same purpose, art—the rival of nature—has devised and produced innumerable ducts and channels. Furthermore, just as a figure is carved into the base of the Larynx <note>The "voice box"</note> destined for forming various pronunciations of voices, so too do flutes have their own slits for producing various differences of voices; the keys <note>original: *palmulæ*, literally "little palms"</note> indeed, fitted for the hands, represent the muscles, now opening the glottis <note>The opening between the vocal folds</note> and now closing it.

The Trumpet can also be aptly compared with the Larynx; for the Craftsman, to produce sounds of every kind, added various parts to the trumpet by which it can be prolonged and extended more or less. Moreover, he twisted the extremities with a flared sound to diffuse the air without offense, exactly as in the Larynx.

For what else does the ringed Trachea <note>The windpipe</note> serve if not for the contraction and prolongation of the rough artery <note>term: *aspera arteria* — an archaic name for the trachea, referring to its ridged, cartilaginous texture</note>, from which the intensity and weakness of the voice necessarily results? And just as the first orifice corresponds to the glottis of the trumpet, so the other orifice toward the lungs corresponds to the flared end of the trumpet. For the air, the material of sound, is carried to the opening and struck out; that same air—now much with intensity, now copious with slowness, now little with speed, now weak with a lowering of strength—is drawn from the lungs by the aid of muscles at our pleasure and propelled into the opening of the trumpet. So too the many-mouthed Pipe, or one pierced with many holes, represents the rough artery in many ways. But since these matters have been treated quite extensively in *Organic Musurgy* <note>referring to Kircher's own work, *Musurgia Universalis*</note>, we refer the reader there.

# C H A P T E R  X I V.

## Concerning the natural voices in animals and their anatomy.

<margin>The Providence of God is wonderful in the construction of animals.</margin>
Lest men should accuse Nature of being a stepmother in the provision of things owed to each creature, she has therefore provided for individuals in such a wonderful way that whoever contemplates a little more deeply those things which shine forth—both in that marvelous order of the distribution of animal limbs and in the admirable construction of the senses—cannot sufficiently praise the divine goodness and majesty of the Creator according to His merits; such great order is in all things, such wise providence in all things, such great and incomprehensible workmanship in all things. But since we have discussed these things extensively in *Book X of The Great Art of Light and Shadow, Part 2* <note>Kircher's *Ars Magna Lucis et Umbrae*</note>, concerning the wonderful nature of things represented through microscopes <note>original: *smicroscopia*</note> and the things recently discovered by us with their help, we refer the reader there. Therefore, in this place, we shall treat only the nature and property of the voice of certain animals, lest we should seem to have omitted anything in this our Musurgical work.

### §. I.

## Concerning the Voices of animals in general.

<margin>The natural voices of animals.</margin>
Animals of three kinds can be considered here: quadrupeds, winged creatures, and insects, none of which is capable of modulation and speech, unless you except some birds (which both soothe the ears of listeners with sweet modulation and seize the listeners with amazement by their imitation of human speech); for since their tongue does not correspond to the Larynx, nor has it any aptitude for pronouncing vowels and consonants, as is evident from their anatomy, it is no wonder that articulate pronunciation is also lacking in them. Content therefore with the natural voice granted to them by nature, Oxen low, Sheep bleat, Dogs bark, Horses neigh, Elephants trumpet <note>original: *barriunt*</note>, Lions roar, Donkeys bray, Bears growl <note>original: *trincant*</note>, etc. Although this sound is not so uniform and monotonic <note>original: *ἰσότονος* (isotonos) — of equal tone or pitch</note> that it does not [lead] some others to passions...

<meta>The catchword "fiones" at the bottom of the page suggests the next page begins with "passiones" (passions/emotions).</meta>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of sound and voice, transitioning from general animal communication to a specific case study of a creature from the New World.</meta>

# Of the Great Art of Consonance and Dissonance

<margin>Animals understand one another through the natural service of the voice.</margin>
...allows for the expression of internal passions. For a dog has one voice when it fawns, another when it is angry, another when it barks at strangers, and another when it is driven by the sting of desire for mating. The same must be said of all other animals; and this is the language by which, through the highest providence of GOD, animals of the same species understand each other. To us, however, this can be fully known by no means except through signs and effects. Whether and how a human might naturally be able to understand the language of all animals, since it does not belong to this place, will be discussed elsewhere as a profession.

### §. II.
## Concerning the American Quadruped which they call "Sloth," and the marvelous formation of its voice.

If Music had first been invented in America, I would certainly say it drew its beginnings from no other source than the voice of this wonderful animal. But before we describe its voice, let us first set down a description of the whole animal, which I received by word of mouth this very year in which I write, from Father Juan Toro, Procurator of the Province of the New Kingdom <note>New Kingdom of Granada, modern-day Colombia</note> in America, who both kept such animals with him and performed many experiments on their powers and properties.

<margin>Description of the animal "Haut."</margin>
The shape and nature of this animal are unusual. It is called *Pigritia* <note>Latin for "Sloth" or "Laziness"</note> because of the slowness of its movement; it is the size of a cat, with a foul face and claws protruding like fingers. A mane on the back of its head veils its neck, and with the slow fat of its belly, it sweeps the very ground. It never rises onto its feet, walking so slowly that it can scarcely progress the distance of an arrow-shot in fifteen continuous days. Furthermore, it is unknown what food it eats, for it is never observed to take any nourishment. It lives mostly in the tops of trees, spending about two days in the ascent and just as many in the descent. Moreover, nature

<margin>What are the weapons of this animal against external force?</margin>
seems to have equipped this animal with a double armor against beasts and enemy animals. The first is in its feet, to which nature gave such strength that whatever animal it seizes, it holds so tightly that the creature can no longer free itself from its claws, but is forced to die of hunger due to the long detention. The second is that this beast so moves the humans who rise against it by its appearance, that they abstain from bothering it, moved by an intimate compassion. For besides the tears it sheds from its eyes, it strikes those watching with such a sorrowful gaze that it easily persuades them that a creature so defenseless and subject to such a miserable bodily condition should by no means be troubled. To gain experience of this matter,

<margin>Marvels of this animal.</margin>
the Father cited a little earlier took care to have one of these animals brought to the College of our Society in Cartagena <note>The Jesuit College in Cartagena, Colombia</note> in the New Kingdom. He also placed a long pole under its feet, which it soon gripped so tenaciously that it would not let go at all. The animal, bound in this way by voluntary suspension, was placed with the pole between two beams in a separate location; it remained hanging thus for 40 whole days without food, drink, or any sleep, its eyes always fixed on those watching, whom it moved so much with its sorrowful gaze that there was hardly anyone who was not touched by compassion for it. Finally, having been freed from this long suspension, they threw a dog at it; it soon seized the dog with its feet and held it so strongly for four whole days until the wretched dog, exhausted by hunger, expired. These things are from the mouth of the said Father.

They add furthermore (to return to our purpose) that a voice is not emitted by this beast except at night, and it is utterly prodigious. For with the duration of a single sigh or half-pause interrupting the voice,

<margin>It perfectly renders musical intervals with its sound and voice.</margin>
it perfectly reproduces the six steps of those common intervals, ascending and descending, just as beginners intone the first elements of Music: *Ut, re, mi, fa, sol, la; la, sol, fa, mi, re, vt* <note>The traditional hexachord syllables, the ancestors of Do-Re-Mi</note>. Thus, when the Spaniards first held those shores and perceived this kind of vociferation at night, they thought they heard humans imbued with the precepts of our Music. It is called "HAUT" by the inhabitants for no other reason than that it repeats this sound, *Ha, ha, ha, ha, ha, ha, etc.*, through each step of the interval of a sixth. But so that you may better comprehend the figure of the animal together with its voice, it seemed best to place everything here before your eyes in one synopsis.

## Figure of the Animal "Haut."

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a sloth (referred to as "Haut"). The creature is shown in profile, walking towards the left. It has shaggy fur, human-like facial features, and paws with prominent claws.</image-desc>

**I**t is a wonder, however, that no anatomy of this kind of animal has ever been performed by anyone; for from its internal constitution, one could easily conjecture regarding its natural faculties. For if it possesses a mouth and teeth, and a stomach, I do not see why nature would have assigned it members for storing food without any use. But I do not doubt that our American Fathers <note>Jesuit missionaries in the Americas</note>, informed by my instruction, will undertake a more exact investigation of all these things in the future.

Since the voices of other quadrupeds are well known everywhere, we shall not dwell on them, but shall turn our pen to describing the voices of certain birds.

# §. III.
## Concerning the Voices of Birds.

<margin>The Parrot, a rival of human speech.</margin>
**F**urthermore, among other animals, birds have obtained the greatest lot in singing and speaking; so much so that, what the elephant provides in prudence and memory, the stag in fidelity, the ape in gesture, and the fox in cunning craft, the Parrot and the Nightingale excel among animals in speech and song. And to speak first of the Parrot: this is an Indian bird <note>In this period, "Indian" often referred generally to the East or West Indies/Americas</note>, and nothing is more common than it; it is equipped by nature with such gifts that in the sagacity of its character and its speech (which are proper to our own intellect), it seems to be not far removed from man.

> While the Parrot brings forth human speech with its voice,
> Parrot, light of birds, eloquent pleasure to your masters,
> Skilled imitator of the human tongue, O Parrot.
<note>original Latin: "Psittacus humanas dum promit voce loquelas..."</note>

Caelius <note>Likely Ludovicus Caelius Rhodiginus, a Renaissance humanist</note> relates a remarkable miracle of a Parrot seen in his own times: A Parrot, he says, which Cardinal Ascanius had purchased in Rome for one hundred gold coins, pronounced the entire Creed of the Christian truth <note>The Apostles' Creed</note> just like a human, skillfully and most articulately, with the words continuing in an unbroken flow.

<margin>A wonderful example of a Parrot.</margin>
By a similar account, when Basil, Emperor of the East, cast his son Leo into prison—as if he were weaving a plot against his father at the persuasion of Santabarenus—the entire household resounded with wailing and grief over this bitter fate of Leo, with the professional mourners even singing certain mournful dirges. A Parrot enclosed in a cage, hearing the name of Leo frequently repeated along with the laments and mournful words, learned it. When Basil heard it speaking in its usual way and carrying the name of Leo in its mouth, he immediately relented somewhat from his bitterness of soul, allowing his hard heart to be bent a little; fearing thereafter lest it be said by posterity that he was surpassed in the feeling of compassion by a bird itself, [he released] his son...
