# The Great Art of Consonance and Dissonance
## Page 28

released him from prison, treated him better, and finally, when about to die, placed him in charge of the kingdom; thus Leo <note>Likely referring to a specific historical figure or emperor named Leo</note> wished his safety to be credited to the talkativeness of a parrot for posterity. Innumerable arguments of this kind are found among historians, which, however, lest I leap beyond the prescribed limits, I have deliberately decided to omit.

<margin>The vocal organ in the Parrot closely approaches the human one</margin>
It is asked, therefore, what sort of aptitude has this bird acquired for forming words? Who, I ask, prepared for the Parrot its "Greetings"? <note>original: "χαῖρε" (chaire) - a Greek greeting</note> Certainly nothing else but the miraculous constitution of living beings and the greatest affinity of its members to the members of a human. It possesses a conspicuous size of the head, a mouth that is spacious and endowed with a large concavity, a mobile upper beak, and jaws that swell in the manner of men; a tongue that is thick, broad, and almost fleshy. It has three processes placed at the root under the tongue, by which it renders it wonderfully voluble. The beginning of the windpipe <term>aspera arteria: the trachea</term> is not, as in other birds, equal to the rest of the canal, but much wider above under the glottis, as I remember observing from its anatomy. Since these features are lacking in other birds, with a few exceptions, it is no wonder that it is endowed by nature above all others with the prerogative of human speech.

<margin>The Magpie, an imitator of the human voice.</margin>
I remember observing this same conformation in part also in the Magpie, whence comes that miraculous chatter of the Magpie, by which it repeatedly leaves even the parrots themselves far behind. I myself have seen a Magpie that had learned not only to speak but also to sing with such industry that one who did not see it would think a human was babbling, according to that line of Martial:

> The talkative magpie, I salute you, Master, with a distinct voice;
> If you should not see me, you would deny I am a bird.

<margin>The wonderful variety and skill of the magpie in forming voices.</margin>
Niphus <note>Agostino Nifo, a 16th-century philosopher</note> reports that Magpies mimic the voice of a hunter so exactly that they even deceive the dogs. Pausanias also, in his *Description of Greece* <note>original: "Arcadicis"</note>, reports that a Magpie expressed the crying of an infant so skillfully that it served as a guide for Hercules and was the cause of finding the son of Athenagoras. Oppian also relates that the Magpie imitates the voices of other animals; it lows like a calf, bleats in the manner of a sheep, and now whistles like a shepherd. They also love the words they speak: they do not merely learn them, but they cherish them, and meditating within themselves, they do not hide their intention with care and thought. All these things are confirmed by the words of Plutarch: A certain barber, he says, had a shop before the temple which they call the Greek Forum; he kept a wonderfully vocal and talkative magpie, which rendered the words of men, the voices of oxen, and the sounds of instruments—not because anyone forced it, but because it studied intensely of its own accord to produce all voices and to express nothing that was not sonorous by singing.

<margin>A wonderful event concerning a magpie.</margin>
It happened by chance that in that neighborhood a certain rich man was carried out <note>for burial</note> with many trumpets playing along, and since, according to the custom of that place, there was a delay, the trumpeters, because they were pleasing, were ordered to prolong the song further. From that day the magpie was so mute, so destitute of every voice, that it did not even utter those things which it used to indicate were necessary for itself; that sudden silence was a wonder to everyone, with different people assigning different causes for it. But there was no other cause than exercise and a vehement zeal for meditating, by which it might express the sound of those trumpets. For suddenly it shone forth again, uttering nothing of its usual voices, but only the songs of the trumpets, which it imitated with such genius that it perfectly expressed all the changes and the rhythmic proportions. Indeed, there is no other reason for this wonderful talkativeness in the magpie except the conformation of the larynx and the tongue, and the bird's most sagacious nature. Furthermore, all other birds that have the same constitution of the vocal organ emulate the magpies, such as jackdaws and crows, concerning which you may consult the Ornithologists who treat them more extensively.

## Section IV.
### Concerning the Nightingale.

Nature has deservedly exhibited in the Nightingale <note>original: "Philomela sive Luscinia"</note> a certain "Idea" of all Music, so that voice teachers <term>Phonasci: ancient Greek/Latin term for singing masters</term> may learn how a perfect system of song is to be arranged and how melodies are to be formed in the throat. Nor is she less ambitious to exhibit the sweetness of her song to listeners than the Peacock is to demonstrate the beauty of his tail. The bird...

<meta>This page continues the anatomical and musical study of sound and voice, specifically focusing on the nightingale (Luscinia/Philomela) as a primary example of natural vocal complexity.</meta>

# Book I. Anatomical Treatise on the Nature of Sound and Voice. 29

<margin>
The ambitious nature of the nightingale and its love of praise.
</margin>
They are not only <gloss>soloists</gloss> <note>original: "μονῳδους"</note> but also <gloss>lovers of glory</gloss> <note>original: "φιλοδόξους"</note>: Indeed, in solitary places, the bird modulates only a simple song as if singing to itself, or for the sake of exercise without any elaborate display. But when it finds listeners nearby, then, as if displaying the riches of its voice, it fashions innumerable sounds with admirable variety; for now it draws them out evenly at length, now it bends the same notes, now it sings more minutely and concisely; now it twists and, as it were, curls the voice, now it intensifies it, now relaxes it. It sings some long verses like heroic poetry; others short, like Sapphic verse; sometimes very short ones, like Adonic verse; indeed, it even possesses, as it were, musical games and scales. Furthermore, other younger birds meditate within themselves and attempt to imitate what they perceive from the adults: the pupil listens with great attention, an improved correction is understood, and a certain rebuke is felt from the teacher.

<margin>
Praise of the Nightingale, and the marvelous variety of its voices.
</margin>
Who, moreover, can sufficiently marvel at such a great voice in so small a body? Such persistent breath? While that sound of the throat is now drawn out at length with continuous breath, now varied with a bend, now distinguished by a cut, joined by a twist, sent forward by a recall, or darkened unexpectedly: sometimes it murmurs to itself, now a sound is affected that is flat, deep, sharp, rapid, extended, and where it seems fit, vibrating; now highest, middle, or lowest. Briefly, all things that human art has devised with so many exquisite instruments <term>tortures of the pipes; likely referring to the complex mechanics of organ pipes or flutes</term> are perfected in such narrow throats. And lest any art be lacking, there are several songs for each individual, nor are they the same for all, but each has its own; they compete among themselves, and a spirited contention becomes public, which often ends its life with death, the breath failing before the song; so that it may deservedly be called the Epitome of all harmonic modulation.

But to return to the purpose: when I had often attended to the marvelous power of this little bird's modulation, not without admiration, a great desire came over me, not only to transfer these far-admirable phrases into musical notes, but also to perform an anatomy of the little bird, so that the cause of such great modulation might become more fully known. Both of these were done: for I have set the phrases of the modulations, taken from the mouths of singing Nightingales, into the musical scale, as will be seen shortly hereafter:

<margin>
Anatomy of the Nightingale.
</margin>
I took care that the anatomy be performed by Giovanni Trulli, a distinguished Anatomist, in my presence, so that I might exactly observe the individual parts of the Larynx.

And indeed, we found first a very short tongue, but a larynx that was fibrous and muscular in a marvelous way; the remaining parts, however, shared no difference in anatomy from other birds. From this I concluded that the entire power of this variety of voices proceeds from the innumerable fibers by which the <term>glottis; the opening between the vocal folds</term> is now tightened, now loosened, now drawn apart, now brought together, and bent in every direction, with the tongue striking out the individual voices like a <term>plectrum; a pick used to pluck strings</term>. From this I also inferred that those guttural "diminutions" <note>rapid melodic flourishes</note> which Eunuchs form in the throat and call "trills," are produced not by the tongue, but immediately by the glottis, and are nothing other than a tremor of the throat produced by the exhaled air in the orifice of the glottis. But to comprehend many things in a few words for the sake of the curious Reader, I wished to exhibit here the musical voices of most of the more sonorous birds, represented by my own experience and observation, lest we should seem to have omitted anything of curious matters.

<margin>
What "glottismus" is.
</margin>
First, therefore, so that I might exhibit the individual chirps and trills or chromatic whistlings (which we shall hereafter call <term>glottisms; sounds formed by the glottis</term>, because they are formed by the aid of the glottis) according to the proper time, I took a string a foot and a half long, which by its course and recourse would exactly reflect the pulse of the artery in a healthy man; and by the aid of this, I measured the time or duration of the nightingale's glottisms.

<margin>
The Author's experiment performed on the Nightingale.
</margin>
Furthermore, I attributed each pair of course-recourses of the string to one moderate measure of harmonic time (which is commonly called the *beat* <note>original: "tactum"</note>, or by the Italians the *battuta*), so that one journey of the string—or course-recourse—would respond to the <term>arsis; the upbeat</term>, and the other to the <term>thesis; the downbeat</term>. These things being duly observed, I began the observation very early in the morning in an appropriate place, and I found indeed that the Nightingales arranged their whistling glottisms sometimes by <term>crotchets; quarter notes</term>, sometimes by <term>quavers; eighth notes</term>, and occasionally by <term>semiquavers; sixteenth notes</term>. But where they adorned the more minute glottisms, which the Italians call Trills, they performed them with such speed that they could by no means be rendered by semiquavers, as these were too slow; therefore, triple-fused or quadruple-fused notes (that is, of which 32 or 64 constitute one measure of time) were necessary to express the property of the modulation. But because I noticed those glottisms to be manifold, I call those very clear glottisms modulated with a limpid and tinkling voice <term>pigolisms</term>, but those which it renders through a certain murmur, <term>teretisms</term>...

<margin>
What a Pigolismus is.
</margin>

<meta>catchword: ...retisms</meta>

He also explains his experimental method for transcribing birdsong into musical notation using a pendulum to measure tempo.</summary>

# Of the Great Art of Consonance and Dissonance

<margin>What is a glazismus?</margin>
it seemed fitting to call them *teretismos* <note>original: "teretismos"; a Greek-derived term for bird chirping</note>; but those which continue with a broken voice, as it were, over the same interval, we call *glazismos*. Under these three terms, we encompass all other species and differences of *glottismos* <note>original: "glottismos"; refers to vocal trills or glottal effects</note>. Indeed, it seemed best to represent these species in Illustration III, lest we appear to have omitted anything serving the reader's curiosity.

Behold, these are the vocal trills which the **Nightingale** <note>original: "Luscinia"</note> exhibits to us, besides which we could note no others that were not the same regarding the inflection of the voice: yet they are innumerable if we consider their mixture. For they exhibit these vocal trills with wondrous industry on every step of the double-octave interval <note>original: "disdiapason"</note>; then they mix these *pigolismos* <note>vocalizations resembling the peeping of chicks</note>, *glacismos*, and these *teretismos* so variously that an almost infinite variety of harmonic modulations flows from them. Therefore, whoever knows how to counterfeit nightingales by whistling, and to perfectly express these inflections of notes, may know that he will perfectly express the song of the **Philomelas** <note>original: "Philomelarum"; a poetic name for nightingales</note> according to all rhythms and tempos.

## COROLLARY.

From what has been said, it is clear that the tremor of the glottis, by which the nightingale plays in these types of *pigolismos* and *teretismos*, is by far the most frequent and equals (as I have also learned by experience) the tremor of a single string stretched to the weight of one pound, the length of which was one pace, and the thickness like that of a straw, which, when struck, used to tremble almost infinitely in the space of one second; and you will find that the glottis trembles just as many times in forming a *pigolismos* or *teretismos*. This matter proves the truly admirable providence of God, who granted to this little animal—destined for the relaxation of the human mind—so high a degree of music that it not only surpasses all mechanical art in the speed of forming musical phrases, but also eludes the industry of all musicians by its genius. For it affects not only the diatonic, but also the chromatic and the **enharmonic** <note>original: "exharmonicam"</note> method of singing, as appears in the third vocal trill in a certain wondrous way: so that no instrument exhibits the **chromatic-enharmonic** steps more exactly than the nightingale does with its throat. Nor do I doubt that this little bird, on account of the fit constitution of its vocal organ, would be most capable of human speech if there were a Master who could perfectly express letters and syllables by whistling.

<margin>Nightingales speaking with a human voice.</margin>
Indeed, what **Aldrovandi** <note>Ulisse Aldrovandi (1522–1605), a renowned Italian naturalist</note> relates concerning the deeds of three nightingales at an inn in Augsburg in a certain monastery—who conversed among themselves by day and night—many judge to be fabulous, or certainly will have it that it could not happen except by some notable imposture or the work of an intervening demon. But what we think of this, we shall indicate more opportunely in the following section.

### §. V.
### Concerning the voices of other birds.

I shall add only this one admirable thing in this place, which I would never have believed unless my own experience had made me certain of the truth of the matter; for the case is thus: There is here in Rome, in a famous monastery of the Order of Preachers, the Very Reverend Father Master **Damiano à Fonseca**, a Portuguese man of great learning and authority, who has a little bird enclosed in a cage in his study. It is of that species of larks which they call a **Calandra** <note>original: "Gallandram"; the Calandra Lark, known for mimicry</note>, so skillfully instructed by the brothers of the said Order that it not only pronounces the Litanies of the Saints as if with a human voice, but also chatters many other things which can by no means be perceived without admiration. When the Most Reverend Lord **Diego de Franchis**, Abbot of St. Praxedes—a man most cultivated in every kind of learning—first reported this marvel to me, I could scarcely be induced to have faith in what was reported, unless I were an ear-witness to such a portent. Therefore, in the year 1648, on the 16th of March, accompanied by the mentioned Abbot and Father **Giacomo Viva**, a distinguished mathematician of our Society, I went to the said Father's chamber. And while we waited attentively in silence for the event, at last the said little bird, after the sweetest songs and various murmurs, clearly [pronounced] several names of Saints in the Italian language...

# Book I. Anatomical Treatise on the Nature of Sound and Voice.

...began to pronounce clearly and distinctly; now subjoining these words (*pray for us* <note>original: "ora pro nobis"</note>) now those (*Jesus Christ Crucified* <note>original: "Iesus Christus Crucifixus"</note>), and then others and others, until it wonderfully exhibited 70 different names in such a way that hardly anyone could persuade themselves that it was a bird whose tongue they perceived to be so similar to a human's. And although I remember reading many exotic accounts of various birds affecting the human voice, as was mentioned a little earlier; nevertheless, I have found none among the historians who noted having observed anything similar in a Calandra lark. From this I infer that Nightingales <note><term>Nightingales</term> (Latin: *Luscinias*), renowned for their complex songs.</note> are not as inept at forming speech as they are at song, as many believe; and thus that story of the Nightingales speaking in an Augsburg inn, on the testimony of Aldrovandi <note>Ulisse Aldrovandi (1522–1605), a famous Italian naturalist.</note>, is not so impossible <note>original: "$\alpha\delta\acute{\upsilon}\nu\alpha\tau o\nu$" (adynaton)</note> as someone might persuade themselves; indeed, I have no doubt that all birds which nature has equipped with harmonious song are also capable of forming the human voice.

The Goldcrest <note>original: *Regulus*</note> follows closest to the Nightingale; it borrows several phrases in forming its vocal trills <note><term>glottismis</term>: technical term for bird-trills or throat-modulations.</note> from the Nightingale, though less aptly and quickly. It also forms trills, but always in the same manner. The Finch, the Linnet, the Titmouse, the Flamingo, the Robin, the Lark, and all such singing birds <note>original: *aues phonascæ*</note>, none of them, however, reach that variety of modulations which the Nightingale expresses.

The remaining birds certainly have a sonorous voice, but it is adorned with none of the aforementioned types of trills; such are the Rooster, the Hen, the Cuckoo, the Swallow, the Hoopoe, the Owl, the Quail, and the like. Since their voice is not ordained for the delight of men, they express only that voice which suffices for explaining the passions of the soul. But I thought I would not be acting without purpose if I were to record the voices of certain ones here in musical notation.

And indeed, the Rooster especially emits various voices; one while moved by jealousy; another while he treads the hens; another while he indicates the time, which is the proper voice of the Rooster, whence also comes "cock-crow" <note>original: *gallicinium*</note>. Moreover, he forms his voice in almost the manner expressed in **Illustration III, mark A**.

<margin>Natural voices of birds.</margin>
Where note that the last articulation of the voice does not end in unison, but from that same sound it immediately declines by a third or a fourth.

The Hen, truly, forms various voices in a similar way; for she cries out one way while she gathers her chicks, another while she is in labor, another while she burns with anger, etc. While she is laying eggs, she makes a leap from a unison through a sixth, as they express in **Illustration III, mark B**. Truly, they indicate the voice of her calling her chicks in the said **Illustration, mark C**.

The Quail always and repeatedly repeats the following chirping <note>original: *pigolismum*</note> in place of a song; as the **notes at D in Illustration III** set forth.

The Cuckoo <note>original: *Coccyx* or *Cuculus*</note>, having received its name from its voice, always warbles a two-syllable voice, not in unison, but at the interval of a most perfect minor third; as appears in the **notes at E of Illustration III**.

In the same way, the songs of all birds could be expressed if someone had the leisure to observe the process of each one in forming its voices; for in each one he will find a certain definite movement of the voice entirely distinct from the rest: thus Swallows twitter, Hoopoes "poop," Thrushes "kickle." Partridges "titibate." The Sparrow chirps, the Magpie "chatter-bits," the Goose cackles, the Starling whistles, etc.

The Goldcrest, the Bee-eater, and the Red-breasted Swallow are accustomed to "zinzulate" with a similar song.

<margin>The Blackbird, a distinguished singer.</margin>
The Blackbird <note>original: *Merula*</note> expresses harmonic differences best of all, so that what the Parrot, Magpie, Raven, and Jackdaw can do in expressing the human voice, and what the Nightingale and other singing birds can do in expressing trills, the Blackbird can do among all other birds by singing—especially if it is instructed by a skilled master teacher. For there is scarcely any kind of song so irregular <note>original: *heteroclitum*</note> that it does not learn it, pronouncing individual notes so exactly that it does not err even in the smallest semitone. There are, besides, other birds to which the ancients attributed great skill in singing, among which the Swan occurs in the first place; there is a report that they sing most sweetly near impending death, whence the "Swan Song" is celebrated in all the monuments of the ancient Poets and Orators. <margin>Whether the Swan sings.</margin> And certainly, if we consider the organ of the voice in the Swan, nothing more wonderful occurs among all animals; <margin>Anatomy of the Swan.</margin> for the windpipe <note>original: *trachea*</note> is seen inserted into the breastbone <note>original: *Sterno*</note>, extended with various bends like a flexible trumpet, received in a certain hollow...

# Of the Great Art of Consonance and Dissonance

receptacle, emerging from there again, and being led around in another bend, it is finally inserted into the lungs by a bifurcated duct. Thus, the construction of the trachea is so admirable that it suggests something divine in the formation of the voice. However, he who knows the hidden counsel of nature in the functions of organs will easily see that these various ducts of the trachea were bestowed upon the Swan not for the sake of music, but for other purposes. <margin>Why nature gave the Swan such a long trachea.</margin> For since the Swan acts as a notable diver, and is frequently occupied for entire hours under water digging in the mud and seeking necessary food, it first required a long trachea in proportion to the length of its neck; and because this was not sufficient, nature inserted a hollow receptacle in the breastbone itself, so that it might contain air within it, like a kind of pouch, necessary for breathing, lest it be forced to emerge constantly and leave its food. Therefore, such a prodigious trachea was given so that it might carry the necessary air with it, as if in a womb. This can be seen in all fishing birds and divers. For if such a trachea had been given to it for music, it is truly a wonder that nowhere and never has such a singular "Swan-song" <note>original: "concentum Cygnæum"</note> been perceived by anyone in these times. Whence I judge this Swan-song to be entirely fabulous, introduced by Poets on the occasion of the Nymphs, whom they called Swans because of their whiteness. For nature does not always ordain the organs of voice in animals for the beautiful formation of sound, but for other uses necessary to the life of the living creature, as was evident in this organ of the Swan. Otherwise the Pig, which by the benefit of nature has been allotted a most beautiful Larynx, would sing best and most sweetly of all—which would be ridiculous, not to say stupid, to assert.

## §. VI.

### Concerning the voices of certain Insects, such as Frogs, Cicadas, Locusts, and Crickets.

<margin>The hiss of the Basilisk.</margin>
Insects also have their voices, instituted by a certain hidden counsel of nature for the proper performance of their operations. Who does not know the hissing of serpents? The hiss of the Basilisk, if Pliny is to be believed, is so formidable that all animals flee in consternation from it. But leaving these things aside, I have undertaken to explain only two things here which bring no small wonder to those who hear them: the croaking of Frogs, and the trilling of Cicadas, Locusts, and Crickets. Certainly, to reach the causes of such a hidden voice, I have omitted no experiments. Whence I established a perfect anatomy of these beasts, by which at last my wishes were fully satisfied. For those things about which I first doubted, I soon learned by perfect and ocular demonstration.

## §. VII.

### Concerning the cause of the croaking of Frogs.

<margin>The "Ololigo" of the Frog.</margin>
How Frogs produce that croaking (which they call *ololigo* <note>A Greek-derived term for a croak or a specific cry of a frog or owl.</note>), even Aristotle doubted. For it is certain that they do not produce the voice with the glottis, but with another part of the body. To find this, we will first exhibit its anatomy, so that the curious Reader may know how skillful, wise, and ingenious nature has been in the fabric of animals, and how admirably she has conducted herself in joining all the parts together—both in the aforementioned animals, and especially in the Frog, whose construction of the organ suggests something divine.

<margin>Anatomy of the Frog</margin>
First, therefore, inspect Figure IV of Diagram II, where you will find the tongue, which is loose toward the throat, but is found to be immobile at the front near the jaw. The slit of the Larynx is difficult to find because of its smallness, and almost escapes the eyes. **A**, therefore, is the upper part of the middle jaw of the Frog. **BB** are the cavities in which the two eyes rest, resembling an oval shape like two large tubercles. **C** is the hole following next, marking the beginning of the throat...

# Book I. Anatomical Treatise on the Nature of Sound and Voice.

noting both the cavity and the organ of the croak. **D.** denotes the Larynx, near which, a little further down, is another opening not unlike the first, resembling a small slit, which is the shape of the Larynx. **E.** The skin that covered the body of the tongue when it was naturally placed inside; but now, being loose on the inside and intentionally turned outward, it allows both the slit and the tongue's shape, size, and position to be observed. **FF.** refers to the lower jaw.

In the seventh figure, you see the lungs filled with air and swollen, presenting an appearance like marble stones affected by various spots and colors. Part **A** is the membranous part covering the sections of the mouth. **B** is the glottis <note>The opening between the vocal folds</note>, or the shape or slit of the Larynx. **C** is that slender body, namely the heart, just as **DD** refers to the lungs.

<margin>How the croak of the Frog is produced.</margin>
These things being established, I say that the croak is produced by the lip of the lower jaw, while the upper jaw remains motionless; for while it receives a small amount of water within the throat, the breath blown out from the lungs strives to penetrate the intervening water held in cavity **C**. Finally, as the lungs blow more vehemently, the air bursts violently through the slit **D**, and by rippling the water, it emits this croak which we hear. Therefore, the sound is not made by the glottis as in other animals, but by the force of the air expired from the lungs through the glottis and striking the water, not unlike what happens in hydraulic pipes <note>Water organs</note> used to imitate the song of birds, where pipes are placed in water and blown into with great force. For we find no muscles suited for forming a voice in the glottis, as are found in other animals. From this we conclude that the croak is not properly a "voice," but a sound caused by the impact of exhaled air against the water contained within the frog's throat; we even excited this sound in a recently dead frog by passing a pipe through the rib and inserting it into the trachea, then blowing forcefully into the water collected in the throat. We wished to describe these things more diligently here so that the common error of those who thoughtlessly believed the frog's croak was made by the help of the glottis and Larynx might be removed; for the croak is not made otherwise than we have described.

<margin>What the purpose of the Frog's croak is.</margin>
Furthermore, one might rightly doubt for what purpose nature primarily ordained this sound. I say, therefore, that the croak was ordained for the particular convenience and good of the animal; since it lives mostly under water and requires a great deal of air (as the swollen and vast lungs sufficiently declare), nature granted this cavity of the throat so that the water collected there might restrain the breath gathered in the lungs. Since it also lacks an epiglottis, to prevent the air in the lung from escaping all at once—not unlike an inflated bladder when opened—nature willed that the water collected in the cavity of the throat should take the place of the epiglottis; so that it might hold the air inside, and when croaking was necessary, partly release the internal air and partly draw in new air.

# §. VIII.
## Concerning the voice of the Cicada, the Locust, and the Cricket.

<margin>The structure of the body of Crickets.</margin>
**A**nother difficulty lies in assigning the cause of the voice of Cicadas and Crickets, which we could by no means achieve without a prior anatomy. For therein lies hidden a truly wonderful craftsmanship of wise nature. For it has a double Drum <note>original: "Tympanum"</note> under the thorax, covered by something like a double scale; likewise, the thorax and abdomen are hollowed out into a large cavern, the upper part of which, enclosed by a yellow membrane like a vault, receives the sound. This sound then echoes back into that ample chamber from the shaken air. Certain very hard small membranes located at the sides shake the air; these seem to be not poorly compared to plates or thin sheets of brass <note>original: "auricalcho"</note>, for when agitated, they produce almost a similar sound. These membranes are protected by their own shell, yet in such a way that they are not entirely enclosed, but a free passage is open to the air; they move freely by two muscles strong in movement, originating from the bone that encircles the upper belly. But let us declare these things in the anatomical figures *I. II. III. IV.* from *Diagram II.*

In the first figure, therefore, you have the entire small body of the Cicada represented. Where **A** is the head of the Cicada. **B.** the thorax or middle belly. **C.** the entire abdomen. **DD.** the larger wings. **EE.** the smaller wings.

# Of the Great Art of Consonance and Dissonance

And although, on account of the animal's smallness and its tender substance, the parts can scarcely be separated from one another unharmed even with the greatest diligence, they have nevertheless been arranged by us with such great industry that each part can be discerned without difficulty, as appears from *Figure II*, in which **A, A,** denotes the double drum <note>original: "tympanum"</note> lurking beneath a double scale. **B.** The double cavern, in which the small membranes are hidden, by which the chirping or sound is produced. **C. The Arch** <note>original: "Fornix"</note> or chamber, where the enclosed air, being struck, resounds.

In order that the organs effective in producing the chirp might be better exposed, we have arranged *Figure III*, in which **A, A,** is the cavern. **B.** is the thin, gold-leaf-like membrane <note>original: "bractealis membranula"</note> that creates the sound.

**C.** The yellow membrane closing the upper part of the arch, which, if ruptured, causes the sound to perish. Truly these things appear even better in *Figure IV*, where the animal excites the membranes to sound, and **B.** marks the arch.

<margin>
The cause of the sound of crickets.
</margin>
We have seen the anatomy; now it is time to show the production of the sound. In this great apparatus of organs, those organs must be chiefly considered by which this genus of insect produces that most acute chirping or sound; these are the double drum lurking under the double scale, the twin cavern, and the arch; to which are added the thin plates or membranes. I say, therefore, that an animal of this kind in the greatest heats (for it does not sing at any time other than summer) is in need of cooler air, and for that reason, while it shakes certain very hard membranes lurking within the drum, it agitates the air as if with a kind of fan, and by striking it against that plate-like membrane, *marked with the letter B in Figure II*, it excites the sound. Furthermore, the air, being drawn into that hollow chamber, or cavern—the arch which that yellow plate-like membrane encircles—produces there, on account of the hollowness of the place (not otherwise than happens in a struck military drum, the cavity of which is full of air), that sound or shrill tinkling. Hence, those vibrating membranes are not entirely closed by the shell <note>original: "cortice"</note> by which they are covered, but free access is always granted to the air. Wherefore nature also bestowed upon it those two muscles with which it can agitate and fan those membranes by voluntary motion; the air, thus fanned and agitated more vehemently, being struck against another yellow membrane stretched before the arch within the hollow, finally produces the sound. Moreover, that small membrane is so apt for sounding that even when separated from the rest of the body it emits a certain tinkling when struck; and since the arch consists of the animal's very dry constitution, being almost bony, it can easily produce such a tinkling. And this is the genuine cause of the voice of crickets: the efficient cause is the animal itself; the formal cause is the plate with the arch; the material cause is the struck air; the final cause is the cooling of the animal, or even the flight of certain birds lying in wait for it, who are restrained by some hidden power of this sound.

From this it is clear how false are the opinions of those who think this chirp is formed by the animal's mouth; they also err who think this sound is produced only by the chirping of the wings, as happens in locusts. Truly, whoever desires to understand the matter more clearly, let him take an experiment in living anatomy, which is easy, and he will know by the thing itself that all we have said is true.

<margin>
The voice of locusts.
</margin>
The genus of locusts, however, produces sound with the wings, so that the wings, placed one upon the other, are moved; the upper wing has on its innermost part a blackish and hard body, placed transversely. The lower wing has a small body of the same substance, placed on the outer part of the extremity of the upper edge; adjacent to this is a beautiful drum; by their mutual friction that chirp is excited (indeed, even in dead ones by the touch of a stylus), and much greater in the living one, since more abundant air is intercepted there. Hence the very thin wings strike the agitated air against that membrane, and hence the sound in the drum. *See Figure II and V of Scheme II.*

<margin>
A double genus of Cricket.
</margin>
There are two species of crickets; for some are of the fields, others are domestic, familiar to heated rooms <note>original: "hypocaustis"</note>, kitchens, and hearths. Both, however, excite sound in exactly the same way as locusts, wherefore we have decided not to dwell on them any longer.

<header>Book I. Anatomical Treatise on the Nature of Sound and Voice.</header>

# CHAPTER XV.

## Problems concerning Sound and the Voice.

It has pleased me to conclude this book with a "finishing touch" <note>original: "coronide," referring to a decorative flourish at the end of a manuscript</note> of several curious questions regarding matters pertaining to sound and the voice. We shall deal first with the causes of sounds, then with the qualities of song and the voice, and especially with those matters that concern the ear.

<margin>Problem 1. Why does a voice sound sharper from a distance and duller from nearby?</margin>
It is asked, therefore, first—something which anyone might rightly marvel at—why a voice is perceived as sharper <note>higher in pitch</note> from a distance and duller <note>lower or more muffled</note> from nearby? For one might persuade oneself that the exact opposite ought to happen; for since gravity <note>low pitch</note> consists in slowness, and sharpness <note>high pitch</note> in speed, and since objects move more slowly the further they are from their starting point, and faster the closer they are to the start (as is seen in the flight of an arrow), surely a sound perceived from afar ought to be much lower than a sound perceived up close. Yet experience contradicts this; for it is perceived as duller up close and sharper from afar, as has been said, the cause of which is sought.

<margin>The solution to the question, therefore, is thinness.</margin>
The answer is that the sound in the more remote part is not actually sharper, but only seems so, because a sound produced in a nearby place is, as it were, "blunted" by the violence of the moved air; whereas in a more remote part, it is gradually weakened, and thus, on account of its thinness <note>exilitatem</note>, it is thought to be sharper.

<margin>Problem 2. Why does cold water pouring from the same vessel sound sharper than hot water?</margin>
It is asked, secondly: Why does cold water pouring from the same vessel make a sharper noise than hot water?
The answer is that since cold water is much denser and more compact than hot water, it gives forth a much sharper sound than hot water, which, having been thinned by fire and dissolved into vapors, causes a certain slowness of motion, from which a lower pitch consequently results.
Added to this is the fact that the surrounding air is made thicker by the vapor of the water, and consequently the sound is made thinner. As for why a man has a sharper voice in summer and a lower one in winter, this has been partly discussed in the preceding sections and will be demonstrated in the following books.

<margin>Problem 3. Why does a calf give a lower voice than when it has become an ox?</margin>
It is asked, thirdly: Why do animals give a sharp voice when they are younger and a deep voice when they are older, with the exception of calves, which give a deeper voice then than when they have already become oxen?
The answer is that the different constitution of the larynx <term>larynx: the hollow muscular organ forming an air passage to the lungs and holding the vocal cords</term> is the cause of this. For since the larynx in calves is usually looser, and gradually contracts into a narrow space with the passage of time, it is no wonder that a calf bellows more deeply than oxen. Other animals, on the contrary, have a larynx that dilates from a narrow state over time, which results in a voice that is either sharper or deeper accordingly.

<margin>Problem 4. Why is it more difficult to sing with a sharp voice than a deep one?</margin>
It is asked, fourthly: Why is it more difficult to sing with a sharp <note>high-pitched</note> voice than a deep one?
The answer is that in forming a sharp voice, the vocal organs are vehemently strained; and from this strain, difficulty arises. In a deep voice, however, this difficulty ceases; thus the proposition is clear. As for why those suffering from fever cannot sing immediately after the fever ceases, I say the cause is the roughness of the throat.

<margin>Problem 5. Why does the sight of a wolf induce hoarseness?</margin>
It is asked, fifthly: Why does the sight of a wolf induce hoarseness? <note>A common classical and medieval belief that if a wolf sees a man first, the man loses his voice.</note>
The answer is that this happens from the vehement dread by which a man is shaken when he sees a wolf; for the heat and the spirits, contracted by fear from the highest to the lowest parts, diminish the power of movement which is in the heat, and render the cooled vocal instruments powerless to bring forth the voice. Thus, in a most splendid assembly of illustrious men, or likewise in the presence of a King or a great Prince, many become mute with fear, having forgotten the things they were to propose. Furthermore, that a wolf pours forth no quality from itself that deprives one of voice is proven to me by this argument: that I have seen many wolves here and there, already tamed and living amicably with men, yet without the effect of that hidden quality which people claim exists in wolves.

<meta>catchword: lunt (continuation of "volunt")</meta>

# The Great Art of Consonance and Dissonance

...they desire, even though a physical axiom teaches us that the same cause always produces the same effect.

<margin>Problem 6. Why do we not listen as gladly to a human imitating a flute as to the flute itself?</margin>
The sixth question is: Why do we not listen as gladly to a human who shapes their voice in the manner of a flute as we do to the flute itself?
The answer is: Because that which belongs to nature occurs more pleasingly than that which belongs to imitation. But as to why we listen more gladly to singers if flutes harmonize with them rather than lyres <note>The author is likely referring to the "lira da braccio" or similar stringed instruments of the 17th century</note>, the reason is that the flute, since it is driven to sound by breath, is more similar to the origin of the voice. It covers the errors of the voice better—indeed, it perfects the voice and keeps it pure. This does not happen with the lyre (which is of a heterogeneous or "different" nature); rather, the lyre detects all the errors of the voice. Therefore, since the sound of the flute blends better with the human voice, the reason why we perceive singers more gladly alongside a flute is evident.

<margin>Problem 7. Why is dissonance perceived better in a deep voice than in a high one?</margin>
The seventh question is: Why is dissonance perceived more easily in a <term>barytone</term> <note>original: "barytona"; here meaning a low-pitched or deep voice</note> voice than in an <term>oxytone</term> <note>original: "oxytona"; here meaning a high-pitched or sharp voice</note> voice?
The answer is: Because a deeper or barytone voice consists of slow motion and takes up more time, whereas a sharp or oxytone voice is fast and consumes less time. Since slow things are grasped better than fast ones, it appears that dissonance can be noted more easily in the former than in the latter.

<margin>Problem 8. Why do those who yawn hear less?</margin>
The eighth question is: Why do we hear less when we yawn?
The answer is: Because the sluggish breath which we shake out from the edges of the throat enters the ears, fills them, and creates a noise that obscures the sound coming in from the outside. Additionally, in yawning, the jaws are pulled apart; by this distraction, a less free passage is granted for the external air to reach the ears.

<margin>Problem 9. Why is sound perceived better inside a house than outside?</margin>
The ninth question is: Why do those inside a house better perceive a sound usually made outside, while those positioned outside perceive less of a sound stirred up inside the house? The reason is that a sound emitted outside the house, having found a free space, is dissipated and grows faint, and therefore is not perceived as well. However, an external sound that glides inside a house is gathered together there, and consequently is presented more firmly to the ears.

<margin>Problem 10. Why are we more delighted by hearing than by reading?</margin>
The tenth question is: Why are we more delighted by hearing than by reading?
The answer is: Various reasons can be assigned for this. First, because we learn and preserve things heard more easily than things read. Second, because the voice of another affects us more by its reading than a book, which is a "mute narrator." Furthermore, in a narration, there is for the most part a social companionship most agreeable to human nature, which does not happen in solitary reading.

<margin>Problem 11.</margin>
The eleventh question is: Why is the sense of hearing easily offended in a tender age?
The answer is: Since a certain proportion is required between a power and its object, if the object is too vehement and the organ is still tender and weak (as it is in infancy), it can easily be harmed.

<margin>Why do divers under water easily lose their hearing?</margin>
As to why hearing shrinks from water more than the other senses, the reason is that water, by its coldness, badly affects the nerve which is the principal part of the ear. Hence, the ears of <term>divers</term> <note>original: "Vrinatorum"; refers to breath-hold divers or "skin divers" of the period</note> under water are often liable to a great danger of rupture. For while divers hold their breath more vehemently under water, the <term>eardrum</term> <note>original: "tympanum"</note>, being more vehemently inflated by the retained breath and unable to yield to the rushing water, must finally break. For this reason, they are accustomed to drop oil into their ears beforehand, because oil restrains the water rushing with force and repels it as something incompatible with itself. From this, the reason is also clear why the ear should not be rubbed at all during yawning; for then the eardrum is distended, and it could easily be injured by such rubbing.

<margin>Problem 12. Whence come those various sounds and ringings in the sick?</margin>
The twelfth question is: From where do those various sounds often arise in the ears of the sick?
The answer is: Those sounds are born from the motion of different humors <note>In 17th-century medicine, "humors" were bodily fluids thought to govern health</note> occupying the inner ear, agitated by various impulses. Thus, a hissing arises from a gentle breath escaping thinly; a <term>ringing</term> <note>original: "tinnitus"</note> from its interrupted course; a clatter from a strong impulse; and a fluctuation from the agitation of the humor. Hence, when an external noise is excited, the internal one ceases, because the lesser expels the greater.

<margin>Problem 13.</margin>
The thirteenth question is: Why are those deaf from birth also mute?
The answer is: This happens because of the communication of the nerves of the tongue and the ear, about which...

And these are the things which we wished to attach here by way of a concluding flourish <note>original: "coronidis loco," referring to a decorative flourish at the end of a book</note>, to which it pleases us to add an Appendix on Phonognomy, which we hope will not be unwelcome to the reader, etc.

# APPENDIX
## CONCERNING
# PHONOGNOMY
### OR
### Concerning the judgment and conjectures that can be made regarding the temperament of any given body based on its sound and voice.

Since the various qualities of the voice depend upon the natural constitution of the acoustic organs, it is certain that an art can be found here by which, from the constitution of the human voice, we may arrive at the interior inclinations and passions of the soul. This art we call, not incongruously, <term>phonognomy</term> <note>The study of character or physical nature through the sound of the voice.</note>; and we have decided that it should be opportunely treated in this place.

<margin>Twofold sound: animate and inanimate.</margin>
It must be noted, therefore, that we can consider two types of sound in this place: animate and inanimate. Animate sound is further divided into rational or irrational. <term>Rational sound</term> is nothing other than the voice produced by an animal by means of the windpipe <note>original: "asperę arterię"</note>, the larynx, and the epiglottis, with the intention of signifying something. By this last detail, we distinguish the human voice from the sounds of brutes and inanimate objects, and from those sounds which are made without intention. <term>Irrational animate sound</term> is the voice of brutes, established to signify the passions of the soul. <term>Inanimate sound</term>, however, is the collision of any non-living bodies, such as thunder, the explosions of cannons <note>original: "tormentorum," referring to siege engines or artillery</note>, the striking together of wood, metals, and other bodies, etc. Therefore, when the sound of one body is sharper <note>higher in pitch</note> than the sound of another body of equal size, it is necessarily concluded that the body with the sharper sound consists of a thinner substance, and carries a predominance of air or fire. But, so that you may proceed more securely in this art, take this experiment.

# PHONOCRITIC EXPERIMENT
->Concerning the investigation of the nature of different kinds of wood, bones, and minerals through sound.<-

<margin>Density or rarity of bodies is gathered from sound.</margin>
Let rectangular blocks <note>original: "parallelopipeda"</note> or cylinders, all of equal size, be made from every kind of wood (previously properly dried); suspend these by a thread and strike them with a plectrum. You will perceive the most diverse types of sounds; for you will find some sound a semitone, others a whole tone, a major third <note>original: "ditonum"</note>, or an augmented fourth <note>original: "tritonum"</note>, others a perfect fourth <note>original: "diatessaron"</note>, a perfect fifth <note>original: "diapente"</note>, or even an octave <note>original: "diapason"</note>, or otherwise. Having grasped the proportions of these harmonies, you will easily judge the natural construction of the bodies. For since the density of any body arises from the greatest packing together of earthy and watery parts, one will be as much denser than another as it has more packed parts: and the more packed a body is, the heavier it will be; and the heavier...

<meta>Catchword: heavier</meta>

It categorizes sounds into rational, irrational, and inanimate, and proposes an experiment using wooden blocks to determine density via musical intervals.</summary>

--- Page 74 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's investigation into "Phonocritics"—the art of judging the internal nature and elemental composition of substances by the sounds they produce when struck or vibrated.</meta>

# Of the Great Art of Consonance and Dissonance

the heavier it is, the more slowly it will be moved; and finally, the more slowly it is moved, the more deeply <note>original: "grauiùs" - referring to low musical pitch</note> it will sound.

Furthermore, the depth of sound in bodies is twofold: for some have a deep sound that is forceful, while others have one that is deep and dull. The former offers signs of an earthy and dry temperament; the latter of a watery, moist, and poorly compacted one. Truly, since thinness <note>original: "raritas" - the opposite of density</note> takes its origin from a highly porous substance and is capable of holding much air, bodies will be thinner the more porous they are; and the lighter they are, the more swiftly they will be moved, and consequently, they will sound higher and sharper in pitch.

Note, however, that we are not speaking here of soft and liquid bodies, such as wool, feathers, liquids, and similar non-compact things not consisting of solid substance, which we shall discuss later; but of solid and hard bodies. Some of these will also have a sharp, forceful, and penetrating sound; others sharp indeed, but dull and weak. Just as the former provides evidence of porosity and lightness, so the latter provides evidence of porosity, but of a flabbier substance. The same must be said of bones and metallic bodies—gold, silver, copper, iron, and lead. You will find all these things explained most diffusely in our *Organic Musurgia* <note>A reference to Kircher's famous 1650 work on music theory and acoustics, "Musurgia Universalis."</note>. Here, however, from what has already been said, we form the following Phonocritic Canons.

### Canon 1. Phonocritics of solid bodies.

<margin>The method of knowing the predominance of qualities in bodies through sound</margin>
If the sound of some solid body is deep and dull compared to the sound of another equal body, it will give manifest evidence of a watery predominance, as happens in lead, for example, on account of the abundance of mercurial moisture. If, however, it has a sound that is deep but forceful, then you shall certainly judge it to be of an earthy, light, and well-compacted temperament, as can be seen in iron and steel. Furthermore, if the sound of some solid body is sharp and thin, it will give signs of an airy temperament and of a porous and flabbier substance, as appears in tin. Finally, if the sound is sharp, forceful, and penetrating, then you will certainly conclude the body is of a fiery temperament, or of a thin and extremely light substance, as we see in fiery things—for example, in the explosions of muskets, etc.

---

## PHONOCRITIC EXPERIMENT
### Concerning the temperament of liquids.

<margin>Different liquids have different sounds.</margin>
Take three, four, or five glass chalices, all equal in shape and size, which you will fill with different liquids—for example, water, wine, and other distilled waters—but in such a way that they are all filled equally. This being done, rub the rim or edge of the chalice with a moistened finger for as long as it takes until you perceive a sound; this sound will indeed be different according to the diversity of the liquids. For the subtler a liquid is, the sharper it will sound; and the thicker it is, the deeper. Hence oil, since it is more compact and slow, will also sound deeper; water will sound deeper than "water of life" <note>original: "aqua vitæ" - concentrated alcohol or spirits</note>, and this deeper than spirits or "quintessences." Note, meanwhile, that oily liquids, although they are much subtler in substance than elementary water (being of an airy nature), nevertheless cause a somewhat greater depth of sound on account of their slowness and viscous substance. In other liquids lacking this slowness, you will easily judge the elemental predominance according to the following canon.

# Book I. Anatomical Treatise on the Nature of Sound and Voice

## Canon 2. The Phonocritic <note>A term coined from Greek meaning "the judgment or diagnosis of sound."</note> of Liquids.

<margin>How the temperament of liquids can be discerned through sound.</margin>
If the sound of a cup is deep and dull, you may conclude that the liquid poured into it has a watery temperament, as seen in spring water; however, spring water sounds sharper than marsh water, which possesses an earthy and dreg-filled temperament. If the sound is sharp and thin, you have an indication of an airy temperament, as is found in all distilled waters <note>original: "aquis stillaticijs," referring to chemically distilled spirits or essences</note>, which always produce a sharper sound than any elemental water. Finally, if the sound is extremely sharp, subtle, and penetrating, it provides an argument for a fiery temperament, as appears in spirits and "quintessences" <note>The "fifth essence" or purest medicinal extract in alchemy.</note>; these, when placed in equal quantities in identical glass cups, cause a sharper sound than other liquids. Therefore, the sound itself will indicate how much more subtle one liquid is than another. For if one of them sounds an octave <note>original: "diapason"</note> higher than the other, it is certain that it is twice as subtle as the other. But concerning these matters, consult our *Musurgia* <note>A reference to Kircher’s famous 1650 work, *Musurgia Universalis*, on music and acoustics.</note>, which treats all these things most extensively in the section on the magic of consonance and dissonance.

---

## Canon 3. The Phonocritic of the Voice of Animals.

<margin>How the temperaments of animals are known from sound and voice.</margin>
Voice was bestowed upon brute animals by nature for this purpose: that through it they might signify their passions, either to humans or to those of their own kind. For it is established by experience that birds, dogs, cats, and oxen form one kind of voice when they are moved by choler <note>The "yellow bile" humor, associated with anger and heat.</note>, another when by melancholy or phlegm, another when they love and fawn, and another when they desire mating, or are afraid, or desire something vehemently. For when they are moved by choler, it is certain they emit a sharper sound than when they are stimulated by hunger; for choler—being rare, subtle, and thin—sharpens the voice, whereas melancholy and phlegm lower it due to the sluggishness of the humor; the boiling of the blood, however, renders it tempered. If, therefore, the passion is choleric, the voice will be more excited and sharper, as can be seen in dogs and cats driven by rage. When they desire mating, they emit a sharp and moaning voice, an indication of glowing blood. Thus, agitated by one humor or another, they emit different voices. From this knowledge, a science could be formed by which one might understand the voice and language of animals, just as we read concerning Apollonius of Tyana <note>A 1st-century Greek philosopher rumored to understand the language of birds.</note>; and we explain the language of these animals most extensively in our work entitled <margin>The book called The Tower of Babel.</margin> *The Tower of Babel*, where in due time the curious reader will find many rare and new things.

---

## Canon 4. The Phonocritic of the Human Voice.

<margin>How the temperament of men can be known through the voice.</margin>
Although the variety and multitude of voices in men is no less than the variety of human faces, the affections of the inner man can perhaps be tracked more easily and certainly through the voice than from the diversity of faces. Hence we read that Plato, when he desired to know the character of a certain youth, was accustomed to say: "Speak, so that I may see you." By this, he seemed to mean nothing else but the method by which he could know the inner character of the soul through the voice. The Sacred Pages <note>The Bible (Genesis 27).</note> also testify that Isaac recognized the difference between the brothers not by touch, but by voice. Galen <note>A prominent Greek physician in the Roman Empire.</note> also knew the capacity of the chest <term>thorax: the chest cavity</term> through the voice, when he said that those who have a strong voice, which they can continue without interruption, have a large chest. But here Galen speaks only of a strong voice, the cause of which is a wide and vast chest with large, ample lungs and strong muscles of the larynx and epiglottis. There are, however, other differences of voices which take their origin not so much from the chest, lungs, and epiglottis, as from the temperament: such as a voice that is slow, fast, sweet, harsh, distinct, confused, shrill, sharp, deep, and bass, mediocre and tempered, etc., all of which [differences] of voices...

It references classical figures like Plato and Galen to support the idea that the voice is a window into the soul and the physical body.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Great Art of Consonance and Dissonance," specifically linking musical theory and vocal qualities to human temperament and character, a field known as musical physiognomy.</meta>

# The Great Art of Consonance and Dissonance
<meta>Catchword: Concerning</meta>

Since the differences [in sound] are indicators of various temperaments, they can easily be compared with colors, as was stated in the *Great Art of Light and Shadow* <note>A reference to Kircher’s 1646 work *Ars Magna Lucis et Umbrae*</note>. Here, however, we shall add some further conjectures regarding the passions and affections of men, which can be gathered and inferred with some probability from their diverse voices.

## Section I.
### Concerning the loud and deep voice.

<insert>2</insert>
<margin>
Conjecture from the voice of the Donkey.
</margin>
Those, therefore, who cry out loudly with a deep voice are, according to the testimony of Aristotle <note>referring to the pseudo-Aristotelian *Physiognomonica*</note>, compared to Donkeys; from which it is inferred that they are injurious, abusive, and petulant. For that donkeys are railers and abusive—their nature being petulant, injurious, and insolent when they are well-fed—is sufficiently evident. Whence Aristotle concludes thus: *The donkey has a very loud and deep voice, and the donkey is indiscreet, petulant, and abusive; therefore, those whose voice is loud and deep are petulant, indiscreet, and abusive.* But it remains for us to see the reason for this. We see that a loud voice is present in those animals that have a large windpipe <term>aspera arteria: the trachea</term> and emit much air therefrom; they have a deep voice who drive much air out of the windpipe slowly. Therefore, large animals have a loud voice, because they possess large instruments [organs].

<margin>
Reason for the assertion.
</margin>
It is probable, therefore, that such men—those who have a loud voice—are also of broad chest, with a large windpipe and a thick neck; for this shows that the element of Earth dominates in them according to their mass.

If, however, we perceive deepness joined with a loud voice (which slowness, the offspring of cold, causes), it indicates a cold and dry temperament—that is, an earthy one. He who has acquired such a temperament is indeed greedy and fearful, but otherwise indiscreet and of a dejected mind; moreover, such people are intolerable in their insolence during prosperity, but in adversity, they are more fearful than little hares. Cornelius Tacitus noted this nature in Caligula.

## Section II.
### Concerning a voice that is deep at the beginning and ends in a high pitch.

<margin>
Conjecture from the voice of Cattle.
</margin>
Those who, beginning with a deep voice at the start, end in a high pitch, are categorized with Cattle as being complainers, prone to anger, and sorrowful; for such is the nature of cattle. We, however, shall explain the reason for this matter in a few words.

It is certain that in those who are sorrowful and suppressed by grief, heat is gathered from the circumference to the center around the heart along with the spirits; consequently, the upper limbs are deprived of heat and left in cold. Because of the cold predominating in that place, the voice is usually slow and deep, and the abundant heat around the heart requires an abundance of air. Whence the sorrowful draw in much air, which, returning slowly, moves much of the external air; consequently, at the beginning, a thick and deep voice emerges. And since by speaking and complaining—to use the words of the Poet <note>likely Ovid</note>—

> *Grief is driven out by tears, and driven out by words;*

It happens that from complaining talkativeness, the heat moved and released from around the heart exits and moves the air with great speed, which motion is necessarily followed by a high-pitched sound.

It argues that physical anatomy and internal "heat" dictate both a person's voice and their moral temperament.</summary>

# Book I. Anatomical Treatise on the Nature of Sound and Voice.

## §. III.
### On the high-pitched, soft, and broken voice.

Whoever, while speaking, emits a voice that is high-pitched, soft, and broken, we judge those men to be soft and effeminate. We call a voice "soft" here which is both slow and relaxed, and moves very little air, such as we hear in women and in children when they are coaxing, or when speakers falter in the middle of their words. The Poet <note>referring to Virgil, Aeneid 4.76</note> testifies that this is a sign of great emotion existing around the heart in this verse:

> She begins to speak, and stops mid-utterance. <note>original: "Incipit effari, mediaque in voce resistit."</note>

I would believe the cause of this thing to be a lack of heat and an excess of waste-fluid <note>humor excrementitius: refers to excess bodily fluids or "phlegm" in the humoral system</note>: for when heat is lacking, it moves the air softly and interruptedly; it fails when compared with the excessive moisture by which it is nearly overwhelmed. Thus we reason: a high, soft, and interrupted voice shows the dominance of moisture over heat; hence the temperament in which this occurs is effeminate and soft, and it shows inclinations toward weakness and dejection of mind. Therefore, a voice of this kind does not create, but rather indicates, a softness of soul and a womanly nature.

## §. IV.
### On the deep and tangled voice.

Whoever, while speaking, has a voice that is deep, loud, and tangled <note>perplexa: meaning confused, overlapping, or rapid speech</note>, they are—according to the Philosopher <note>Aristotle, likely referring to his "Physiognomonica"</note>—daring, brave, and ready of hand. We call a voice "tangled" when the words are confused and inarticulate among themselves due to the excessive speed of the speaker, and are uttered from the mouth so hurriedly that one syllable following another confuses the listener. This is how those usually speak who, being naturally daring and struck by great mental agitation, seem to make noise rather than to speak.

<margin>The reason why men of deep and tangled voices are daring.</margin>
We give this cause for the matter: since a brave person has a temperament that is intensely hot and dry, the earthy and dry quality produces a deep voice. The heat moves a great deal of air, from which a loud and tangled voice is born. For while the intense heat moves the air—since this movable air is in a way disproportionate to the heat's power and vigor—it drives it more violently than is proper. When propelled violently, the later voice overtakes the earlier one and is almost mixed with it, and thus the voice is rendered "tangled." Whoever, therefore, possesses a voice of this kind, we judge to be daring, headlong, and vehement, as well as physically strong and robust.

## §. V.
### On the soft voice without strain.

Those who are capable of a soft voice without strain are the opposite of the preceding types; for they are mild-mannered and are compared to sheep, according to the Philosopher. You will perceive this in children and young maidens when they are cheerful, without disturbance, and disposed according to nature. From this we form the following reasoning:

<margin>The reason for the soft voice.</margin>
Whoever, while speaking naturally and without affectation, has a small, soft, and relaxed voice, they are mild and of a somewhat timid nature, just like sheep, which have a voice of this kind and are themselves mild and timid.

# Of the Great Art of Consonance and Dissonance

Hence, those who easily endure injuries, are only moderately prone to anger, and do not rise up for revenge; such people, for the most part, possess a sheep-like voice <note>original: "ouina voce"</note>. From this we gather that they are of a moist and cold temperament <note>In the theory of the four humors, a "moist and cold" temperament was associated with a phlegmatic, passive, or calm disposition.</note>; to which both the habits of the mind and the voice accommodate themselves.

## §. VI.
### Concerning the Sharp and Intense Voice.

<margin>The reason for a sharp voice.</margin>
Those who, when they speak, emit a sharp and intense voice, are numbered among the wrathful, the petulant, and the lustful; they are compared to Typhon <note>A monstrous giant from Greek mythology associated with storms and chaos.</note> and to goats. For the goat is an animal of a hot temperament verging toward dryness, and possesses a melancholy mixed with phlegm. Since these humors do not harmonize well, they indicate a certain corruption of moisture within the dryness, which is heavy-smelling, as the odor they exhale declares. Whence, whoever possesses this nature will also have a voice similar to goats, and the same impulsive inclinations. 

However, let no one think we take "inclination" in this context to be so violent that it cannot be corrected by the opposing acts of virtue; for no one is so malicious and perverse that they cannot be capable of virtue. I conclude, therefore, with the Poet <note>The author is quoting the Roman poet Horace, from his Epistles (1.1.38-40).</note>:

> The envious, the wrathful, the lazy, the drunkard, the lover,
> No one is so wild that he cannot become gentle,
> If only he lends a patient ear to cultivation.

HAVING EXAMINED in the preceding book—and discussed according to the order of nature—each of those things which seemed in any way to pertain to the origin of sound and the voice; the logic of our arrangement now demands that we extend the thread of our dissertation toward artificial sound, or Music, which we shall pursue in the exact manner in which it matured within the minds of men. It must be understood, moreover, that Music, in the very first cradles of the world, laid the foundations of its own nursery (as it were) in the various striking together of bodies, as well as in the voices of animals; these foundations Man, the end <note>The "final cause" or ultimate purpose of creation.</note> of all things, later cultivating more deeply, brought forth into a structure perfect in all its parts. For just as simple sound preceded the voice, so the simple voice preceded the harmonic voice or sound (much like certain elements in the composition of things); concerning which indeed

<header>Of the Great Art of Consonance and Dissonance</header>

In this book, we have resolved to discuss the origin, age, propagation, and changes of harmonic sound, especially among the Hebrews and Greeks. And so that we do not keep the reader’s eager mind in suspense with a long introduction, we shall begin our proposed subject "from the egg," as the saying goes <note>original: "ab ovo"; a Latin idiom meaning from the very beginning</note>.

# CHAPTER I.
## On the Invention of Music.

<margin>First principles and origin of Music</margin>
In this worldly theater of things, nothing is more common than sound. Yet, just as it does now, it gave the earliest race of mortals various occasions and opportunities for its discovery. First and foremost, the whistling of the winds, as well as that of certain animals—which those first mortals, scattered here and there through the fields, repeatedly perceived—gave the first occasion for the construction of pipes. These they fashioned in imitation of nature from oat stalks, reeds, the leg-bones of cranes, and other hollow stems of plants. Furthermore, the sound and noise of hollow bodies gave birth to a variety of percussion instruments, as will be seen later.

<margin>The first rudiments of pipes from reeds.</margin>
Since the earliest mortals for the most part devoted themselves to rustic and pastoral life, and pitched their dwelling tents where rich pastures occurred and places watered by streams—and consequently filled with rushes, reeds, papyrus, and similar growths of a tubular nature—it was impossible for a people who were idle for most of the day to exist without some relaxation of the mind. It is likely, therefore, that these men first fashioned various pipes for themselves from the aforementioned hollow stems, and indeed with no less skill than we see shepherds and others living a rustic life do in this time; and perhaps even with greater skill, as their natural wits were still more vigorous then.

<margin>Origin of Musical Instruments.</margin>
Since, moreover, those same first mortals were for the most part skilled in the smith's art (without which the society of the human race would perish), it could not have happened otherwise than that they occasionally excited various sounds and noises from the accidental collision of different bodies while working. From this, it is highly probable that they arrived at the knowledge of various percussion instruments by this occasion, in exactly the same way as still happens to us from time to time today.

<margin>All inventions of things seem to have been accidental.</margin>
Music, therefore, had the first origin of its invention not from the Greeks, or Egyptians, or Chaldeans, but from the first men before the Flood. Indeed, if all men were now removed from the world except for a few children ignorant of things, I have no doubt that these, driven by necessity as well as by chance and experience, could in time stumble upon various inventions necessary to the human race. For the inventions of things are innate to man; they are not learned from books alone, but are drawn out by the intellect when some great necessity compels, or by chance, experience, or the suggestion of genius. Reason itself dictates, as I have said, that Music existed from the beginning, quite apart from the fact that the Sacred Scriptures clearly testify to it. Certainly, in the 4th chapter of Genesis, verse 21, the invention of musical instruments is openly ascribed to Jubal in these words:

->And his brother's name was Jubal: he was the father of all such as handle the harp and organ.<-
<note>original Hebrew: "ושם אחיו יובל הוא היה אבי כל תפש כנור ועוגב"</note>

And the name of his brother was Jubal; he himself was the Father of those treating or striking the Lyre and the Organ <note>The Latin "Organum" here refers to a pipe or wind instrument, not the modern church organ</note>, where the Seventy <note>The Septuagint, the ancient Greek translation of the Old Testament</note> have: "This was he who first showed the psaltery and the lyre" <note>original Greek: "οὗτος ἦν ὁ καταδείξας ψαλτήριον καὶ κιθάραν"</note>.

<margin>Jubal, Author of Music.</margin>
What sort of instruments were actually invented by them will be discussed in the Chapter on the Music of the Hebrews.

<margin>Music invented in Egypt.</margin>
But truly, after the Flood, the Egyptians were the first restorers of lost Music. For these men, instructed by Ham and his son Mizraim, so illustrated Music that the very name "Music" took its etymology from the Egyptian word <term>Moys</term> <note>Kircher links "Music" to the Egyptian word for water, "Moys," suggesting it was invented near the Nile</note>, because it was discovered and restored at the stagnant marshes of the Nile, on the occasion of the reeds and papyrus growth (from which they fashioned their pipes) budding there in abundance. But see what we have treated more at length on this subject in the <term>Prodromus Coptus</term> <note>Kircher's 1636 work "The Coptic Precursor," an early study of the Coptic language and Egyptian history</note>. From the Egyptians, it was afterwards translated to the Greeks, and from them to the Latins and other Nations, by no...

It emphasizes that musical instruments were first modeled after natural sounds like wind in reeds and the rhythmic noise of smithing.</summary>
