# CHAPTER VI.

## Concerning the object of Music and its subalternation, and that Music is a true speculative science.

<margin>The object of Mathematics.</margin>
Every faculty, by the order of nature, presupposes some object about which it is primarily occupied. The absolute and adequate object of Mathematics is finite quantity; as this is twofold—continuous <note>e.g., geometry</note> and discrete <note>e.g., counting numbers</note>—so it gives birth to two faculties: Geometry and Arithmetic. Each type of quantity can further be viewed either absolutely (simply and by itself) or relatively (in relation to something else). The former way constitutes the object of the pure Mathematics, the latter of the mixed Mathematics.

<margin>The formal object of Optics.</margin>
Insofar as both types of quantity pertain to the "matter" or substance, they provide a common material object for both pure and mixed sciences. However, insofar as they pertain to the "form," they constitute a formal object, whose function is to distinguish the science occupied with it from all others. In this way, the formal object of Optics is the visual line; that of Music is the **sonorous number** <note>original: "numerus sonorus"</note>. For no other faculty besides Optics and Music is concerned with these specific properties. For although both Optics and Geometry have the "line" as their common material object, Geometry considers it under a different reason and formality than Optics: the former considers it as extended, while Optics considers the line insofar as it is also visual. This formality, as stated, is the formal object of Optics, by which it is distinguished from Geometry and all other sciences. Similarly, both Arithmetic and Music have numbers as their common object. But Music considers them under a different formality than Arithmetic: namely, insofar as they are sonorous, or insofar as they express certain proportions of sounds. Through this formality, Music—as if by a formal object—differs from all other sciences. What this "sonorous number" actually is, so that the nature of the formal object of this science may be more fully known, remains to be explained.

<margin>What is sonorous number?</margin>
Therefore, sonorous number, taken broadly, is nothing other than a number expressing a certain relation to voices and sounds, which is also found artificially within a sounding body. Taken strictly, however, it expresses a relation only to consonant intervals, which are nothing other than certain proportions and specific forms of consonances considered in the first place in Music.

<margin>How the ancients investigated harmonic intervals.</margin>
The first musicians investigated these ratios on a single string alone. They found that the quantity of the sound of the string was equal to the number of parts into which it was divided. Thus, by knowing the length of the string and the parts into which it was cut, they formed an infallible judgment concerning the distance between the low and high pitch. So, in a string divided into two equal parts, by comparing the sound of one part to the sound of the whole string, they clearly arrived at the knowledge of the interval or voice of the **diapason** <note>original: "diapason"; the Greek term for an octave</note> (which they commonly call the Octave). They necessarily inferred that this consonance consists in a double proportion <note>a 2:1 ratio</note>, according to the analogy of the divided parts, because it relates as two to one.

<margin>Voices and sounds are the matter, while numbers and proportions are its form.</margin>
Furthermore, since a low sound is born from slow motion and a high sound from swift motion, they inferred another point: namely, that the half-part of the divided string vibrates twice as fast as the whole, so that if the whole vibrates a thousand times, the half vibrates two thousand times. They formed the same judgment concerning all other consonant and dissonant intervals, all of which I would examine more extensively here, had I not reserved them for the following books. And from this it is clear that just as voices and sounds are, as it were, the "matter" of harmonic intervals, so numbers and proportions are their "form." From the addition of these results the **sonorous number**, the true and formal object of Music. Moreover, although all bodies are capable of producing sounds, they do not possess that form...

<summary>This page defines Music as a mathematical science distinguished by its "formal object," the sonorous number. It explains how musical intervals like the octave are derived from mathematical ratios and the physical vibration of strings.</summary>
<keywords>Mathematics, Music theory, sonorous number, proportions, diapason, octave, acoustics, formal object, harmonic intervals</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the mathematical foundations of music, specifically focusing on how music relates to other sciences like arithmetic and geometry.</meta>

# Of the Great Art of Consonance and Dissonance

always, unless they are reduced to one certain and determined harmonic form, are they suitable for generating consonances.

<margin>Music is a subordinate science.</margin>
And from this we first infer that Music is a **subordinate science** <note>Latin: *scientia subalternata*; a medieval scholastic term for a science that takes its principles from a higher, more abstract science</note>. To understand this, it must be noted that some sciences are primary, and others are less primary. The primary ones depend on general principles known by the light of nature or the cognition of the senses; from these, they deduce their conclusions. Such are, for example, **Geometry** and **Arithmetic**, and they are called "subordinating" sciences. The less primary ones, however, are those which—in addition to those principles known by the light of nature or acquired by the help of the senses—also assume as their own principles the conclusions already demonstrated by a Primary science. And although they may have the same common material object as the Primary science, they nevertheless consider in this same subject or common object a certain perfection or "formality" from which they chiefly receive their specific classification (as was said a little earlier regarding **Optics** in respect to **Geometry**), to which they are subordinated both by reason of their Principles and their subject or object.

<margin>What is required for the subordination of any science?</margin>
<margin>Music can also be subordinated to geometry, insofar as it considers the sounding line.</margin>
This being established, it is also clear that Music is a science subordinated to **Arithmetic**; which is proved thus. Three things are required for the subordination of any science from what has been said. First, namely, the same material object common to both the subordinating and the subordinated science. Second, the same common principles for both to use in forming conclusions, and furthermore that the subordinated science assumes as its own principles the conclusions made by the subordinating one. Third, that the subordinated science considers the common Object as affected by a certain perfection and formality, which is the foundation and basis of its specification. But these three are found in Music; Therefore, etc. For first, it considers **number** common to both. Next, it uses Principles accepted from Arithmetic. Finally, it considers number not precisely and by itself, but as affected by a certain perfection of **sonority**, or number shining forth in sounding bodies; namely, while it exhibits sounds having any given degrees of highness and lowness; while, given any sound, it finds others in a given proportion by varying the length, circumference, tension, and density of the sounding bodies; while, finally, it counts, adds, subtracts, divides, and multiplies any given sounding numbers, etc. Furthermore, if this sonority is applied to lines explainable by number (as we have provided in our **Harmonic Geometry**), Music will be subordinated not only to Arithmetic, but also to Geometry, and will have as its formal object the **sounding line**, as will be proved more extensively in the *Echosophia* <note>A reference to the author's work on the wisdom of echoes and acoustics</note>.

<margin>Music properly called is a science.</margin>
It is clear, therefore, how Music is a subordinate science. It is also clear that it is not pure Mathematics, but **mixed**, intermediate between Arithmetic or Geometry and Physics. Finally, it is clear that Music is a Science properly and strictly so called, because it is subordinated, as said, to Arithmetic and Geometry. Since, therefore, these subordinating branches are sciences properly so called (as **Blancanus** <note>Giuseppe Biancani (1566–1624), a Jesuit astronomer and mathematician</note> learnedly and solidly demonstrates in his *Mathematical Passages in Aristotle*), it follows that the subordinated ones are also such, since they use the same principles and, moreover, Music proceeds in a truly scientific manner. For it employs three kinds of principles, namely **Definitions**, **Postulates**, and **Axioms**, from which at last a perfect science is born, as **the Philosopher** <note>Aristotle</note> proves at length in the books of the *Posterior Analytics*; and the definitions are indeed perfect and essential, such as the definitions which we proposed in the third book.

<margin>Definitions in Music.</margin>
*As, for example: A **harmonic interval** is a space capable of tension and relaxation, etc. A **double multiple interval**, or **octave** <note>Latin: *diapason*</note>, is a greater harmonic space containing a smaller one twice, etc. A **triple multiple interval** is a greater harmonic space containing a smaller one three times, and so on for the others, which are essential definitions, and cannot be otherwise; indeed, they are of eternal truth, as we demonstrate elsewhere, and have a necessary connection with the nature of things.* There are also **Axioms** or Pronouncements in the Science of Music, which are Propositions

<margin>Axioms in Music.</margin>
<margin>Postulates in Music.</margin>
known through themselves and by the light of nature. Such as: *Whatever harmonically measures another, also measures that which is measured by it, etc. A harmonic composite is resolved into those simple parts from which it is composed, etc. Whatever measures the part taken away and the remainder, also measures the whole;* and many similar ones which we treat elsewhere by design. The **Postulates** are: *Given an equal string, whatever the proportion of space to space is, such is also the proportion of sound to sound.* And similar things, from which the most certain conclusions, as in this whole work...

<summary>This page argues that music is a "subordinate science" that derives its logical foundations from arithmetic and geometry while applying them to the physical reality of sound. It establishes music's status as a rigorous science by identifying its specific definitions, axioms, and postulates.</summary>

# CHAPTER III.
## On the Division and Definition of Music.

<margin>Worldly Music.</margin>
Music, taken in its widest sense, is nothing other than a discordant concord or a concordant discord of various things concurring to establish a single whole, concerning which we shall treat at length in the final book; it is either natural or artificial. Natural music embraces the whole nature of things harmonically arranged—or what is the same thing, Worldly Music <note>original: "Musica Mundana"</note>—containing the wonderful harmony of the heavens, the elements, and mixed bodies. To this is also recalled Human Music, which consists either in the harmonic composition of the body, or in the marvelous order, arrangement, <term>tempered mixture <note>Greek: synkrasia</note></term>, and concord of the senses, both internal and external, as will be said in its proper place.

<margin>Twofold Artificial Music.</margin>
Artificial music is either organic or harmonic. Organic music contains the ingenious construction and use of instruments in a threefold order: Wind <note>original: "Pneumaticorum"</note>, Stringed <note>original: "Encordorum"</note>, and Percussion <note>original: "Pulsatiliumque"</note>, which we treat professionally in the entirety of Book V. Harmonic Music is that which, by means of sense and reason, considers the differences of sounds, melodies, and consonances, which we treat in various ways across four entire books, namely the Third, Fourth, Fifth, and Sixth.

<margin>What are speculative and practical Music?</margin>
Some divide Music into speculative and practical. Hence, the theoretical Musician is said to be he who, excluding the senses, judges by reason alone whatever can occur in harmonic Music. The Practical musician, however, relying on sense alone, is ignorant of the cause of his operation as such; for which reason the theorist is as much nobler than the practitioner as science is more eminent than art. Yet both are imperfect, according to that saying:

->The one who sings by habit, not by art, is a beast, not a singer.<-

<margin>Who is the perfect Musician.</margin>
Therefore, he alone is and ought to be called a perfect Musician who joins Theory to practice; who not only knows how to compose, but can also give a rational account of every single thing. To perform this with dignity, however, a knowledge of almost all sciences is required: namely Arithmetic, Geometry, the Proportions of sounds, practical Music (both vocal and instrumental), Metrics, History, Dialectics, Rhetoric, and finally an absolute knowledge of all Philosophy. Thus, we define Music, taken in its strict sense, as nothing other than *the perfect science of harmonic sounds occurring in any genre, of which we place a certain archetype in this Universal Musurgy, or the Great Art of Consonance and Dissonance.* But having premised these things <term>more generally <note>Greek: katholikōterōs</note></term>, let us now follow each point in order, beginning with the various Music of different Nations.

# CHAPTER IV.
## ON ANCIENT MUSIC
## And the Instruments of the Hebrews, and what kind they were.

<margin>The many-stringed Lyre before the flood.</margin>
Just as the invention of strings was easy, so too no one should doubt that it is most ancient and belongs to the earliest times. Since nothing is so necessary as the use of threads for binding various things together, and every stretching of any kind of threads excites a certain pleasing sound—and from the varying tension of strings, various sounds are born—nothing was easier for musical men, first taught by this experience, than to eventually invent instruments of every kind. Indeed, the Sacred Scriptures teach us that the many-stringed Lyre <note>original: "Cytharam polychordam"</note> existed before the flood, and the Book of Kings and the Psalms [teach] that David used the ten-stringed Psaltery...
<meta>The text continues on the next page with "and of the psalms."</meta>

<summary>This page defines music in its broadest philosophical sense, dividing it into natural (world/human) and artificial (instrumental/harmonic) categories, while establishing the "perfect musician" as one who masters both theory and practice.</summary>

# The Great Art of Consonance and Dissonance
<meta>catchword: or</meta>

...and bears witness to both. Therefore, in order to more deeply investigate the quality and condition of these types of instruments formerly used among the Hebrews, I have earnestly turned over and re-read as many commentaries of the Rabbis and volumes of Talmudic works treating them as I could obtain. I wished to learn at the source what should be thought regarding the true form of said instruments amidst such a great confusion of authors; I have thus decided to communicate in this place to the curious reader what I have accomplished.

<margin>The Gate of the Mighty.</margin> <note>original: "thor hag in." likely an abbreviation for the Hebrew title "Shilte Ha-Gibborim" (Shields of the Mighty).</note>
Among the other Hebrew authors, the author of *The Shields of the Mighty* <note>A 16th-century work by Abraham Portaleone discussing the Temple service.</note> treats these instruments with exactness; he says there were 36 different musical instruments of the Sanctuary, and that David was the master of playing all of them; I join his words:

->It is remembered that King David was familiar with thirty-six musical instruments.<- <note>original Hebrew: יזכור ששה ושלשים כלי נגון שהכירו בהם דויד המלך</note>

It is recorded that David had knowledge of playing 36 musical instruments; and in the same place:

->Regarding the twenty-two types of song that King David knew in the Holy House, he wrote a complete discourse on the conditions of the art of song.<- <note>original Hebrew: לשני ועשרים מני זמרה שדע בם דויד המלך עם בבית הקדש ויכחוב מאמר שלם בתנאי הזמרה המלאכותיה</note>

That is: our Fathers wrote that the songs and soundings of the House of the Sanctuary were very many, for they record 22 different kinds of instruments, the knowledge of playing all of which King David held, peace be upon him. And Saadia <note>Saadia Gaon, a prominent 10th-century Jewish philosopher.</note> in his commentary on Daniel attributes to him the skill of 36 instruments. The Talmudic treatise *Arakhin* counts 34 of them. So that all these may be brought out of their confusion into a harmonic order, we shall begin with the multi-stringed instruments, which are called <term>Neghinoth</term> <note>Hebrew for "stringed instruments" or "melodies."</note> by the Hebrews.

### §. I.

## Concerning the Multi-stringed Instruments of the Hebrews, or Neghinoth

<margin>What kind of instruments the Neghinoth were to the Hebrews.</margin>
That the *Neghinoth* of the Hebrews were instruments played by hand, or with small sticks, or a plectrum, the word itself—*nagan*, which is the same as "to strike"—declares more than sufficiently. *The Shields of the Mighty* describes them thus in chapter 4:

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of three early stringed instruments. In the center and left are two large instruments with rounded backs and long necks, labeled 'E' and 'A', with small male portraits appearing above them. On the right is a smaller instrument with a figure-eight or waisted body labeled 'C'. Above the instruments, the Hebrew terms 'מנים Minnin' (Strings) and 'מחול Machul' (Dance/Pipe) are printed.</image-desc>

> They were long, round wooden instruments
> with hollow spaces beneath them, having three strings
> made of animal sinew or gut; when they played
> them, they would scrape the strings with a bow made
> of horse-tail hair pulled tight.
> This instrument is called in the Greek language the <term>trichordon</term> and in Latin
> the <term>trifidium</term>. <note>Both terms refer to a three-stringed instrument.</note>

And the *Neghinoth*, he says, were long and round wooden instruments, and beneath them were many holes; they consisted of three strings made from the intestines of animals, and when they wished to sound them, they scraped the strings with a bow constructed from horse-tail hair bound tightly; in Greek it is called a <term>trichordon</term>, in Latin a <term>trifidium</term>. Indeed, the *Neghinoth* instruments were the Psaltery, the Nablum, and the Cithara...

<vocab>Schilte haggibborim (Shields of the Mighty), Neghinoth (stringed instruments), David, trichordon (three-stringed), trifidium (three-split/stringed), Machul (dance/flute), Minnin (strings), instrumenta musica (musical instruments)</vocab>

<summary>This page investigates the musical instruments of the ancient Hebrews, citing Rabbinic sources like the "Shilte Ha-Gibborim" to identify the types and quantities of instruments used by King David. It specifically begins a technical description of the "Neghinoth," identifying them as stringed instruments played with a bow or plectrum.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philological discussion of Hebrew and Greek musical instruments from Book II, focusing on the specific physical forms of the Psaltery and related stringed instruments.</meta>

# Book II. Philological Treatise on Hebrew and Greek Music.

...or what is the same: the *Assur*, *Neuel*, *Kinnor*, *Magbul*, and *Minnim*, of which each must be briefly discussed.

<margin>What was the Davidic Psaltery like?</margin>
As to what the Davidic Psaltery <note>The "Psaltery" refers to the stringed instrument used by King David to accompany the Psalms.</note> was like, no one has yet correctly defined it. Some believe it was not so much a specific instrument as a term denoting certain types of harmony and the modulation of the voice and sound. Josephus records that it had twelve sounds and was plucked with the fingers. Hilary, Didymus, Basil, and Euthymius call it the "most upright" of all musical instruments, containing nothing perverse or crooked within it, because it was struck on the upper part. From this, it seems to have lacked the hollow shell of a lute. Augustine says it was carried in the hands of the performer and had its "shell"—that is, the hollow wood which the strings lean upon to resonate—on the upper part, whereas Lyres <note>original: "Cytharæ"</note> have this on the lower part. Jerome describes this instrument in the shape of a square shield with ten strings. Hilary maintains it is the same as the *Neuel*, or *Nablium*. It is certain that the Psaltery was equipped with strings, and the word for "psalming" <note>psallendi</note> signifies, according to Suidas, plucking the strings with the tips of the fingers. A great many musicians attribute to it the likeness of our modern Harp, which they commonly depict in his [David's] image in a triangular shape, which Apollodorus (quoted in Athenaeus) says was called a <term>magadis</term> <note>original: "μάγαδιν" - an ancient high-pitched stringed instrument</note>, Aristotle calls a <term>trigonon</term> <note>original: "τρίγονον" - literally "a triangle"</note>, and Suidas calls a Sambuca. Consequently, the strings in the psaltery were of unequal length, which Porphyry also records regarding the Sambuca in an old lexicon. Some say this psaltery is the same as the *Nablium*, citing Ovid in the third book of *The Art of Love*:

->*Learn also to sweep the festive nablia with both palms,*<-
->*They are suited to sweet measures.*<-

This agrees with what Schilte asserts; for he says the *Nebel* was a stringed instrument and was divided into three octaves with twenty-two strings. The *Assur*, however, consisted of ten strings; the *Kinnor* of thirty-two; the *Machul* of six; and the *Minnim* of three or four strings. The *Neuel* was constructed in the shape of a trapezoid, while the *Kinnor* was made in the likeness of a modern Lyre; the *Machul* and *Minnim* were like the Viola and the Lyre <note>original: "Chelym" - a tortoise-shell lyre</note>. But lest I be too wordy in listing the *Negginoth* <note>stringed instruments</note>, I have decided to share them here with the reader as taken from an ancient Vatican manuscript, where the psaltery is shown under

<image-desc>Three engravings of ancient Hebrew musical instruments. On the left, a rectangular harp with a decorative canopy and twelve strings, labeled 'A' below and 'Ten-stringed' (עשור) above. In the middle, a simple triangular harp labeled 'I' below and 'Harp/Lute' (נבל) above. On the right, a triangular instrument with a thick soundbox and soundholes labeled 'B' below and 'Lyre' (כנור) above.</image-desc>

form A, the Cinnor under B, the Machul under C, the Minnim under E, and the Neuel under I. Mersenne, however, defines the new and ancient Lyre under a different figure. It appears that it bore a resemblance to our Harp, distinct with twenty-four strings across a triple-octave <note>original: "trisdiapason"</note> range. What that mystical ten-stringed instrument <note>original: "decacordon"</note> was—reflecting the harmony of the triple world—will be described more extensively elsewhere, as well as in our *Hieroglyphic Music*.

The *Haghniugab*, a stringed instrument (mentioned in the Targum of both Onkelos and Uzziel), was not unlike the larger stringed instrument commonly called the Viola da Gamba, and it consisted of six strings. See Rabbi Hannase, chapter 10 of the *Schilte Haggibborim*; it is frequently confused with the *Machul*, for they differ only in the number of strings.

It includes visual reconstructions from a Vatican manuscript and compares ancient forms to contemporary instruments like the harp and viola da gamba.</summary>
<keywords>Psaltery, Kinnor, Neuel, King David, musical archaeology, stringed instruments, Hebrew music, organology, Schilte Haggibborim</keywords>

# §. II.

## Concerning the Percussion Instruments of the Hebrews.

The Hebrews used <term>percussion instruments</term> <note>original Latin: "instrumentis κρουστοις [kroustois] sive pulsatilibus"</note> according to the testimony of the *Shields of the Mighty* <note>original Hebrew: "Schiltehaggibborim," a 16th-century rabbinic work by Abraham Portaleone</note>, where he describes them in these words:

<insert>“And our fathers also knew of the *Haschufanim*, the *Hammechilath*, the *Haschbufangnaduth*, the *Mesbrakitha*, the *Hakitbros*, the *Haschabra*, the *Hasphanderin*, the *Hamningnanghin*, the *Hasimphunia*, the Talmudic *Hamagrepha*, the *Haordaulus*, the *Hatabla gutgana*, the *Haiugab*, the *Hataph*, the *Hamningnangin*, the *Hazalzaloth*, and similar things.”</insert>

That is to say, our Fathers were acquainted with these instruments, concerning each of which we shall speak.

### I. Concerning the Hebrew Drum, called *Thoph* (תוף).

The author of the *Shields of the Mighty* asserts that the Hebrew drum had its origin among the Egyptians.

<insert>“For the Egyptian priests especially used the drum in the shrine of the Great Mother of the Gods, who is called the Goddess Vesta among them and represents the foundation of the earth; and our fathers received it from them.”</insert>

(Behold its form from the cited author;) Rabbi Abraham the Prince <note>Rabbi Abraham Hannasè</note> testifies that it had a different shape from modern ones. For he says it bore a resemblance to a ship or a small boat, which is why it was called a *Cymbalum* by the Greeks; for to the Greeks, a *Cymbalum* is nothing other than a musical instrument in the likeness of a boat <note>original Latin: "Cymba"</note>. He says, moreover, that this instrument was covered with the skin of an animal, and then struck with a plectrum like a pestle or a rod of iron or brass, with a stroke sometimes strong and sometimes relaxed, with sounds now thick and frequent, now rare and dull, just as is customary with our drums. Truly, I have thought it necessary to add its likeness here, along with the citation of the text from the aforementioned author:

->*Thoph* (תוף)<-

<image-desc>Woodcut illustration of an ancient Hebrew percussion instrument identified as a "Thoph" or "Tympanum". It depicts a semi-oval, boat-shaped vessel or drum with a textured exterior, possibly representing stretched skin or a metallic casing. Two small ring-like handles are visible on the sides of the bowl. Above the instrument, a pair of hands emerging from the left and right sides holds two beaters or drumsticks with rounded heads, positioned as if striking the surface. The Hebrew word תוף (Thoph) is printed above the beaters, and a capital letter 'N' is placed centrally below the instrument.</image-desc>

<insert>“The *Thoph* is called a *Cymbalin* because of its shape, which resembled a small ship called a *Cymba* in the Greek language; and it is called *Cymbalin* because it is like a small metal vessel without a cover, such as one of copper or another metal, long and narrow at its head and end, and wide and slightly hollow in its middle.”</insert>

### II. *Machul* (מחול).

In the preceding sections, we numbered the *Machul* instrument among the stringed instruments according to the opinion of certain Hebrews. However, Abraham the Prince by no means considers it a stringed instrument—

<summary>This page discusses ancient Hebrew percussion instruments, specifically focusing on the "Thoph" (drum), its purported Egyptian origins, and its boat-like physical structure as described by Rabbinic scholars.</summary>
<keywords>Hebrew music, percussion instruments, Thoph, drum, Schiltehaggibborim, Abraham Portaleone, Cymbalum, Vesta, Egyptian influence</keywords>

--- Page 87 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of ancient Hebrew musical instruments from Book II, focusing on percussion instruments and their comparisons to Egyptian and Greek counterparts.</meta>

# Book II. Philological Treatise on Hebrew and Greek Music.

...he believes he has demonstrated with the clearest arguments that it was not a stringed instrument, but a percussion instrument. For he says it was the same as the Egyptian *Sistrum* <note>A rattle-like percussion instrument used in ancient Egypt</note> or the Greek *kremastra* <note>original: κρέμαסטρα; likely referring to hanging bells or clappers</note>. He claims it was a circle surrounded by many small bells, usually made of iron, bronze, silver, or gold. But let us hear his own words:

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a musical instrument labeled with the Hebrew word Machol (מחול). It consists of a handle with two cross-guards and a large oval metal frame. Small decorative bells are attached to the outer circumference of the frame, and three horizontal rods pass through the interior of the oval.</image-desc>

> Behold, the *Machol* <note>original: מחול; often translated as "dance," but here interpreted as an instrument</note> is called in the Greek language a *Sistrum* or *Chrousma* <note>original: כרוסמאו / κροῦσμα; a rhythmic stroke or beat</note>, and in the Latin language a *Tintinnabulum* <note>A small tinkling bell</note>. It is a vessel of copper, silver, or gold that is round and resembles a large ring, open on all sides. Around its rim, which is about three fingers wide, small bells were fixed; and by striking the clappers against the bells, they would produce sounds of joy.

However, having made a comparison, we might say more accurately that a *Sistrum* of this kind can be numbered among the instruments called *Zalzsaloth* and *Minangngbinim* <note>original: צלצלים (Cymbals) and מנענעים (Rattles)</note>—that is, among those consisting of many small bells.

For an instrument of this type does not in any way correspond to the *Sistrum* of the Egyptians. As we have shown at length in our *Hieroglyphic Music*, the *Sistrum* was an iron instrument not equipped with bells, but with iron rings and a handle; when shaken, a certain mournful murmuring arose from the collision of the iron rings both with each other and with the iron or bronze rods upon which they were threaded. You will find their form and mystical significance described most extensively in our *Oedipus* <note>Referring to Kircher's "Oedipus Aegyptiacus"</note>, in the book on Egyptian Hieroglyphic Music. Nevertheless, I am led to believe that the Hebrew *Sistrum* or *Thoph* <note>original: תוף; usually a timbrel or drum</note> was fashioned in imitation of this, because virgins everywhere used this kind of *Sistrum* in the dances of weddings, as we read in Exodus and the Book of Judges regarding Miriam the sister of Moses and the daughter of Jephthah. I have learned from trustworthy witnesses that the use of this *Sistrum* still persists in Palestine today.

---

### III. עצי ברושים Fir-Wood Instruments (Gnetse Berusim).

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a percussion instrument labeled "Gnetse Berusim". It depicts a small, hollow wooden vessel resembling a mortar (mortariolo) and a separate wooden stick or pestle (pistillo) with bulbous, rounded ends and a small central handle.</image-desc>

**I** have found no one among the Rabbis who has correctly described what or of what kind this instrument was. Some confuse it with *Crotala* <note>Ancient clappers or castanets</note>; some wish it to be the same as the rustic instrument commonly called *Gnaccari* <note>Nakers; small kettledrums</note>. Others confuse it with the *Sistrum*. Only Rabbi Hannasè <note>Rabbi Judah ha-Nasi</note> seems to have touched upon the truth when he describes it in these words:

<margin>*Gnetse Berusim.*</margin>
> The *Gnetse Berusim* <note>original: עצי ברושים; literally "woods of cypress/fir"</note> are, in my opinion, special instruments that produce a collective sound that is not tuned to a specific melody. They are two instruments made of fir wood, one of which resembles a small mortar <note>original: מורטאריולום / mortariolum</note>. However, the other wooden tool resembles a small pestle; it is long and round, slightly thin in the middle, and wide at its two ends, resembling a knob or the head of an axe. They thought these instruments were called *Kritalon* <note>original: כריתלון / κρόταλον; castanets</note> in Greek and *Naqari* in the vernacular.

As far as I could discern, he says the *Gnets Berusim* were joined instruments, sounding with a clear voice but without harmony, as is usually the case with other stringed instruments. In substance, they are nothing other than two pieces of fir wood. One is like a small mortar, the other like a small pestle—long and round, in the middle of which is a small handle, and on its two heads there were certain protruding knots...

<sig>G 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: ...dam (protruding)</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the identification of the Hebrew 'Machol' and 'Gnetse Berusim', comparing them to Egyptian sistrums, Greek clappers, and Latin bells. The author critiques previous Rabbinical definitions, favoring a percussion-based interpretation for these ancient musical tools.</summary>
<keywords>Machol, Sistrum, Gnetse Berusim, Hebrew music, musical instruments, Rabbi Judah ha-Nasi, percussion, organology</keywords>

<page-num>52</page-num>
<header>Of the Great Art of Consonance and Dissonance.</header>

...some were spotted as if by hail; those who played them would take a small mortar in the left hand, and with a pestle in the right hand, they would strike once upon the rim of the mortar and once upon its opening with the two ends of the pestle—once indeed with one end of the pestle and once with the other. They are called *Crotalum* <note>original: "Crotalum"; a type of clapper or castanet</note> by the Greeks and Latins, and *Gnaccari* <note>original: "Gnaccari"; likely related to the Italian 'nacchere' or castanets</note> by the Italians.

### IV. *Minanghninim* <note>original: מנענענים; likely referring to the *Mena'ane'im*, often identified as the sistrum</note>.

There is no small controversy among authors concerning the meaning of this name; some take it for a psaltery, others for a cymbal, and many for children's rattles. The Talmudic treatise *Aruchin* <note>The *Arachin*, a volume of the Talmud dealing with valuations and Temple assessments</note> says they are instruments which, through their violent shaking, strike small spheres within themselves and emit various noises through an opening. Hannaše <note>Rabbi Hananeel or a similar commentator cited by the author</note> explains it best of all in the cited passage, with these words:

> This instrument is made of a square piece of wood, and at the top it has a handle to hold it. Upon the board on this side and that are hollowed-out wooden spheres, or balls, attached by an iron chain or by hemp cords in the winter; and when they shake the board, they strike the aforementioned balls and produce a sound heard from afar.

He says: This instrument is a certain square wooden board, at the head of which is a handle suitable for gripping; upon the board, however, on both sides, there are wooden or bronze globules inserted into iron chains or even cords made of hemp stretched over the board. When the board is shaken, those globules, struck both against each other and against the board, produce a very clear sound perceivable from a distance.

->*Minanghninim*<-

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a musical instrument. It consists of a rectangular wooden board with a handle at the top. The board features two decorative circular rosettes on its face. A central vertical rod or chain is mounted to the board, holding five spherical beads or bells. Below the instrument is the letter "R".</image-desc>

The *Magraphe Tamid* <note>A shovel-like instrument or gong used in the Temple</note> was a different instrument from the *Magraphe* of the *Aruchin*; the former was a percussion instrument by which the people were summoned to the Temple, possessing a sound so powerful that Hannaše says it could be heard in the city of Jericho. As to what its shape was and how it produced such a portentous sound, there is no one who can explain it. They say, however, that it was placed at the back of the Jerusalem Temple, and the place was so arched that the voice of this instrument, variously reflected and multiplied by the arched surface of the vault, stirred up a sound that they could perceive even in Jericho. It was an instrument established for summoning the Priests and Levites, and for stationing those who were unclean at the doors of the Temple. Certainly, if one may resolve the matter by conjectures, I would surely say that this instrument had something in common with our larger bells, which are commonly known to be heard at a very remote distance. What the *Magraphe* of the *Aruchin* was, however, shall be told shortly hereafter.

<meta>The letter 'R' appears as a figure label under the illustration. A small 'q' or catchword is visible at the bottom left.</meta>

Book II. Philological Study of Hebrew and Greek Music.

# §. III.
## On the Wind Instruments of the Hebrews.

I. *Mashroqita* <note>original: "Maſrakitha"</note>, an instrument named for the whistling sound it produced, was a <term>polycalamon</term> <note>Greek: "many-reeded"</note> or an instrument of many reeds. These were bound together and fixed step-wise into a wooden frame shaped like a case. The reeds were open at the top, but at the bottom they were plugged with a certain wood and covered with skin. It was equipped with a handle, from which the widened box gradually narrowed into a small space. The instrument was applied to the lips, and by blowing into it—while the fingers alternately closed and opened holes on the side—various sounds were perceived according to the length, width, or shortness of the pipes, or even the intensity of the breath. From this, I gather that this instrument was exactly the same as the *Syrinx* or the seven-piped flute of Pan, as will be shown shortly.

II. *Sampunia*, a word corrupted from the Greek <term>symphonia</term> <note>Greek: "concord of sound"</note> unless I am mistaken. Mention is made of this, as well as the preceding instrument, in the third chapter of Daniel in these Chaldean <note>Aramaic</note> words:

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a pan-pipe style instrument (Mašroqitā). It features ten pipes of increasing length, held together in a rectangular wooden block base. The Hebrew word משרוקיתא (Mashroqita) is printed above the pipes.</image-desc>

> That at the time you hear the sound of the horn, the pipe (the many-reeded one),
> the lyre, the trigon, the psaltery, and the bagpipe, and all
> kinds of music...

That is: "at the time when you shall hear the voice of the horn, the pipe (the many-reeded one), the cithara, the sambuca, the psaltery, and the symphony, and every kind of musical instrument, etc." To which the Greek version corresponds: <term>that as soon as you hear the sound of the trumpet, the syrinx, and the cithara, the sambuca, and the psaltery, and the symphony, and every kind of music</term> <note>original Greek: "ἵνα ὡς ἂν ἀκούσητε τῆς φωνῆς τῆς σάλπιγγος, σύριγγός τε καὶ κιθάρας, σαμβύκης τε καὶ ψαλτηρίου καὶ συμφωνίας, καὶ πάντος γένους μουσικῶν"</note>. Where for the word "horn," the Greek reading has <term>the sound of the trumpet</term> <note>original Greek: "φωνῆς τῆς σάλπιγγος"</note>. *Sampunia*, therefore, is understood not as a simple pipe, but as described in the <term>Shilte ha-Gibborim</term> <note>The "Shields of the Heroes," a 17th-century work by Abraham Portaleone describing Temple music</note>. It was a <term>dyaulon</term> <note>Greek: "double-pipe"</note> instrument of two pipes, between which was placed a round bag made of the skin of a ram or wether. The two aforementioned pipes were inserted into this bag, one pointing upward and the other downward. When the upper channel was blown into, the bag filled with breath; when compressed, it supplied air to the lower pipe. This pipe, through the closing or opening of holes arranged upon it by the "midwifery" of the fingers, brought forth various sounds. We provide its figure elsewhere. From this, it is clear that it was entirely similar to our bagpipe <note>original: "vtriculo"</note>, which shepherds and peasants use everywhere; and it is truly remarkable that in Italy to this day, it is called by this name: the pastoral *Zampogna*.

<image-desc>Two woodcut illustrations of pipes. On the left, a curved wind instrument with a bulbous end and finger holes. On the right, the lower portion of a U-shaped pipe or horn.</image-desc>

<meta>catchword: Macra-</meta>

<summary>This page describes two ancient Hebrew wind instruments: the Mashroqita (a pan-pipe or syrinx) and the Sampunia (identified as an early bagpipe). The author uses linguistic evidence from the Book of Daniel and Greek translations to link these ancient tools to contemporary European folk instruments like the Zampogna.</summary>
<keywords>Mashroqita, Sampunia, pan-pipes, bagpipes, Book of Daniel, musical archaeology, Hebrew instruments, Zampogna, syrinx</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of ancient musical instruments, specifically focusing on those mentioned in Jewish tradition and the Talmud, following the previous descriptions of stringed instruments.</meta>

# Of the Great Art of Consonance and Dissonance

III. The <term>Magrepha d'Aruchin</term> <note>original: "Macraphe d'Aruchin"; a shovel-shaped instrument described in the Talmud</note> was a Talmudic musical instrument, similar to our church organs; for as the <term>Shields of the Mighty</term> <note>original: "Schilte gibborim"; a 16th-century work by Abraham Portaleone describing Temple rituals</note> teaches, it consisted of many rows of pipes and was brought to life by bellows marked L and M. Furthermore, it had openings and keys marked by Roman numerals corresponding to each pipe, which, when pressed by the organist's hand, opened the air valves and produced a wonderful variety of sounds. We have sketched its form here as best we could.

->The Magrepha according to the description of Rabbi Hannasè.<-

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a 'Magrepha', an early organ-like instrument. It consists of a rectangular chest with ten slider keys labeled with Roman numerals I through X. Above the chest are two ranks of organ pipes of varying sizes. Behind the pipes are two sets of bellows, labeled 'L' and 'M', which appear to be operated by levers or handles at the top.</image-desc>

### Concerning Pipes and Trumpets

The **P**ipes used by the Hebrews were of three kinds; first, they used the horns of animals adapted into pipes, as is found in 1 Chronicles, chapter 25. Second, they used a certain...

<margin>**Abub**, **Keren**, **Halil**</margin>

<image-desc>An illustration of three wind instruments. On the left is a curved pipe labeled 'Abub' (אבוב). In the center is a large, spiraling horn labeled 'Keren' (קרן) and 'Curved Trumpet' (Lituus retortus). On the right is a straight, vertical flute with finger holes labeled 'Halil' (חליל).</image-desc>

-> Book II. Philological Treatise on Hebrew and Greek Music. <-

from a certain kind of pipes, which they likewise fashioned from animals, such as Cranes, Storks, etc. Third, there were instruments curved in the manner of a Bull’s or Wild Goat’s horn, which, beginning with a slender opening, widened with a continuous, gradual increase toward the other end, exactly like our instruments commonly called <term>Cornetto</term> <note>A "cornett" or "zink," a wooden wind instrument with a mouthpiece like a trumpet but finger holes like a recorder</note>; some, however, confuse this instrument with that which they call <term>אבוב</term> *Abub* <note>original: "Abub"</note>; for the *Abub* was a type of flute or pipe which the Levites used in sacrifices. But as I have said, <term>חליל</term> *Halil* <note>original: "Halil"</note>, <term>אבוב</term> *Abub*, and <term>קרן</term> *Keren* <note>original: "Keren," meaning horn</note> are confused here and there in both sacred and profane literature.

Mention is made of various pipes throughout the Holy Scriptures, which can nevertheless always be traced back to one of the three aforementioned types.

<margin>
Gloss on Hagigah, folio 14, 2.
</margin>
Mention is also made in the Talmudic writings of a certain instrument which Rabbi Hannose calls <term>אודחלוס</term> *Orthaulum* <note>original: "Orthaulum"</note>; and it was nothing other than a Water Organ; as the corrupted word shows; for it should not be called *Orthaulus*, but correctly <term>υδραυλος</term> *Hydraulus* <note>original Greek: "hydraulos," a water-powered pipe organ</note>. Truly, since we treat this elsewhere as a profession, I did not wish to dwell longer on its description. Having thus seen the instruments of the Hebrews, let us now run through the manner of singing used by them.

### §. IV.

## On the Use of Musical Instruments among the Hebrews.

There is no doubt that the Music of the Hebrews in the time of David and Solomon was most perfect; for since David acted as a Musician from his boyhood and was moved by it in a wonderful way, it certainly could not have happened that, having been raised to a higher rank of dignity, he would not have promoted it by every means. Solomon, indeed, imbued with infused knowledge, must be believed to have been instructed by God in music just as in all other things; for I do not see how that Divine building <note>The Temple of Jerusalem</note>, elaborated in every part according to the rules of harmonic proportions, could have been made without the greatest science and skill in music. Certainly, only one who does not understand the order and arrangement of the individual things occurring in this wonderful and divine structure could fail to know that all the vessels were distributed in a marvelous order, and especially that the musical instruments were elaborated with the highest craftsmanship and established with the greatest variety and wisdom.

<margin>
Music was highly cultivated by Solomon.
Book 8 of Antiquities, chapter 2.
</margin>
Certainly Josephus says that Solomon fashioned forty thousand musical instruments, which are called <term>nabla</term> and <term>cynnirae</term>, out of amber <note>or "electrum," a precious alloy</note> for the use of hymn-singing; where by *cynnirae*, nothing else is understood but the *Kinnor*, the nature of which was described above. From this it is clearly evident that the music of Solomon surpassed our own by many leagues; nor can it be believed that an innumerable supply of musical instruments constructed with the highest wisdom served only to produce certain unformed sounds, devoid of all art. Rather, it must be thought that the excellence of these instruments was entirely equaled by the genius and skill of the Musicians and Organists. Therefore, there was a marvelous order of Singers and Voice-trainers, a marvelous distribution of choirs, and a marvelous harmony of hymns adapted to harmonic measures; nor is it likely that the instruments of any one choir all gave a unison voice, but rather, by a marvelous and ingenious texture, with high and low voices harmonically tempered, they rendered a <term>πολυπόικιλον</term> *poly-poikilon* <note>original Greek: "variegated" or "richly diverse"</note> harmony. For it was not fitting that the praises to be paid to God through musical organs should be discordant with such great order of the mystical temple and the wonderful arrangement of things. Furthermore, not just any instruments responded to any praises and ceremonies; but different ones to different ones, according to the witness of the *Shilte ha-Gibborim* <note>A 16th-century Hebrew treatise on the Temple and its music</note>. Rabbis Calonimus and Hannose recount 10 different kinds of spiritual songs, for which they destined as many different instruments; his words are the following:

> With ten kinds of song they shall praise the Lord: with melody, with conducting, with a psalm, with a song, with praise, with prayer, with blessing, with thanksgiving, with "Happy are they," and with Hallelujah. <note>original Hebrew: "בעשרה מיני זמרה ישבחו את יהוה..."</note>

<summary>This page discusses the classification of Hebrew wind instruments, including the water organ (Hydraulus), and argues that the music of David and Solomon reached a peak of divine perfection and harmonic complexity.</summary>
<keywords>Hebrew music, Solomon's Temple, David, Hydraulus, Kinnor, Nabla, musical proportions, Jewish liturgy, Josephus, Shilte ha-Gibborim</keywords>

# The Great Art of Consonance and Dissonance

that is, in ten different kinds of instruments, they would praise God with instruments that served to express profound matters; secondly, things pertaining to victory; thirdly, for the recitation of Psalms to God; fourthly, for songs of love; fifthly, for divine praises; sixthly, for prayer and the mind's trust in God; seventhly, for blessings; eighthly, for confessions; ninthly, for the beatitudes; and tenthly, serving the dances of the soul; to which some add:

> For the leader, for the sons of Korah, a maskil, do not destroy, a song of loves, a golden poem, a wandering song, according to the lilies, a testimony, the silent dove of those far away. <note>original: "למנצח לבני קרח משכיל אל תשחת שיר ידידות עלמות מכתם שגיון נחילות עדות יונת אלם רחקים" — These are Hebrew musical directions and titles found in the Book of Psalms.</note>

<margin>
Different instruments served different affections of the soul among the Hebrews.
</margin>
<margin>
Great variety of Hebrew music.
</margin>
These names are indeed the Titles of the psalms; and so, to excite different affections of the soul, different instruments were employed: To excite divine praises in the souls of men, they employed pipes of every kind; for hymns of Confession and thanksgiving, they used the *Nablum* <note>A Phoenician harp or lute</note>, the psaltery and similar instruments; to excite spiritual joys and dances, they used flutes, the *Maghiloth*, *Magrephis*, and *Zalzalim* <note>Various ancient Hebrew percussion and wind instruments</note>. In the Temple, however, while the solemn rites were being performed, not everyone sounded their instruments indiscriminately, but the singers, distributed as if into choirs, sang together with alternating melodies. For the voices, as Kalonymus <note>A reference to the medieval Jewish philosopher and translator Kalonymus ben Kalonymus</note> rightly says, were not mixed with certain instruments of louder noise and sharper sound (such as Trumpets and Trombones) or Cymbals, lest the power of the words be lost, smothered by the vehement noise of the percussion; rather, they were joined to the *nablia* and to instruments of a gentler sound. Sometimes only stringed instruments were heard, sometimes wind instruments; occasionally <term>psalms</term> <note>original: "ψαλ." (psal.) — likely an abbreviation for the Greek 'psalmoi'</note> were heard only by alternating choirs; often they competed, mixed both among themselves and with voices, to create a greater variety.

<margin>
The singers of David: Heman, Jeduthun, Asaph.
</margin>
The leader of all these was the Royal Prophet himself, the Author of the Psalms, some of which he sang with his own mouth. For if we believe Sixtus of Siena <note>A 16th-century Dominican scholar and theologian</note>, four thousand singers with instruments were established by David; among whom three were preeminent, who sang songs with him in the temple on Harps, Psalteries, and Cymbals: namely Heman, Jeduthun, and Asaph. Ezra testifies that Asaph praised God "by the hands of King David," that is, with the Psalms written by the hand of King David.

---

### §. V.
## Concerning the More Famous Musicians among the Hebrews.

Asaph, son of Berachiah, the chief of the singers sounding with bronze cymbals and praising the Lord, ministering in the presence of the Ark; he himself, according to the mind of the Interpreters, is thought to have composed certain psalms.

Heman the Ezrahite, a singer and son of Joel of the sons of Kohath, most skilled in cymbals and the trumpet; he was in some way equal to Ethan in erudition and wisdom, and is thought to have composed the eighty-first psalm <note>Psalm 88 in modern numbering</note> which begins, *O Lord God of my salvation*; because he gave it to the sons of Korah to be sung, he inscribed it with both his own name and theirs.

Ethan the Ezrahite, son of Kushaiah of the sons of Merari, clashing with bronze cymbals, was endowed with such wisdom that, according to the Book of Kings, no mortal except Solomon alone was ever wiser than he. There were also three distinguished singers, the sons of Korah: Assir, Elkanah, and Abiasaph, occupied in composing Psalms.

<margin>
The excellence of Solomon's music.
</margin>
Jeduthun, an extraordinary singer and most excellent Harper, whom many confuse with Orpheus. Since, therefore, such great men were the leaders of Divine praises—indeed, divinely imbued with the science of singing and playing—it is right that we believe they sang their hymns (composed with the highest artistry and genius) not with a rude and unpolished confusion of voices, but with an equal excellence of voice. No one should doubt that there were many others, especially in the time of King Solomon, most excellent masters of song, who held authority over the divinely instituted choir; for it was fitting for the wisest of mortals to have the wisest music, perfect in every measure, surpassing all the music of the Greeks and of posterity.

Book II. Philological Treatise on Hebrew and Greek Music.

...music was both more excellent and more effective at stirring the emotions; however, what kind of music that was, or by what notation it was expressed, the antiquity of those times has begrudged us the knowledge.

### CHAPTER V.
### Concerning the Music of David.

With these things having been thus prefaced, it will at least be worth the effort for us to discuss in this chapter, a little more deeply, the style and method observed by David in composing hymns.

### Section I.

## Whether the Davidic Psalms were composed in free prose, or indeed in verse, and in what genre of verse?

<margin>The heretics who disparage David are mentioned by Philastrius.</margin>
<margin>It is established that the Psalms were written poetically.</margin>
Philastrius of Brescia <note>A 4th-century bishop and writer on heresies</note> reports that there were heretics who asserted that David was a composer of profane and worldly ditties, an error also attributed to Paul of Samosata. The Anabaptists in our own century say that the Book of Psalms was written not by David, but by certain more recent Jews after the death of Christ. However, the most holy Gospel of God itself, by quoting the psalms, easily refutes them. There are others who think that the metric genre of writing is soft and profane, suited only for composing fables or as an aid for sketching various impure emotions of anger or love; nor can they persuade themselves that any sacred books were ever written poetically. Indeed, for this very reason, there were those who thought the Book of Job was not a true history, but a tragic-comic fiction of the Hebrews. But it shall be our part to utterly abolish this unfair judgment and to show from every kind of erudition that David composed his psalms in no other style than the metric one.

<margin>2 Chronicles 29:27; 1 Ezra 3:10; 2 Kings 23; Ecclesiasticus 47 & 44.</margin>
<margin>David composed the psalms in a metric style.</margin>
First, there is no lack of irrefragable testimonies of Holy Scripture which clearly assert this: *The priests stood in their offices, and the Levites with the instruments of the songs of the Lord, which King David made, singing the hymns of David with their own hands.* And in 1 Ezra 3 <note>Commonly referred to as the Book of Ezra in modern Bibles</note> the same is said; and in 2 Kings 23 <note>2 Samuel 23 in modern Bibles</note>, an elegant passage holds thus: *These are the last words which David the son of Jesse said, the excellent psalmist in Israel.* And in Ecclesiasticus 47, it is said of David: *He made singers stand before the altar, and in their sound he made sweet melodies.* There are those who think that as often as mention is made of "songs" in the Scriptures, it is understood to be about the psalms of David, as in Ecclesiasticus 44: *Let us praise glorious men, etc.* and: *In their youth telling the songs of the Scriptures.* For it seems it should be read there as "in their youth" <note>original: "pueritia"</note>, not "in their skill" <note>original: "peritia"</note>, although the Greek word found there signifies both. Moreover, Saint David loved pious music from his cradle and from his childhood (these are the words of Augustine, Epistle 131, near the end) and he himself inflames us in the study of it more than any other author.

<margin>In the hymn on the Passion of the Lord and in Sedulius’s Paschal Work.</margin>
Secondly, from the harmonious voice of the entire Church and the Fathers, asserting that David was a divine poet and that his psalms are songs, the same point is concluded. Whence in the hymn on the Passion of the Lord (the author of which is Venantius, a Christian poet), it is said:

> *Fulfilled are the things which David*
> *Sang in his faithful song*

And Sedulius, in his *Paschal Work*, says thus:

> *Why should I, accustomed to Davidic chants, etc.*

Blessed Jerome, in the prologue of the Bible to Paulinus, speaks of David thus: David, the Si— <note>The text cuts off mid-word; likely "Simonides" or "Siren," referring to David as the Hebrew lyricist.</note>

<summary>This page argues that the Psalms of David were intentionally composed as formal poetry and music rather than prose, refuting historical claims that poetic meter was too "profane" for sacred scripture. The author cites biblical passages and Church Fathers like Augustine and Jerome to prove David's status as a divine poet.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the divine origins of music and poetry, specifically focusing on the Hebrew tradition as the source for classical forms.</meta>

# Of the Great Art of Consonance and Dissonance. Book 1

...our Simonides, Pindar, and Alcaeus, and also Flaccus <note>Horace</note>; Catullus, and Serenus likewise sound forth Christ upon the lyre, and upon the ten-stringed psaltery wake Him as He rises from the dead, etc. He <note>St. Jerome</note> holds the same in his *Preface to the Books of Kings*. And St. Ambrose, in his *Preface to the Psalms*, speaks divine truths concerning David, the sacred leader of songs, and likewise concerning Moses, the composer of two songs. Regarding these same two, Eusebius in Book 11 of *Preparation for the Gospel*, chapter 3, says that their songs are in hexameter, or trimeter, or tetrameter <note>Technical terms for poetic meters consisting of six, three, or four measures</note>; and that those things pertaining to the diction are most elegant, most grave, and most pleasant, while those pertaining to the meaning are comparable to no other human writing—so he says. St. Augustine in *The City of God*, Book 17, chapter 14, and in *Letter 131* near the end, also speaks. All these authors marvelously extol the songs of David and hand down the reasons that impelled him to compose these poems. To these, add St. Athanasius speaking illustriously of David in his *Synopsis*. Josephus in Book 7, chapter 10, says: "David, having now finished with wars and dangers, and living in the deepest peace, composed Odes and hymns in honor of GOD in various kinds of verse, partly in trimeter and partly in pentameter; and having prepared musical instruments, he taught the Levites to sing the praises of GOD to their beat." Cassiodorus, in his *Prologue to the Psalter*, chapter 15, says that every mode of poetic expression took its beginning from the divine Scriptures, namely from David; just as all authors record that all other literature was derived from the Hebrews to the Greeks and then to the Latins. Certainly, it is clearly evident that before the founding of Troy, before the voyage of the Argonauts, and before the beginning of the Olympiads, Moses, Deborah, the Mother of Samuel <note>Hannah</note>, and David had already observed the laws of song with precision when they composed their Canticles according to metrical rules; indeed, it is established by the testimony of Josephus, Justin, Origen, and Eusebius that profane <note>Secular/Pagan</note> authors borrowed much from here.

<margin>
The Greeks learned Poetics from the Hebrews.
</margin>

---

### §. II.
### Concerning the Titles of the Psalms.

<margin>
The Psalms were composed to be sung with Instruments.
</margin>

**T**hat the Psalms were accustomed to be sung with organs and musical instruments is sufficiently declared by their titles. This is evident from the passages of Scripture and the Fathers already cited, but most of all from the titles of the psalms themselves, many of which contain certain words that signify the musical instruments themselves to which such-and-such a psalm was to be sung. For example, the title of Psalm 4 reads thus: <term>למנצח</term> <note>Hebrew: La-menatzeach</note>. *To the end, In songs, a Psalm of David.* The phrase *To the end* is explained by Doctors of the Church not in one way, but in various ways. St. Jerome translates it as <term>To the Victor</term>. Others, like Cajetan, as *To the One who overcomes*. However, "Victor" in this context signifies, as the Hebrews believe, the Prefect of the singers, the <term>Maestro di Capella</term> <note>Choirmaster</note>. For in the choir during David's time, there was a certain Leader and Master who directed the other singers, who possessed both a more excellent voice and a more exact skill in musical matters; and to him David gave the psalms to be sung to musical instruments. Regarding this "Victor" (or him whom we now call the Cantor, from singing; or the Choragus, as the head of the choir), there are those who think that in the title of the fourth psalm, one should understand that passage from Habakkuk 3: *And he will lead me upon my high places, the Victor*, singing in psalms. Therefore, the "Victor" is the same as the Chief Musician, or the leader of the singers.

<margin>
What is the meaning of the phrase "To the end" in the titles of the Psalms?
</margin>

<margin>
In the titles of the Psalms, the Instruments to which the Psalm was to be sung are noted.
</margin>

Furthermore, what follows in the title of the same psalm, *In songs*, greatly favors our argument: for many among the Hebrews believe that the Hebrew word found there signifies a specific musical instrument established for singing joyful and triumphal matters. Whence, where our version has the whole title as: *To the end, In songs*, the Hebrews have: *To the Prefect of the Singers, upon musical instruments*. Rabbi Solomon <note>Rashi</note>, in his exposition of the title of Psalm 4, signifies by this title that effort by which the singers strove to surpass and conquer one another in singing with joy: all of them singing so heartily that they seemed to contend as if for victory, etc. It cannot be denied, therefore, that it is literally hinted that the psalm itself is a poem; and thus it is a song to be sung in praise of GOD, even if in a spiritual and higher sense the phrase *To the end* signifies Christ, who is the end, and the mysteries of the Church which were to be carried out at the end of the ages.

<summary>This page argues that the origins of poetry and music lie in Hebrew scripture rather than Greek tradition, citing various Church Fathers and historians. It specifically analyzes the titles of the Psalms to prove they were intended for instrumental accompaniment under the direction of a "Chief Musician."</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Book II regarding the philology of Hebrew and Greek music, specifically focusing on the musical directions found in the headings of the Psalms.</meta>

# Book II. Philological Discussion on Hebrew and Greek Music.

<margin>The titles of the Psalms indicate that the Psalms are songs:</margin>
Again, Psalm 5 has it thus: *To the Chief Musician upon Nehiloth, a Psalm of David* <note>original: "למנצח אל הנחילות מזמור לדוד"</note> **A lyric song of David**, for the Leader of the Singers. Where Vatablus <note>François Vatable, a 16th-century French humanist scholar</note> says regarding *hannehiloth*: It was some notable musical instrument, etc. Therefore, this psalm of David was committed to the most skillful leader of the harpists, to be sung to the *hannehiloth*. And Lyranus <note>Nicholas of Lyra, a medieval Franciscan teacher</note> also feels the same. And the title of Psalm 6 is *for the Sheminith* <note>original: "השמינית"</note> **The Eighth**, that is, in the most intense tone and clearest voice: (in the <term>diapason</term> <note>The Greek term for an octave</note>) for this eighth voice, as we have shown elsewhere, is most suitably adapted for boys, women, and men indiscriminately; so that even from this it may be evident that the use of the octave, which the Greeks call *diapason*, was already practiced long ago. This psalm was given to a very distinguished leader of the symphonists, to be sung to the <term>Neghinoth</term> <note>A stringed instrument</note>, a musical instrument. Whence the Chaldean <note>The Aramaic Targum</note> translates it thus: *In praise for the praiser, and on a harp of eight strings.* And in Psalm 9, it is held in Hebrew as *upon Muthlabben* <note>original: "על מותלבן"</note> **A Psalm for Muthlabben**, that is, to the measures of a common folk song, the beginning of which was *Muthlabben*. And in Psalm 21, the title has *To the Chief Musician upon Aijeleth Shahar* <note>original: "למנצח על אילת השחר"</note> **A song of David concerning the doe of the morning**, that is, to be sung in the manner of a common folk song, the beginning of which was, *The Doe of the Morning*. So says Vatablus: for Saint Jerome also translates it, *For the doe of the morning*. Felix, *For the doe of the dawn*. All of which clearly express that the psalms themselves are songs. Otherwise, we would have to admit that prose orations were sung to musical instruments, which is almost unheard of and absurd among all nations. For it should be known that just as among the Comic and Tragic Poets, the type of instrument to which they were to be sung was immediately marked in the titles (namely, with equal or unequal pipes, as in Terence: *Performed at the Megalensian games with two pipes*: which Eugubinus also noted in the title of Psalm 4, etc.), so in the titles of the Psalms certain instruments are named to which they were to be sung, as we have shown.

<margin>The Winepresses in the title of Psalm 8.</margin>
But also the title of Psalm 8 ought not to be passed over. For it has it thus: *To the Chief Musician upon Gittith* <note>original: "למנצח על הגתית"</note> **To the end, for the Winepresses**. Rabbi David thinks the Hebrew word found there, namely, <term>Githith</term>, is the name of a musical instrument to which that psalm was to be sung. Others say it is the name of a city of the Philistines called Gath: because that psalm was sung by David in that city; or because the instrument to which it was to be sung had been brought from that city, as in Pindar, the *Dorian Lyre*. For so says Rabbi Solomon, as does Lyranus here. Cajetan <note>Thomas Cajetan, a 16th-century Italian cardinal</note> also adds here that this harp had the shape of a winepress, and the etymology of the word is taken from winepresses. Whence the Seventy <note>The Septuagint (Greek Old Testament)</note> and Saint Jerome translated here, *The Winepresses*: Others say this psalm is so named, *Githith*, because it was handed over to be sung to a certain singer, the son of Obed-edom the Gittite. I think it was named after the city of Gath in which this kind of melody flourished, just as the Greeks spoke of Dorian, Lydian, and Phrygian songs from the peoples among whom they were in use; and the use still flourishes today. Although perhaps it also took its name from the winepress because it was accustomed to be sung at the time of the winepresses, during the Feast of Tabernacles, shortly after the fruits were gathered and the grapes squeezed in the presses: concerning which see Leviticus 23. Nor is it inconvenient that this psalm was accustomed to be sung at the time of the winepresses in thanksgiving for the fertility of the year and the abundance of the vintages, along with Psalm 80 and 83, which are marked with the same title, etc. Provided that these also signify in a mystical sense the higher mysteries of Christ and the Church; which I not only do not condemn, but rather I think those are to be most vehemently rebuked who, content with that husk of the bare letter and fed on chaff, as it were, cast away the true food which is provided in the spiritual sense.

<margin>What the word Selah signifies, placed 73 times in the Psalms.</margin>
Furthermore, what that word <term>Selah</term> is, usually gives the Doctors much trouble. First, that word is never found in the Hebrew books (as Cajetan well notes on Psalm 3) except in the Psalms (and indeed seventy-three times) and in the Song of Habakkuk, chapter 3, once. From this, the common opinion of the Hebrews is rendered probable: that it is a word pertaining to the singing of the meter. Whence some say that by that word a change of meter is signified, or a <term>diapsalma</term> <note>A musical interlude or shift</note>. Others, a change of the song. Others, a lifting of the voice. Others, the insertion of a pause or silence, or a pausing of the Holy Spirit. Others, a division of the sentence, that is, the end of one and the beginning of another. Others, the beginning of a certain musical variety, etc. And so it can be that all those meanings, gathered into one, form a total and full...

<summary>This page analyzes the musical terminology in Psalm headings, arguing that they prove the Psalms were intended as songs accompanied by specific instruments or melodies. The author discusses terms like Nehiloth, Gittith, and Selah, drawing parallels between Hebrew music and Greek or Roman traditions.</summary>
<keywords>Psalms, Hebrew music, musical instruments, Gittith, Selah, Nehiloth, diapason, biblical philology, Vatablus, Cajetan</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's technical analysis of musical and structural elements in the Psalms, moving from the definition of musical terms to the formal construction of acrostic poetry.</meta>

# Of the Harmony and Dissonance of the Great Art
## Page 60

For they express the force of this little particle. The word *Selah* is derived from the root *Salal*, as **Pagninus** <note>Santes Pagnino (1470–1541), a famous Hebraist and philologist.</note> noted in his *Treasury of the Holy Tongue*, from the word *Sal* and the word *Salal*. Where, citing **Rabbi Abraham**, he says the word *Selah* is not meaningful <note>i.e., it does not carry a lexical definition like a noun or verb</note> but is a direction for the singing, derived from the verb *Salal*, which means to exalt, to lift up, or to emphasize. Regarding this, **Vatablus** <note>François Vatable, a 16th-century French humanist scholar.</note> in his greater commentaries on Psalm 3, says: "Formerly the singers, whenever this word occurred, were accustomed to strain the voice and cry out, as if they were saying:
> O heavy calamity: let all our friends see and weigh it well."

**Cajetan** <note>Thomas Cajetan, a prominent 16th-century theologian.</note> on Psalm 3 holds the same view, saying this word is "functional"—that is, it has the function of elevating and prolonging the voice. **Rabbi Abenezra** asserts the same. From these I gather that an elevation of the song and the intermission of a certain silence are signified by this word, which in Italian songs is expressed by *piano* and *forte*. Or, by this word of pausing, one may also understand <term>agoge & thesis</term> <note>original: ἀγωγή & θέσις; Greek terms for the "leading" and "setting" of a musical beat, similar to tempo and downbeat.</note> as the moderator of all Music-making; concerning which, see my more extensive treatment elsewhere.

---

### §. III.
### On the Acrostic Verses of the Psalms.

<margin>Psalms composed and sung in alphabetical order.</margin>
I truly do not know what musical mystery lies hidden in the fact that several psalms are written with remarkable skill in alphabetical order. This style of writing by the letters of the alphabet—so that each verse begins with a single letter in its proper order—is a method specific to meters and poems. Indeed, **Saint Hilary** on Psalm 118 said there were only three acrostic psalms (that is, composed in the order of the alphabet): namely Psalms 110, 111, and 118. **Saint Jerome**, in his Letter to Paula of Rome, added another to those three: namely Psalm 144.

<margin>There are seven psalms written alphabetically.</margin>
He says those who think there are more are mistaken. Yet the truth of the Hebrew Psalter shows that, besides those, there are also Psalms 24, 33, and 36—though the latter proceeds with an interrupted alphabet. This was observed by **Cajetan** on those psalms, as well as by **Titelmans, Jansen, Genebrard, and Sixtus of Siena** in Book 1 of his *Library* under the word "Psalter." The Lamentations of Jeremiah are also written in this alphabetical order, as is Chapter 31 of Proverbs, which is called the *Metropedia* <note>A Greek-derived term meaning "Instruction of a Mother."</note> of King Samuel.

<margin>The reason for the acrostic composition.</margin>
The reason (which seems to be the literal one for this acrostic composition) is that handed down by **Rabbi David Kimhi** and other Hebrew Doctors, as **Sixtus** and others note: it is familiar and customary for Hebrew poets to arrange poems dealing with illustrious subjects according to the sequence of the alphabet, so they may be more easily imprinted on the memory and, once imprinted, may stick more tenaciously. It may also be true, as **Saint Jerome** and other ancients thought (as **Genebrard** noted), that these psalms were digested into this series of letters not without great mysteries and Sacraments of the Church. See **Jerome** in that Letter to Paula which begins *Nudius tertius* in Volume 3, etc. But my interest now is not to pursue allegories and spiritual meanings, but the bare literal text.

Similarly, we have an example of this in the hymns of the Church: for the priest **Sedulius** composed a hymn alphabetically. It begins with the letter *A*, "From the point of the sun's rising" <note>original: *A solis ortus cardine*</note>; then *B*, "Blessed author of the age" <note>original: *Beatus author seculi*</note>; and thirdly *C*, "The womb of the chaste parent" <note>original: *Castæ parentis viscera*</note>, etc. A part of this hymn is that which is sung on the day of Epiphany, "O impious enemy Herod" <note>original: *Hostis Herodes impie*</note>; and then *I*, "The Magi went [following the star] they had seen" <note>original: *Ibant Magi quam viderant*</note>; then *L*, "The washings of the pure flood" <note>original: *Lauacra puri gurgitis*</note>; and so it proceeds all the way to *X* and *Z* in the uncorrected Hymn.

<margin>That in acrostic Psalms a letter is sometimes omitted.</margin>
But we cannot pass over what is touched upon by others: namely, what the reason might be that in those seven mentioned Davidic psalms, the order of the alphabet is sometimes overturned. For in Psalm 24, two letters are omitted, namely *Vau* and *Koph*, from which no verse begins. In Psalm 33, the letter *Vau* is again omitted. In Psalm 36, a double verse (and sometimes a triple one) begins from each Hebrew letter, but *Ayin* <note>original: *Hain*</note> is omitted, for no verse begins with it; and in its place *Tsade* is put twice. In Psalm 144, the letter *Nun* is missing, and thus even in the Hebrew codex that verse which should have begun with that letter is lacking—although the seventy interpreters <note>The Septuagint (LXX) translators.</note> added a verse of their own which [corresponds] to that letter...

<summary>This page discusses the musical meaning of the Hebrew term 'Selah' as a vocal instruction and examines the structure of acrostic (alphabetical) Psalms and hymns, noting irregularities in their alphabetical sequences.</summary>
<keywords>Selah, acrostic psalms, Hebrew poetry, musical notation, Saint Jerome, Sedulius, biblical philology, alphabet hymns</keywords>
