# Of the Great Art of Consonance and Dissonance

## Proposition II.

-> Given any two numbers, to assign a harmonic mean proportional between them. <-

**I**ntermediate to any two given numbers, you shall find a harmonic mean proportional <note>The harmonic mean is a type of average often used in music theory to determine the pitch that sits "harmonically" between two others.</note> in this manner. Take the number produced by the difference of the given numbers, multiply it by the smaller of them, and divide the product by their sum; add the resulting quotient to the smaller number, for the resulting sum will be the mean sought. For example, let the two proposed numbers be 15 and 60. To obtain the harmonic mean, multiply their difference, 45, by the smaller, 15, and divide the product, 675, by their sum, 75. For if you add the quotient, 9, to the smaller, 15, you will produce the mean term 24, as is evident in this example: 15, 24, 60. You will find the same mean if you multiply the same difference, 45, by the larger, 60, and divide the product, 2700, by their sum, 75; for the quotient, 36, subtracted from the larger, 60, will leave the same mean term, 24.

---

## Proposition III.

-> Given any two numbers, to assign a third term in harmonic proportion. <-

<margin>When a third harmonic proportional can be added to two given numbers, and when it cannot.</margin> **N**ote first that this operation cannot always be performed; however, the operation itself beautifully teaches when it is possible, in this way: We shall divide the number generated from one multiplied by the other by the number that remains when the difference between both is subtracted from the smaller given term; for the quotient will be the third term, larger than both given, which we seek. But if the divisor is ever found to be 0, or when the difference of both cannot be subtracted from the smaller term, it is impossible for a third larger number to be added in harmonic proportionality to the two given numbers. Let us declare the matter by example. Let the two smaller terms be 12 and 16. We shall divide the number produced from them, 192, by 8—which is the number remaining if the difference of both, 4, is taken away from the smaller, 12. For the quotient, 24, establishes with the two given numbers this harmonic proportionality: 12, 16, 24; and 24 is the sought third term of the same. We shall extend this if we add a third to the two numbers 16 and 24, namely by dividing 384 (the number made from 16 times 24) by 8 (the number remaining after the subtraction of the difference of both, which is 8, from the smaller, 16). For the number 48 will be found, and thus the 4 harmonically proportional numbers will stand as 12, 16, 24, 48. If, however, we attempt to add another larger one to these, we shall labor in vain. For given the last two, 24 and 48, the divisor will be found to be 0. But if someone proposes these numbers, 10 and 12, a third larger than both, 15, will be added to them. To these two, 10 and 11, a third, 12 ½, will be applied. And to the two 90 and 99, a third, 110. But truly, to 3 and 6, none can be added, because the difference between both, which is 3, taken from the smaller, 3, leaves 0. From which it follows that when the given numbers have a <term>double proportion</term> <note>original: "proportionem duplam"; a 2:1 ratio, such as an octave in music.</note>, a third proportional cannot be added to them, because the difference of both is always equal to the smaller term. Furthermore, when the given numbers have a proportion greater than double, a third larger harmonic proportional is likewise impossible, because the difference of both is then always greater than the smaller term, so that the subtraction can by no means be performed. As if two numbers 3 and 7 are given, whose proportion is <term>double sesquitertia</term> <note>original: "dupla sesquitertia"; a ratio of 7:3.</note>, which is clearly greater than double, you see the difference of both, 4, is greater than the smaller term, 3. Therefore, some third cannot be added to them. It is evident, therefore, from these things, that for a third harmonic proportional to be added to any two given numbers, it is neces-

<summary>This page provides mathematical instructions for finding the harmonic mean between two numbers and determining a third number to extend a harmonic series. It explains the limits of these calculations, noting that a third proportional cannot be found if the ratio between the first two numbers is 2:1 or greater.</summary>
